1 | O humanity! Be mindful of your Lord Who created you from a single soul, and from it He created its mate, and through both He spread countless men and women. And be mindful of Allah—in Whose Name you appeal to one another—and ˹honour˺ family ties. Surely Allah is ever Watchful over you. | The surah opens with a sweeping address to all humankind: “O humanity!” This call immediately establishes that the guidance to follow is not for one tribe or nation but for every human being, because the principles of justice, family, and God-consciousness are universal. The first command is to “Be mindful of your Lord,” a translation of the Arabic term taqwā, which combines God-consciousness, reverent fear, and a constant awareness that one will be held accountable. The verse then anchors this mindfulness in a powerful reminder of human origins: “Who created you from a single soul, and from it He created its mate.” This single soul, according to the overwhelming majority of commentators, refers to Adam, and its mate to Eve, who was created from Adam’s own self. The expression dissolves any basis for racial, tribal, or gender superiority, because all people trace back to the same source. It also establishes the family as the fundamental unit of human society, since from that pair Allah “spread countless men and women.” Every human being, regardless of status, is a descendant of that first couple, which gives every person an innate, God-given dignity. The verse then reinforces the command by appealing to a deeply rooted Arab custom: “And be mindful of Allah—in Whose Name you appeal to one another.” In pre-Islamic Arabia, people would ask one another for rights or favours “by Allah,” and this appeal carried moral weight. The verse seizes upon that recognition of Allah’s name as the highest authority and links it directly to the next obligation: “and honour family ties.” The instruction to uphold kinship bonds is placed side by side with mindfulness of Allah, showing that cutting off family is a violation of that same God-consciousness. The final sentence, “Surely Allah is ever Watchful over you,” is a gentle but firm warning. The divine name Raqīb, translated as Watchful, means that Allah observes every action, every neglect of family, and every act of faithfulness. This opening verse thus lays a threefold foundation: recognition of a common human origin, the twin duties of fearing Allah and joining ties of kinship, and the constant divine vigilance that makes those duties inescapable. It reminds believers that piety is not merely ritual but profoundly social, beginning with how one treats the people closest to them.
2 | Give orphans their wealth ˹when they reach maturity˺, and do not exchange your worthless possessions for their valuables, nor cheat them by mixing their wealth with your own. For this would indeed be a great sin. | After establishing the sanctity of family ties in the opening verse, the surah immediately turns to one of the most vulnerable groups within the family: orphans. The instruction is direct and unequivocal: “Give orphans their wealth when they reach maturity.” This command addressed a widespread injustice in pre-Islamic Arabia, where guardians would often consume the inheritance of orphaned relatives or refuse to hand it over once the child grew up. By commanding that the wealth be given, Allah establishes the guardian’s role as that of a trustee, not an owner. The phrase “when they reach maturity” indicates a specific time when the orphan has attained the age and sound judgment to manage their own affairs, a theme elaborated later in verse six. The verse then warns against two specific forms of financial abuse. The first is “do not exchange your worthless possessions for their valuables.” A guardian might be tempted to swap a poor-quality item of his own for a valuable asset belonging to the orphan, effectively plundering the estate under the cover of a transaction. The second warning is “nor cheat them by mixing their wealth with your own.” This refers to the practice of incorporating an orphan’s property into the guardian’s own assets so that it becomes indistinguishable and is gradually consumed. Both actions are forms of subtle theft, exploiting the guardian’s position of trust. The verse closes with a severe verdict: “For this would indeed be a great sin.” In Arabic, the expression used is ḥūban kabīran, a grave and enormous wrongdoing. This moral weight is particularly striking because the victim is defenceless; the orphan has no father to protect their rights, so Allah Himself takes up their cause. This verse thus sets a precedent for the Qur’anic vision of social justice, where the strong are accountable for how they treat the weak, and economic transactions are governed by honesty rather than opportunism. In a broader sense, it teaches that every position of authority, whether over an orphan’s wealth or any public trust, is a test from the Ever-Watchful Lord mentioned in the previous verse.
3 | If you fear you might fail to give orphan women their ˹due˺ rights ˹if you were to marry them˺, then marry other women of your choice—two, three, or four. But if you are afraid you will fail to maintain justice, then ˹content yourselves with˺ one or those ˹bondwomen˺ in your possession. This way you are less likely to commit injustice. | This verse addresses a specific historical concern while laying down a permanent framework for polygyny. The immediate context, as reported in authentic hadiths such as that narrated by ʿĀʾishah, involved guardians who were in charge of orphan girls. Some guardians wished to marry these orphans for their beauty or wealth but did not intend to give them the full bridal due owed to free women. The verse therefore opens, “If you fear you might fail to give orphan women their due rights if you were to marry them,” that is, if you worry that you will not deal fairly with them, then do not marry them; instead, “marry other women of your choice—two, three, or four.” The verse thus permits polygyny up to four wives but immediately conditions it on a stringent requirement: “But if you are afraid you will fail to maintain justice, then content yourselves with one.” Justice here refers primarily to equality in material provision, such as food, housing, and clothing, and to fair rotation of nights. The verse acknowledges that not every man is capable of such justice, and in that case, marriage to a single wife is the clear directive. The mention of “those bondwomen in your possession” reflects a transitional social institution of the time, which provided legal rights and responsibilities; it is not a permanent model but was already being regulated toward eventual abolition through strong encouragement to free slaves. The conclusion, “This way you are less likely to commit injustice,” reveals the deep logic of the legislation. The whole system is designed to minimise oppression. The verse should never be read as an encouragement to polygyny without restraint; rather, it limits an already existing unlimited practice, places strict moral conditions upon it, and ultimately directs believers toward monogamy as the safer path. This careful balancing of permission and restriction shows how the Qur’an reforms deep-seated social customs gradually yet decisively, always pushing society toward greater justice for women and orphans.
4 | Give women ˹you wed˺ their due dowries graciously. But if they waive some of it willingly, then you may enjoy it freely with a clear conscience. | Having addressed the broader framework of marriage, the surah now zooms in on one of the most concrete rights of a wife: the bridal due, or ṣadāq. The command is clear: “Give women you wed their due dowries graciously.” The dowry is not a purchase price for the woman but a gift given freely by the husband as a token of commitment and respect. The word “graciously” (niḥlah) conveys that it must be offered willingly, with a good heart, not grudgingly or as a reluctant obligation. This establishes that a woman’s financial rights in marriage are not a favour from the man but a divinely mandated right that she receives with full dignity. The verse then contemplates a situation in which a wife, of her own free will, might decide to forgo a portion of her dowry: “But if they waive some of it willingly, then you may enjoy it freely with a clear conscience.” The wording is precise; the waiver must be “willingly,” meaning without coercion, pressure, or manipulation. If the wife, being of sound mind and full legal capacity, chooses to give back some of her dowry as a gift to her husband, then he may accept it with a good conscience, because it has become a lawful gift. This provision protects women from being emotionally blackmailed into surrendering their financial security while also respecting their autonomy to make generous choices within the marriage. The phrase “with a clear conscience” (hanīʾan marīʾan) uses two Arabic words that evoke a sense of pleasant, wholesome enjoyment free from any spiritual harm. The verse thereby educates the community that a woman’s financial rights are inviolable unless she herself, in full freedom, decides otherwise. This teaching stands in stark contrast to practices where a wife’s property was automatically absorbed by the husband, and it reinforces the Qur’anic principle that women possess independent economic personality.
5 | Do not entrust the incapable ˹among your dependants˺ with your wealth which Allah has made a means of support for you—but feed and clothe them from it, and speak to them kindly. | Having protected the wealth of orphans, the surah now addresses the broader responsibility of managing assets for those who lack sound judgment. “Do not entrust the incapable among your dependants with your wealth.” The term “incapable” (sufahāʾ) refers to those who cannot manage money sensibly, whether due to mental immaturity, intellectual disability, or reckless prodigality. The wealth is described as “your wealth,” but it is immediately qualified as that “which Allah has made a means of support for you.” This indicates that wealth is ultimately a provision from Allah, entrusted to people not just for their own pleasure but as a support system for the entire household. The guardian is therefore responsible for preserving it and using it wisely. The verse then provides a balanced set of instructions for how to treat such dependents: “but feed and clothe them from it, and speak to them kindly.” The guardian is not allowed to neglect their material needs. They must be fed and clothed adequately from the very wealth they are not capable of managing themselves. More than that, the guardian must speak to them “kindly,” with words that are gentle and respectful. This is a profound ethical directive. A person who is financially dependent can easily be spoken to with condescension or harshness, but Allah commands that their dignity be preserved through kind speech. The verse thus intertwines financial stewardship with spiritual and emotional care. It teaches that looking after vulnerable family members is not just about avoiding theft or waste; it is about actively nourishing them and treating them with honour. In a society that often equates a person’s worth with their economic productivity, this verse establishes a counter-cultural principle: those who cannot manage money still possess full human dignity and a right to the support of their families, accompanied by gracious treatment.
6 | Test ˹the competence of˺ the orphans until they reach a marriageable age. Then if you feel they are capable of sound judgment, return their wealth to them. And do not consume it wastefully and hastily before they grow up ˹to demand it˺. If the guardian is well-off, they should not take compensation; but if the guardian is poor, let them take a reasonable provision. When you give orphans back their property, call in witnesses. And sufficient is Allah as a ˹vigilant˺ Reckoner. | This verse establishes a detailed protocol for guardians handling orphans’ wealth. It begins with the instruction to “Test the competence of the orphans until they reach a marriageable age.” Guardians are not to simply wait passively; they must actively assess the orphan’s maturity and financial sense by gradually involving them in small transactions and observing their judgment. The “marriageable age” is not just biological puberty but the stage at which a person is typically ready for the responsibilities of family life, which naturally implies a degree of intellectual and emotional maturity. The verse then stipulates, “Then if you feel they are capable of sound judgment, return their wealth to them.” The Arabic term rushd, translated as “sound judgment,” means the ability to manage one’s affairs correctly, especially financial ones. The guardian’s subjective assessment is guided by the observable competence of the orphan. The next clause issues a stern warning: “And do not consume it wastefully and hastily before they grow up to demand it.” Some guardians, fearing they would lose control of the wealth once the orphan matured, would squander it quickly. The verse prohibits such devouring of property without right. A critical distinction follows regarding the guardian’s own financial situation: “If the guardian is well-off, they should not take compensation; but if the guardian is poor, let them take a reasonable provision.” A wealthy guardian must serve without any financial recompense, viewing the task as an act of duty. A poor guardian, who may be expending his time and effort to the detriment of his own livelihood, is permitted to take a fee that is fair and appropriate (maʿrūf), but never excessive. The verse then commands transparency: “When you give orphans back their property, call in witnesses.” This ensures a clear record of the handover, preventing later disputes. The final sentence, “And sufficient is Allah as a vigilant Reckoner,” reminds all parties that even if human witnesses can be evaded, Allah’s accounting is absolute and unfailing. Together, these instructions create a comprehensive system of trust, testing, restraint, fairness, and accountability, transforming the guardianship of orphans from a potential avenue of exploitation into a sacred duty overseen by God Himself.
7 | For men there is a share in what their parents and close relatives leave, and for women there is a share in what their parents and close relatives leave—whether it is little or much. ˹These are˺ obligatory shares. | This brief verse delivers a revolutionary principle in a society that had systematically denied women any inheritance. In pre-Islamic Arabia, inheritance was reserved for adult males who could fight and defend the tribe; women and children were routinely dispossessed. The verse overturns that custom with unmistakable clarity: “For men there is a share… and for women there is a share.” The deliberate repetition of “a share” for both genders stresses that inheritance is not a gift or favour from male relatives but a divinely ordained right. The source of the estate is “what their parents and close relatives leave,” meaning this ruling applies to the immediate family property, not some discretionary allowance. The phrase “whether it is little or much” is particularly significant. It closes the door to any argument that a small estate is not worth dividing or that women should only receive something if the inheritance is substantial. No matter how meagre the total wealth may be, women’s entitlement is as real and enforceable as that of men. The final words, “These are obligatory shares,” use the Arabic term farīḍah, which denotes a fixed, binding duty prescribed by Allah. This means that no human authority, no family council, no cultural tradition has the right to alter or ignore these shares. By framing inheritance as an obligatory share from Allah, the verse elevates women’s economic rights to the level of religious obligation. The immediate effect in the early Muslim community was that women who had been treated as property to be inherited now became owners of property in their own right. This verse is the gateway to the detailed inheritance laws that follow, and it stands as a permanent witness that Islam, from its inception, recognised the financial independence and legal personality of women.
8 | If ˹non-inheriting˺ relatives, orphans, or the needy are present at the time of distribution, offer them a ˹small˺ provision from it and speak to them kindly. | This verse complements the legal precision of obligatory shares with a profound ethical instruction. The context is the actual occasion when an estate is being divided. At such a gathering, it is likely that people who are not legal heirs will be present. The verse specifies three categories: “non-inheriting relatives, orphans, or the needy.” Non-inheriting relatives could be distant kin who, due to the presence of closer heirs, receive no fixed share. Orphans are those who have lost their fathers and are particularly vulnerable. The needy are the poor who may have no family tie to the deceased at all. The command is to “offer them a small provision from it.” This is not a legally enforceable share but a voluntary act of kindness, a gift from the estate before or during its formal division. The Arabic word translated as “provision” (rizq) conveys the sense of sustenance and blessing. The amount is intentionally left unspecified; it should be “small” so as not to encroach unjustly on the rights of the legal heirs, yet meaningful enough to bring comfort. The verse then adds: “and speak to them kindly.” The material gift must be accompanied by gracious words. Those who have no legal claim might feel embarrassed or grieved at watching others receive what they themselves will not. They might even be tempted to harbour envy. The heirs are therefore instructed to speak kindly, to soften the disappointment, to avoid boasting, and to make the non-inheritors feel valued and respected. This teaching transforms a potentially tense and sorrowful occasion into an opportunity for generosity and dignity. It shows that while the law protects fixed rights, the spirit of Islam encourages believers to go beyond the letter of the law and to embrace compassion, especially in moments when wealth is changing hands and hearts may be vulnerable.
9 | Let the guardians be as concerned ˹for the orphans˺ as they would if they were to ˹die and˺ leave ˹their own˺ helpless children behind. So let them be mindful of Allah and speak equitably. | This verse appeals to the deepest human emotion of parental love to instil justice and mercy in those who oversee orphans. It draws a striking analogy: “Let the guardians be as concerned for the orphans as they would if they were to die and leave their own helpless children behind.” Every guardian is asked to imagine himself on his deathbed, leaving his own young children at the mercy of other people. How would he want those future guardians to treat his little ones? The intense anxiety and protective love a parent feels for his own children become the standard for treating the orphan under his care. This psychological technique is exceptionally powerful because it bypasses abstract moralising and directly accesses the heart’s natural empathy. The verse then draws the conclusion: “So let them be mindful of Allah and speak equitably.” Mindfulness of Allah here is the driving force that makes the imagined scenario actionable. It is because one fears Allah and knows His watchfulness that one translates that emotional concern into actual fair treatment. The command to “speak equitably” adds the dimension of verbal justice. The guardian must not only handle the orphan’s wealth correctly but also speak to the orphan with fairness, avoiding harsh words, intimidation, or belittling remarks. The term “equitably” (sadīdan) implies speech that is truthful, appropriate, and aimed at what is right. Some commentators note that the guardian should address the orphan with the same gentleness and respect he would love his own children to receive. This verse thus educates a community to internalise compassion as the guiding principle of social care, making the orphan’s welfare not a cold legal duty but a heartfelt responsibility rooted in the most universal of human feelings: the love for one’s own offspring.
10 | Indeed, those who unjustly consume orphans’ wealth ˹in fact˺ consume nothing but fire into their bellies. And they will be burned in a blazing Hell! | After the compassionate appeal in the previous verse, the surah delivers a terrifying warning to those who ignore that appeal and persist in exploitation. The verse opens with a striking metaphor: “Indeed, those who unjustly consume orphans’ wealth in fact consume nothing but fire into their bellies.” What they think is delicious, illicit gain is, in spiritual reality, not nourishing wealth but blazing fire that they are swallowing. This image serves to completely reframe the sinner’s perception. In the moment of eating, drinking, or spending that ill-gotten money, they feel pleasure or satisfaction, but the unseen truth is that every mouthful is fuelling their own destruction. The Arabic expression uses the intensive form, conveying that this consumption is an ongoing, deliberate action. The second part of the verse makes the outcome explicit: “And they will be burned in a blazing Hell!” The fire that they metaphorically consumed in this world will manifest as literal, burning punishment in the Hereafter. There is a terrible appropriateness here: the belly that was filled unlawfully becomes the very site of torment. This severe warning is not without reason. Orphans are among the most defenceless members of society. To steal from them is to exploit someone who has lost their natural protector, to kick a person who is already down. Because the crime is so heinous and the victim so vulnerable, the punishment corresponds in intensity. The verse also serves as a psychological barrier for anyone entrusted with the property of others. The graphic imagery of eating fire lodges in the mind and acts as a deterrent far beyond what a simple legal prohibition could achieve. It reminds believers that worldly transactions have spiritual consequences, and that every bite of unjust gain will be accounted for on a Day when no wealth will avail except a sound heart.
11 | Allah commands you regarding your children: the share of the male will be twice that of the female. If you leave only two ˹or more˺ females, their share is two-thirds of the estate. But if there is only one female, her share will be one-half. Each parent is entitled to one-sixth if you leave offspring. But if you are childless and your parents are the only heirs, then your mother will receive one-third. But if you leave siblings, then your mother will receive one-sixth—after the fulfilment of bequests and debts. ˹Be fair to˺ your parents and children, as you do not ˹fully˺ know who is more beneficial to you. ˹This is˺ an obligation from Allah. Surely Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | This verse begins the detailed exposition of inheritance shares, and it does so with the divine authority of “Allah commands you.” The first and perhaps most discussed ruling is that “the share of the male will be twice that of the female” when children inherit together. This has often been misunderstood as a blanket devaluation of women, but the verse must be read within the entire Islamic economic framework. In Islam, a woman’s wealth is her own; she has no legal obligation to spend on anyone, while a man is legally required to provide for his wife, children, and needy relatives. His extra share is therefore not a privilege but a responsibility-laden allocation. The verse then addresses cases where only daughters survive: “If you leave only two or more females, their share is two-thirds of the estate. But if there is only one female, her share will be one-half.” This guarantees daughters a substantial portion, directly countering pre-Islamic customs that gave them nothing. The parents’ shares are then detailed: “Each parent is entitled to one-sixth if you leave offspring.” If the deceased is childless and the parents are the only heirs, the mother receives one-third and the father the remainder. If the deceased has siblings, the mother’s share is reduced to one-sixth, with the father receiving the rest. All of this is “after the fulfilment of bequests and debts.” Debts are given priority, then any valid bequest up to one-third of the remaining estate. The parenthetical instruction, “Be fair to your parents and children, as you do not fully know who is more beneficial to you,” is a profound exhortation. People sometimes favour one child or one parent over another based on emotion or perceived benefit, but Allah, who is All-Knowing, has already apportioned the shares in perfect wisdom. The believer is called to trust that divine distribution is more just and more beneficial than any human preference. The verse closes by declaring these shares “an obligation from Allah” and reiterating two divine names: All-Knowing, All-Wise. This assures the believers that these laws are not arbitrary but are rooted in absolute knowledge and wisdom, and it calls them to submit even when the division might not match their personal inclinations.
12 | You will inherit half of what your wives leave if they are childless. But if they have children, then ˹your share is˺ one-fourth of the estate—after the fulfilment of bequests and debts. And your wives will inherit one-fourth of what you leave if you are childless. But if you have children, then your wives will receive one-eighth of your estate—after the fulfilment of bequests and debts. And if a man or a woman leaves neither parents nor children but only a brother or a sister ˹from their mother’s side˺, they will each inherit one-sixth, but if they are more than one, they ˹all˺ will share one-third of the estate—after the fulfilment of bequests and debts without harm ˹to the heirs˺. ˹This is˺ a commandment from Allah. And Allah is All-Knowing, Most Forbearing. | Continuing the inheritance rulings, this verse addresses the shares of spouses and the special category of maternal siblings. It first establishes the husband’s share from his deceased wife: “You will inherit half of what your wives leave if they are childless.” If the wife has no surviving children, the husband receives half the estate. However, “if they have children, then your share is one-fourth of the estate.” The children, whether from the current marriage or a previous one, reduce the husband’s portion, because his financial responsibilities are partly offset by the children’s own shares. The verse then mirrors these provisions for the wife: “And your wives will inherit one-fourth of what you leave if you are childless. But if you have children, then your wives will receive one-eighth of your estate.” The wife’s share is half of the husband’s share in the parallel situation, again reflecting the Islamic economic structure in which men bear primary financial responsibility for the household. The phrase “after the fulfilment of bequests and debts” is repeated, underscoring that no heir receives anything until all legitimate debts and any valid bequests are settled. The verse then introduces the case of kalālah, a person who dies leaving neither parents nor children. For such a person, maternal siblings inherit: “And if a man or a woman leaves neither parents nor children but only a brother or a sister from their mother’s side, they will each inherit one-sixth, but if they are more than one, they all will share one-third of the estate.” This special share for uterine siblings ensures that relatives on the mother’s side, who might otherwise be overlooked in a patrilineal system, are not left destitute. The condition “without harm to the heirs” is a crucial ethical limitation. Any bequest made must not deliberately injure the legal heirs by, for instance, trying to disinherit them or giving away so much that their obligatory shares are compromised. The verse closes with a repetition of the divine attributes “All-Knowing, Most Forbearing.” The mention of forbearance (ḥilm) is especially poignant here: Allah knows that people may feel discontent or confusion about these fixed shares, but He is not hasty in punishing that discontent. Instead, He gives time for reflection, learning, and acceptance.
13 | These ˹entitlements˺ are the limits set by Allah. Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger will be admitted into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever. That is the ultimate triumph! | After detailing the inheritance portions, the verse emphatically states: “These entitlements are the limits set by Allah.” The word “limits” (ḥudūd) refers to the divinely prescribed boundaries that must not be transgressed. In the context of inheritance, stepping beyond these limits means either adding to or subtracting from the fixed shares, denying rightful heirs, or creating illegitimate claims. The verse elevates adherence to these laws from a mere legal transaction to a matter of faith and eternal consequence. It declares, “Whoever obeys Allah and His Messenger will be admitted into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever.” Obedience is not restricted to inheritance; the phrase encompasses the whole of the religious message, but the immediate context links it tightly to respecting these financial bounds. The reward is described with a vivid image of eternal bliss: gardens with flowing rivers, permanent residence, never to be expelled. And then the verse exclaims: “That is the ultimate triumph!” The Arabic word fawz conveys a spectacular success, an escape from peril combined with the attainment of the highest good. The inheritance laws are thus reframed as a test of submission. The true believer recognises that these shares come from the One who knows all hidden needs and future consequences, and that following them is not a worldly loss but the path to the greatest victory imaginable. This verse ensures that Muslims do not see inheritance merely as a cold, mathematical division of property but as a profoundly spiritual act of worship, where every correctly distributed dirham becomes a cause for entry into Paradise.
14 | But whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger and exceeds their limits will be cast into Hell, to stay there forever. And they will suffer a humiliating punishment. | The stark contrast to the previous verse is immediate. If obedience to the inheritance limits leads to eternal bliss, “whoever disobeys Allah and His Messenger and exceeds their limits will be cast into Hell, to stay there forever.” The phrase “exceeds their limits” refers directly to transgressing the prescribed shares, whether by denying an heir their right, claiming more than one’s due, or manipulating the rules through fraudulent bequests. Such actions are not considered minor legal mistakes but acts of defiance against Allah and His Messenger. The punishment described is both physical and psychological: being cast into Hell and remaining there forever is the ultimate loss, but it is compounded by “a humiliating punishment.” The humiliation is deeply tied to the nature of the sin. A person who grabbed more wealth than they deserved, who perhaps felt clever or powerful by circumventing the divine law, will be disgraced in the most public and absolute manner. The greed that caused them to trample over the rights of orphans, widows, or siblings will be exposed, and the very thing they wrongly consumed will become a source of eternal shame. By placing this verse immediately after the promise of Gardens, the surah creates a powerful motivational structure: the believer is suspended between the hope of ultimate triumph and the fear of ultimate loss. This dual presentation of reward and punishment is characteristic of the Qur’anic method of education, which engages both the human longing for joy and the human aversion to pain, channelling them toward righteousness. The inheritance laws are thus invested with a gravity that transforms a court ruling into a matter of eternal destiny.
15 | ˹As for˺ those of your women who commit illegal intercourse—call four witnesses from among yourselves. If they testify, confine the offenders to their homes until they die or Allah ordains a ˹different˺ way for them. | The surah now turns to a different societal concern: the regulation of sexual conduct. This verse addresses a specific case: “As for those of your women who commit illegal intercourse.” The phrase “your women” refers to women within the Muslim community. The crime indicated is zinā, illicit sexual relations outside of marriage. The verse first lays down an evidentiary requirement: “call four witnesses from among yourselves.” Four male witnesses of upright character must have directly observed the act. This standard is deliberately stringent, almost impossible to meet in normal circumstances, which effectively shifts the emphasis from punitive action to the protection of privacy and reputation. False accusations, as will be explained in Sūrat al-Nūr, carry severe penalties. If, however, the impossible standard of four witnesses is met and the guilt is established, the prescribed punishment at this stage was “confine the offenders to their homes until they die or Allah ordains a different way for them.” This ruling was an early, transitional punishment before the later revelation of flogging and stoning in specific cases. The confinement was a form of social isolation and restriction, and the phrase “until they die” indicates that the punishment could be lifelong unless Allah provided a new ruling. The closing words, “or Allah ordains a different way for them,” contain a prophecy that the law would be modified, and indeed later verses introduced flogging for unmarried offenders and the established sunnah prescribed stoning for married adulterers under strict conditions. This verse, understood in its chronological context, reveals the gradual divine pedagogy: the early Muslim community was being weaned away from the deep-rooted sexual licentiousness of pre-Islamic society, and the laws were given in stages to make the transition psychologically and socially feasible. The principle of requiring four witnesses remains a permanent feature, safeguarding individuals from slander and ensuring that punishments are only applied when there is no shadow of doubt.
16 | And the two among you who commit this sin—discipline them. If they repent and mend their ways, relieve them. Surely Allah is ever Accepting of Repentance, Most Merciful. | The previous verse addressed the case of women and prescribed confinement. This verse turns to the case of two individuals, understood by many scholars to refer to the two men involved in homosexual acts or, according to another view, to an unmarried couple. The text says: “And the two among you who commit this sin—discipline them.” The Arabic word for “discipline” (ādhūhumā) implies causing them harm as a corrective measure, which could include verbal reprimand, beating, public censure, or other forms of punishment short of the fixed legal penalty that would later be instituted. At this early stage, the emphasis is on correcting the behaviour through community-enforced discipline. The verse then opens a powerful door to reform: “If they repent and mend their ways, relieve them.” Repentance is not a mere verbal apology; it must be accompanied by “mending their ways,” a visible, sustained change in conduct. When such sincere transformation is evident, the command to the community is to “relieve them,” to stop the disciplinary measures. This creates a direct incentive for the offenders to turn back to Allah, knowing that the community’s response will be one of forgiveness and reintegration rather than permanent stigma. The verse grounds this merciful approach in the divine nature: “Surely Allah is ever Accepting of Repentance, Most Merciful.” The Arabic tawwāb means that Allah constantly turns back to those who turn to Him; He not only accepts repentance but actively facilitates it. The community is thus called to reflect divine mercy in its own dealings. This combination of firmness against sin and openness to reform is a hallmark of Qur’anic ethics. It prevents the community from becoming either permissive on the one hand or unforgiving and cruel on the other, charting a balanced path that seeks the spiritual recovery of the offender.
17 | Allah only accepts the repentance of those who commit evil ignorantly ˹or recklessly˺ then repent soon after—Allah will pardon them. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | Building on the theme of repentance from the previous verse, this passage clarifies whose repentance is genuinely accepted. “Allah only accepts the repentance of those who commit evil ignorantly or recklessly then repent soon after.” The phrase “ignorantly or recklessly” (bi-jahālah) is crucial. It does not mean that the sinner was completely unaware that the act was wrong; rather, it describes the state of every person who sins, because at the moment of succumbing to desire or anger, their knowledge of the consequences is clouded, and they are acting under a kind of reckless ignorance. No true believer sins with full, calm, and deliberate defiance of Allah while fully conscious of His majesty. The verse further stipulates that they “repent soon after,” meaning they do not delay and allow the sin to harden into a persistent habit. The time frame is not specified in days or weeks; it is a matter of the heart’s quick return to remorse and correction. “Allah will pardon them” is a divine promise of forgiveness for such people. The verse ends with “And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.” He knows the sincerity of the repentance, the hidden intentions, and the future resolve of the person, and He wisely determines whether the repentance meets the standard of acceptance. This teaching guards against two dangerous extremes: despair on the one hand, where a person thinks their sin is too great to be forgiven, and complacency on the other, where a person assumes they can sin indefinitely and repent on their deathbed. It encourages an immediate, responsive conscience that quickly recoils from wrongdoing and seeks Allah’s pardon without procrastination.
18 | However, repentance is not accepted from those who knowingly persist in sin until they start dying, and then cry, “Now I repent!” nor those who die as disbelievers. For them We have prepared a painful punishment. | The previous verse described the repentance that Allah accepts. This verse contrasts it with two categories of people whose repentance is rejected. The first category comprises “those who knowingly persist in sin until they start dying, and then cry, ‘Now I repent!’” Persistence in sin here implies a deliberate, habitual continuation without remorse, treating Allah’s forbearance as a licence to transgress. When death finally arrives and the person sees the angels of death, only then does fear produce a panicked declaration of repentance. Such a declaration is not born of genuine love for Allah or true remorse for the sin; it is a desperate reaction to an unavoidable reality. The Qur’an likens this to the repentance of Pharaoh, who declared faith only as he was drowning. The second category is “those who die as disbelievers.” A person who leaves this world in a state of disbelief has closed the door of faith permanently, because the test of worldly life has ended. For both groups, the verdict is devastating: “For them We have prepared a painful punishment.” The direct implication for believers is a stark warning against procrastination in turning back to Allah. The verse draws a clear boundary between the worldly realm, where repentance is open and effective, and the moment of death, which slams that door shut. It cultivates a sense of urgency, urging people to examine their lives now, to repent sincerely, and to abandon sinful patterns before the sudden arrival of a moment when repentance will be of no avail.
19 | O believers! It is not permissible for you to inherit women against their will or mistreat them to make them return some of the dowry ˹as a ransom for divorce˺—unless they are found guilty of adultery. Treat them fairly. If you happen to dislike them, you may hate something which Allah turns into a great blessing. | The surah returns to the theme of women’s rights within marriage, addressing two specific pre-Islamic abuses. “O believers! It is not permissible for you to inherit women against their will.” In pre-Islamic Arabia, when a man died, his heir could effectively inherit the widow as part of the estate, forcing her into marriage or preventing her from remarrying so he could control her wealth. This verse categorically abolishes that practice: a woman is not property to be inherited. The second prohibition is “or mistreat them to make them return some of the dowry as a ransom for divorce.” A husband who wanted to divorce his wife but also wanted to reclaim the dowry he had given her might subject her to harassment, neglect, and harm until she, in desperation, offered to give back the dowry to secure her release. The verse forbids such calculated cruelty. The exception “unless they are found guilty of adultery” refers to cases where the wife is proven guilty of blatant immorality, which might justify a different course of action, but that is not a licence for domestic oppression. The verse then instructs: “Treat them fairly.” This simple command encompasses all aspects of marital life: kindness in speech, justice in provision, and respect in daily interaction. Then comes a profound psychological insight: “If you happen to dislike them, you may hate something which Allah turns into a great blessing.” A husband may feel aversion toward his wife for some reason, but Allah may have placed immense good in her and in the continuation of the marriage, whether through righteous children, personal growth through patience, or unforeseen happiness. The verse teaches believers to transcend transient feelings and to trust in Allah’s wisdom, maintaining honourable treatment even when their emotions fluctuate. This is a timeless marital counsel that has saved countless families from breaking apart over resolvable discontent.
20 | If you desire to replace a wife with another and you have given the former ˹even˺ a stack of gold ˹as a dowry˺, do not take any of it back. Would you ˹still˺ take it unjustly and very sinfully? | Having forbidden the mistreatment of a wife to extort the dowry, the verse now addresses the situation where a husband voluntarily decides to divorce one wife in order to marry another. “If you desire to replace a wife with another” captures the scenario: the husband is not being driven by the wife’s misconduct; the decision arises from his own inclination. The verse then poses a hypothetical but striking image: “and you have given the former even a stack of gold as a dowry, do not take any of it back.” A “stack of gold” (qinṭār) was an enormous amount of wealth, perhaps a treasure piled high. Even if the dowry was that immense, the husband has no right to reclaim a single coin. The dowry, once given, becomes the wife’s exclusive property, and the divorce does not nullify that ownership. The verse then drives the point home with a rhetorical question full of moral outrage: “Would you still take it unjustly and very sinfully?” The phrase “unjustly” (buhtānan) suggests a blatant falsehood, because taking back a gift after intimate marital life is a kind of slander against the wife’s dignity and a denial of the validity of her right. “Very sinfully” (ithmam mubīnan) emphasises that this act is not a minor error but a clear, manifest sin. This verse seals the financial protection of women at the point of divorce, ensuring that a husband cannot use the dissolution of the marriage as an opportunity to plunder what he had earlier given. It upholds the integrity of the dowry as a pure gift and a symbol of the seriousness of the marital contract, a contract that even when voluntarily ended does not erase the financial rights that have accrued to the wife.
21 | And how could you take it back after having enjoyed each other intimately and she has taken from you a firm commitment? | The previous verse forbade a husband from reclaiming the dowry when he divorces a wife to marry another. This verse intensifies the moral argument by appealing to the sacred intimacy that the marital relationship has created. It asks, with incredulity, “And how could you take it back after having enjoyed each other intimately?” The expression “enjoyed each other intimately” translates the Arabic afḍā, which means to be alone together in the full consummation of marriage, to share the most private and vulnerable physical union. The verse reminds the husband that the dowry was given in exchange for that lawful intimacy, and once that bond has been formed, reclaiming the dowry is a profound breach of decency. The second part of the verse adds another dimension: “and she has taken from you a firm commitment?” This refers to the solemn covenant, the mīthāq ghalīẓ, the marriage contract itself, which is described elsewhere in the Qur’an as a strong and weighty pledge. A woman enters marriage relying on that commitment; she gives up her single life, places her trust in her husband, and structures her future around the security of this bond. For the husband to then dissolve the marriage and demand back what he gave is to treat that firm covenant as trivial. Some scholars note that the “firm commitment” also alludes to the words spoken during the marriage contract, often including the command to hold the wife in honourable companionship or release her in kindness. By evoking the intimacy and the covenant, the verse dismantles any remaining justification for financial exploitation at divorce. It appeals to the husband’s sense of honour, gratitude, and God-consciousness, making it clear that taking back the dowry after the marriage has been consummated is not merely a legal violation but a moral and emotional betrayal that contradicts the very spirit of the marital bond.
22 | Do not marry former wives of your fathers—except what was done previously. It was indeed a shameful, despicable, and evil practice. | From the financial protection of women, the surah moves to a series of prohibitions regarding which women one may marry. This verse begins with a clear command: “Do not marry former wives of your fathers.” In pre-Islamic Arab society, it was customary for a son to inherit his father’s wives along with the rest of the estate, marrying them or giving them away. The verse abolishes this custom entirely. Marrying one’s stepmother, a woman who was in a marital relationship with one’s own father, violates the fundamental respect owed to the father and blurs the lines of familial relations in a deeply harmful way. The verse adds the clause “except what was done previously,” meaning that those who had entered such marriages before the revelation of this ruling were not to be punished retroactively; the prohibition applies from this point forward. The past is pardoned, but the practice must cease. The verse then delivers a threefold condemnation: “It was indeed a shameful, despicable, and evil practice.” The Arabic words used are fāḥishah (an indecent, obscene act), maqtan (something intensely hateful and detestable), and sāʾa sabīlan (an evil path). This piling up of strong negative descriptions underscores that the act is not just unlawful but morally repulsive on multiple levels. It corrupts the father-son relationship, destroys the dignity of the woman who moves from being a mother-figure to a wife, and creates a confused household structure where former intimacies are overturned. The emphatic language serves to eradicate any lingering cultural attachment to this custom and to reorient the community’s moral compass, establishing that marriage must be built on clear, dignified relationships, free from the shadows of previous generational entanglements.
23 | ˹Also˺ forbidden to you for marriage are your mothers, your daughters, your sisters, your paternal and maternal aunts, your brother’s daughters, your sister’s daughters, your foster-mothers, your foster-sisters, your mothers-in-law, your stepdaughters under your guardianship if you have consummated marriage with their mothers—but if you have not, then you can marry them —nor the wives of your own sons, nor two sisters together at the same time —except what was done previously. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | This verse presents a comprehensive list of women with whom marriage is permanently forbidden, establishing the core framework of prohibited degrees of kinship in Islamic law. It begins with the closest blood relations: “your mothers, your daughters, your sisters.” The prohibition of marrying one’s mother or daughter is an almost universal human taboo, rooted in the natural respect owed to the origin of one’s existence and the fruit of one’s loins. The sister is added as the closest collateral relative. The verse then extends to aunts: “your paternal and maternal aunts,” and further to nieces: “your brother’s daughters, your sister’s daughters.” These categories cover the immediate extensions of the parental and sibling relationships, preserving the purity of the extended family circle and preventing the complex emotional entanglements that would arise if uncles married nieces. The verse then moves to relationships created through breastfeeding: “your foster-mothers, your foster-sisters.” Islamic law accords breastfeeding the same legal effect as blood relationship regarding marriage prohibitions, based on the principle that the suckling infant becomes a child of the wet-nurse, and her children become siblings. The next category is relationships by marriage: “your mothers-in-law, your stepdaughters under your guardianship if you have consummated marriage with their mothers—but if you have not, then you can marry them.” The stepdaughter is forbidden only if the marriage to her mother has been consummated, creating a real family bond. If the marriage was never consummated and ended, then the stepdaughter remains lawful. The verse also prohibits “the wives of your own sons,” protecting the son’s marital bond from any encroachment, just as the father’s wife was protected earlier. Finally, the verse prohibits marrying “two sisters together at the same time,” which would cause jealousy and enmity between sisters who should be each other’s closest supporters. The closing “except what was done previously” again pardons pre-Islamic actions, while “Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful” assures that past transgressions in ignorance are forgiven. This list is not merely a set of legal rules; it draws clear boundaries that protect family harmony, define the limits of natural affection, and prevent the household from becoming a place of sexual competition.
24 | Also ˹forbidden are˺ married women—except ˹female˺ captives in your possession. This is Allah’s commandment to you. ˹But˺ lawful to you are all beyond these—as long as you seek them with your wealth in a legal marriage, not in fornication. Give those you have consummated marriage with their due dowries. It is permissible to be mutually gracious regarding the set dowry. Surely Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | Continuing the prohibitions, this verse adds a critical category: “Also forbidden are married women.” A woman who is already married cannot be married to another man, as this would destroy the existing marital bond and lead to chaos in lineage. The only exception is “except female captives in your possession,” which refers to the specific historical context of captives taken in legitimate warfare. Such women, whose marriages were effectively severed by the circumstances of war and captivity, became lawful to their captors under strict regulations, provided they were not pregnant and the proper waiting period was observed. This ruling was a transitional measure within a broader system that strongly encouraged the freeing of slaves and the elevation of their status. The verse then declares: “This is Allah’s commandment to you,” underscoring the divine authority of these boundaries. Having laid out the forbidden categories, the verse opens the door to the lawful: “But lawful to you are all beyond these—as long as you seek them with your wealth in a legal marriage, not in fornication.” The phrase “seek them with your wealth” refers to the dowry, a necessary component of a valid marriage. The condition “not in fornication” explicitly excludes temporary, secret, or purely carnal arrangements. The verse then reinforces the command to give dowries: “Give those you have consummated marriage with their due dowries.” Once the marriage is consummated, the dowry becomes an absolute right. If the marriage is dissolved before consummation, half the dowry is due according to other rulings. The verse then adds a note of flexibility: “It is permissible to be mutually gracious regarding the set dowry.” Either spouse may willingly agree to adjust the amount, whether by the wife reducing it or the husband increasing it, as long as the adjustment is based on mutual goodwill, not coercion. The verse ends with “Surely Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise,” reminding the believers that these regulations are not arbitrary but issued from perfect knowledge of human nature and perfect wisdom in structuring a healthy, stable society.
25 | But if any of you cannot afford to marry a free believing woman, then ˹let him marry˺ a believing bondwoman possessed by one of you. Allah knows best ˹the state of˺ your faith ˹and theirs˺. You are from one another. So marry them with the permission of their owners, giving them their dowry in fairness, if they are chaste, neither promiscuous nor having secret affairs. If they commit indecency after marriage, they receive half the punishment of free women. This is for those of you who fear falling into sin. But if you are patient, it is better for you. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | This verse acknowledges the economic realities of the early Muslim community and provides a lawful alternative for men who could not afford the dowry and maintenance of a free woman. “But if any of you cannot afford to marry a free believing woman, then let him marry a believing bondwoman possessed by one of you.” The condition of faith is paramount; the bondwoman must be a believer. The verse then offers a profound equalising statement: “Allah knows best the state of your faith and theirs. You are from one another.” In the sight of Allah, the free and the slave are equal in their essential humanity and in the value of their faith. Social status does not determine spiritual rank. This teaching undercuts any arrogance based on free status and affirms the full religious personhood of the bondwoman. The marriage must be conducted with propriety: “So marry them with the permission of their owners, giving them their dowry in fairness.” The bondwoman is not to be used without the consent of her guardian-owner, and she must receive a fair dowry, just like a free woman. The requirement that they be “chaste, neither promiscuous nor having secret affairs” protects the moral integrity of the marital bond. The verse then sets a distinct legal penalty: “If they commit indecency after marriage, they receive half the punishment of free women.” This halving of the penalty reflects their different social circumstances and the reduced degree of autonomy they held, yet it still affirms their accountability before the law. The verse identifies the target audience: “This is for those of you who fear falling into sin.” For a man who struggles to remain chaste, marrying a bondwoman is a lawful release. Yet the verse closes with a higher exhortation: “But if you are patient, it is better for you.” Patience and self-restraint, even in difficulty, are more praiseworthy. The final words, “And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful,” console those who may have already slipped and encourage them to seek lawful means and trust in divine mercy. This verse thus offers a pragmatic solution while constantly pointing toward higher spiritual ideals.
26 | It is Allah’s Will to make things clear to you, guide you to the ˹noble˺ ways of those before you, and turn to you in mercy. For Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | After the detailed legal rulings on marriage, dowries, and social relations, this verse pauses to explain the divine purpose behind these instructions. “It is Allah’s Will to make things clear to you.” The Arabic yurīdu Allāh conveys a deliberate, ongoing divine intention. Allah does not leave humanity in confusion about how to live; He reveals detailed guidance so that the path of righteousness is distinct from the path of error. The ambiguity of pre-Islamic customs is swept away by the clarity of revelation. The verse then adds a second purpose: “guide you to the noble ways of those before you.” The “ways” (sunan) refer to the righteous patterns established by previous prophets and their followers. By studying the lives of Abraham, Moses, and other messengers, and by observing how they built just, God-fearing communities, the Muslims can follow in their footsteps. This connection to past righteousness provides a sense of belonging to a long, honourable tradition rather than feeling like an isolated new movement. The third purpose is deeply tender: “and turn to you in mercy.” The Arabic yatūba ʿalaykum literally means to turn in forgiveness, but it is paired here with mercy. The very act of sending these laws is an act of divine mercy, because through them, people can repent from their past ignorance and wrongdoings and be guided to a life that pleases Allah. The verse closes with “For Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.” The rulings may sometimes feel challenging, but they issue from a knowledge that encompasses every hidden consequence and a wisdom that orders human life for the best outcome in both this world and the next. This verse reassures the believers that the legal details they have been given are not a burden but a manifestation of Allah’s loving desire to clarify, connect, and forgive.
27 | And it is Allah’s Will to turn to you in grace, but those who follow their desires wish to see you deviate entirely ˹from Allah’s Way˺. | Building on the previous verse, this verse continues to describe the divine will and contrasts it with the will of those who are driven by lust. “And it is Allah’s Will to turn to you in grace.” The word “grace” (tawbah) is the same root as repentance, but here it emphasises the positive, merciful turning of Allah toward His servants, inviting them to a life of purity and blessing. This divine will is not grudging or reluctant; it is an active, generous desire to see believers succeed. In stark opposition stands another force: “but those who follow their desires wish to see you deviate entirely from Allah’s Way.” The people of unbridled desires—whether they are hypocrites, disbelievers, or those within the community who prioritise their passions over divine law—are not content with their own deviation. They actively wish for the believers to abandon the straight path and tumble into the same moral confusion. The word “entirely” (maylan ʿaẓīman) indicates a great, wholesale deviation, not a minor slip. This verse exposes the spiritual battle at the heart of the surah’s social legislation. Every divine command that restricts sexual licence, mandates financial honesty, or upholds family dignity is a direct challenge to the desires of those who want a society without limits. The believers are warned that they will face pressure, both subtle and overt, to compromise. Yet the verse also comforts: Allah’s will is stronger, and His turning toward the believers is a protective shield. If they hold fast, they are aligning themselves with the Creator’s intention, while the followers of desire are fighting a losing battle against the very purpose for which humanity was created.
28 | And it is Allah’s Will to lighten your burdens, for humankind was created weak. | This verse delivers one of the most merciful declarations in the surah. “And it is Allah’s Will to lighten your burdens.” After the extensive legal commands and prohibitions, one might feel a sense of overwhelming obligation. This verse assures the believers that Allah does not intend to overburden them. His legislation is designed to be bearable, to remove hardship, not to impose it. The shariah is full of concessions: the permission to marry bondwomen for those who cannot afford free women, the allowance for divorce when reconciliation is impossible, the permissibility of shortening prayers during travel, and many other easements. All of this flows from the divine will to alleviate, not to crush. The verse then provides the reason: “for humankind was created weak.” This is a profound anthropological statement. Human beings are weak in their physical constitution, their emotional resilience, their resistance to temptation, and their resolve. They tire easily, they falter, they are susceptible to despair, lust, and impatience. Allah, the Creator, knows this inherent frailty because He is the one who placed it there as part of the human condition. The laws are therefore tailored to this weakness, not to an idealised standard of angelic perfection. The prohibition of certain indulgences is itself a mercy, because unregulated desire would destroy the weak human. The permissions and concessions are a mercy, because demanding constant perfection would break the weak human. This verse inoculates believers against two extremes: despair over one’s failings, and arrogance that treats the law as a rigid, merciless code. It invites a humble recognition of one’s own weakness and a grateful reliance on the Lord who knows that weakness and legislates accordingly.
29 | O believers! Do not devour one another’s wealth illegally, but rather trade by mutual consent. And do not kill ˹each other or˺ yourselves. Surely Allah is ever Merciful to you. | The surah returns to financial ethics with a sweeping command. “O believers! Do not devour one another’s wealth illegally.” The verb “devour” (taʾkulū) paints a vivid picture of consuming wealth greedily and destructively. The prohibition encompasses all forms of unlawful acquisition: theft, fraud, bribery, gambling, usury, and any transaction that does not meet the standards of justice. The verse then provides the positive alternative: “but rather trade by mutual consent.” Commerce based on mutual agreement, free from coercion and deception, is the lawful means of wealth transfer. The phrase “mutual consent” (tarāḍin) elevates the principle of free and informed agreement to a core requirement of economic exchange. Neither party should feel cheated, pressured, or misled. The verse then shifts to an even graver prohibition: “And do not kill each other or yourselves.” The expression “do not kill yourselves” has been interpreted in two complementary ways: do not kill one another, since the believers are like a single body; and do not commit suicide. Both meanings are valid and interconnected. The prohibition of murder and self-harm follows the prohibition of devouring wealth, perhaps because financial exploitation can lead to desperation, violence, and even suicide. The verse closes with a reminder of the divine attribute that grounds all these protections: “Surely Allah is ever Merciful to you.” It is Allah’s mercy that prohibits people from harming one another’s lives and property. These are not arbitrary restrictions but safeguards flowing from a Lord who cares for the well-being of His servants. The verse thus weaves together the sanctity of wealth and the sanctity of life, both essential for a stable, merciful society.
30 | And whoever does this sinfully and unjustly, We will burn them in the Fire. That is easy for Allah. | The previous verse prohibited devouring wealth unlawfully and killing. This verse now pronounces the consequences for those who violate those prohibitions. “And whoever does this sinfully and unjustly” refers back to the acts of consuming others’ wealth and killing. The two qualifiers, “sinfully” (ʿudwānan) and “unjustly” (ẓulman), together indicate an intentional, aggressive transgression without any mitigating circumstance. The person knows the prohibition and deliberately crosses it, driven by enmity and injustice. The punishment is described starkly: “We will burn them in the Fire.” The use of the first-person plural “We” emphasises the majesty and power of the divine speaker. The burning is not a momentary pain but an ongoing, enveloping torment. The verse then adds a phrase that magnifies the severity of the threat: “That is easy for Allah.” This short statement has a profound impact. It reminds the sinner that the eternal punishment, which to human imagination seems unimaginably difficult, poses no difficulty whatsoever for the Creator. Allah’s power is absolute; the execution of justice is effortless for Him. No one can escape, no one can resist, no one can exhaust His ability to punish. At the same time, the verse serves as a warning that this ease of punishment should not be tested. The believer is called to reflect: if evading this consequence is impossible, then the only rational course is to refrain from the forbidden acts and to seek the mercy that Allah also abundantly offers. The juxtaposition of this severe verse with the previous reminder of divine mercy creates a balanced state of hopeful fear, which is the essence of a sound believer’s heart.
31 | If you avoid the major sins forbidden to you, We will absolve you of your ˹lesser˺ misdeeds and admit you into a place of honour. | After the stern warning of the previous verse, this verse opens a wide door of hope and relief. “If you avoid the major sins forbidden to you” establishes a clear condition. Major sins, known as kabāʾir, are those grave transgressions that carry specific warnings of punishment in the Qur’an and Sunnah, such as murder, usury, consuming orphans’ wealth, slandering chaste women, and associating partners with Allah. The verse promises that for those who keep away from these enormous offenses, Allah “will absolve you of your lesser misdeeds.” The minor sins (ṣaghāʾir), those everyday slips and errors that no human being can completely avoid, will be forgiven and wiped away as a consequence of avoiding the major ones. This does not encourage complacency toward minor sins; rather, it teaches that the primary focus should be on the great boundaries, and if those are respected, the smaller stains will be cleansed by Allah’s grace. The reward goes beyond mere forgiveness: “and admit you into a place of honour.” The Arabic mudkhalan karīman evokes an entry that is noble, dignified, and generous. Paradise is not merely a place of physical pleasure; it is a realm where the believer is received with honour, without humiliation or reproach. This verse demonstrates the beautiful balance of Islamic ethics: it does not demand absolute sinlessness, which is humanly impossible, but it directs the believer’s struggle toward the major fronts of the battle against evil, entrusting the rest to divine mercy. It inspires a practical, hopeful piety that motivates continuous self-improvement without leading to despair over inevitable human frailty.
32 | And do not crave what Allah has given some of you over others. Men will be rewarded according to their deeds and women ˹equally˺ according to theirs. Rather, ask Allah for His bounties. Surely Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things. | This verse addresses one of the most destructive diseases of the heart: envy and discontent with Allah’s distribution of gifts. “And do not crave what Allah has given some of you over others.” People naturally differ in wealth, beauty, intelligence, social status, and talents. These differences are not random; they are part of Allah’s deliberate design and test. Craving what another has been given leads to bitterness, ingratitude, and a perpetual sense of deprivation. The verse then specifically addresses a common social tension: “Men will be rewarded according to their deeds and women equally according to theirs.” In a context where inheritance and financial responsibilities were being discussed, some might wonder why men received larger shares. The verse reminds them that every person, male or female, will be rewarded based on their own deeds, not on the size of their worldly inheritance. The differing shares in this world do not indicate favouritism in the Hereafter. Spiritual merit is entirely independent of material allocation. Having discouraged envy, the verse directs the heart to a more constructive pursuit: “Rather, ask Allah for His bounties.” Instead of looking sideways at what others possess, look upward to the source of all gifts and ask Him directly. Duʿāʾ, humble supplication, is the believer’s alternative to envious craving. The verse closes with “Surely Allah has perfect knowledge of all things,” affirming that He knows exactly what each person needs, what will test them best, and what will benefit them most. The believer trusts that the distribution, even when it seems unequal, is rooted in wisdom that surpasses human understanding. This teaching cultivates contentment, personal accountability, and an active reliance on Allah rather than a passive resentment toward others.
33 | And We have appointed heirs to what has been left by parents and next of kin. As for those you have made a pledge to, give them their share. Surely Allah is a Witness over all things. | After the lengthy inheritance regulations, the verse reaffirms that inheritance shares are a divinely appointed system. “And We have appointed heirs to what has been left by parents and next of kin.” The term “appointed” (jaʿalnā) indicates that these shares are not human inventions but direct designations from Allah. The verse then mentions a specific category: “As for those you have made a pledge to, give them their share.” In the early period of Islam, before the detailed inheritance verses were revealed, the Muhājirūn (emigrants from Mecca) and the Anṣār (helpers in Medina) had established bonds of brotherhood, and they would inherit from one another. This verse confirms that any such valid pledges made previously should be honoured, and those individuals should be given their agreed-upon share. According to many commentators, this ruling was later narrowed or abrogated by the fixed inheritance shares, but the principle of fulfilling a solemn pledge remains. The verse ends with a powerful reminder: “Surely Allah is a Witness over all things.” Every division of property, every fulfilment or neglect of a pledge, every attempt to cheat or to be just—all of it occurs under the watchful eye of Allah. He witnesses the intentions, the hidden resentments, and the secret dealings that accompany the distribution of a deceased person’s estate. This divine witness serves as the ultimate deterrent against injustice and the ultimate comfort for those who are wronged, because they know that the true Judge is watching and will settle all accounts. The verse thus closes the inheritance section with a reminder of the overarching accountability that gives the entire legal framework its moral weight.
34 | Men are the caretakers of women, as men have been provisioned by Allah over women and tasked with supporting them financially. And righteous women are devoutly obedient and, when alone, protective of what Allah has entrusted them with. And if you sense ill-conduct from your women, advise them ˹first˺, ˹if they persist,˺ do not share their beds, ˹but if they still persist,˺ then discipline them ˹gently˺. But if they change their ways, do not be unjust to them. Surely Allah is Most High, All-Great. | This is one of the most discussed verses in the surah, and it requires careful, contextual explanation. “Men are the caretakers of women” translates qawwāmūn, which means protectors, maintainers, and managers of affairs. This role is based on two reasons: “as men have been provisioned by Allah over women and tasked with supporting them financially.” The qawwāmah is not a grant of inherent superiority but a functional responsibility tied to the financial obligation that men bear. In the Islamic economic system, a woman’s wealth is her own, while a man must spend on his wife and family. This responsibility gives him the corresponding duty of leadership within the household, but it is a leadership of service, not tyranny. The verse then describes the qualities of righteous women: “righteous women are devoutly obedient and, when alone, protective of what Allah has entrusted them with.” Their obedience is first and foremost to Allah; their protection extends to their chastity, their husband’s honour, and his property in his absence. The verse then addresses a situation of marital discord: “And if you sense ill-conduct from your women.” The Arabic nushūz refers to wifely disobedience, disrespect, or moral laxity that threatens the marriage. The response is to follow a graduated sequence of steps: “advise them first,” meaning a private, gentle, and wise counsel. If the behaviour persists, “do not share their beds,” a symbolic separation within the home that communicates the seriousness of the issue without violence. Only if that too fails, “then discipline them gently.” The Arabic waḍribūhunna has been intensely debated, but the context—especially the Prophet’s explicit instructions—restricts it to a light, non-injurious tap that leaves no mark and is not on the face, used only as a last resort and as a means of psychological awakening, not as an outlet for anger. The Prophet never struck any of his wives, and he said the best of men are those who are best to their wives. The verse then immediately restricts any punitive action: “But if they change their ways, do not be unjust to them.” Any continued hostility after the wife has reformed is forbidden. The verse closes with “Surely Allah is Most High, All-Great,” reminding husbands that their authority is not absolute; above them is the Most High, who will hold them accountable for any injustice. The overall framework is one of restraint, graduated response, and a clear preference for reconciliation and gentleness.
35 | If you anticipate a split between them, appoint a mediator from his family and another from hers. If they desire reconciliation, Allah will restore harmony between them. Surely Allah is All-Knowing, All-Aware. | After addressing the husband’s steps in cases of nushūz, the verse now addresses the community’s role when a marriage reaches a critical breaking point. “If you anticipate a split between them” refers to a situation where the conflict between the spouses has escalated beyond the ability of the couple to resolve it themselves. The family and the broader community are not to be passive bystanders; they are to intervene constructively. The instruction is to “appoint a mediator from his family and another from hers.” These two arbitrators should be trustworthy individuals who understand the situation and have the welfare of the couple at heart. The mediator from the husband’s side can speak frankly to him, and the mediator from the wife’s side can do the same for her. Together, they form a reconciliation council that can hear both sides, separate fact from accusation, and propose solutions that preserve the marriage if possible, or arrange an amicable separation if necessary. The verse then adds a condition that points to the ultimate source of success: “If they desire reconciliation, Allah will restore harmony between them.” The arbitrators must genuinely seek to bring the couple back together, not to score points for their respective families or to deepen the divide. When the intention is sincere, Allah’s help descends, and He creates harmony (tawfīq) in unexpected ways. Hearts that were hardened can soften, and grievances that seemed insurmountable can dissolve. The verse closes with “Surely Allah is All-Knowing, All-Aware.” He knows the hidden resentments, the unspoken fears, and the genuine desires of each heart. Even when mediators are in the dark, Allah is fully aware, and His guidance can steer the process toward justice and mercy. This verse establishes marriage not as a purely private matter but as a concern of the community, to be sustained and healed with wisdom and divine reliance.
36 | Worship Allah ˹alone˺ and associate none with Him. And be kind to parents, relatives, orphans, the poor, near and distant neighbours, close friends, ˹needy˺ travellers, and those ˹bondspeople˺ in your possession. Surely Allah does not like whoever is arrogant, boastful— | With this verse, the surah shifts its lens from specific marital laws to the broad canvas of social and spiritual obligations. It begins with the foundational commandment: “Worship Allah alone and associate none with Him.” Pure monotheism is the root from which all ethical actions grow. Without tawḥīd, good deeds may lack the correct intention and their ultimate reward. From that vertical relationship, the verse immediately descends to the horizontal: “And be kind to parents.” The placement of kindness to parents directly after the worship of Allah underscores its immense importance. No other human relationship is given such proximity to the right of the Creator. The verse then expands the circle of kindness outward: “relatives, orphans, the poor.” These are the vulnerable ones within the extended family and community, whose care is repeatedly emphasised in this surah. Then come two categories that broaden the scope of social responsibility: “near and distant neighbours.” The near neighbour is the one whose house adjoins yours; the distant neighbour is the one further away, whether physically or in terms of relationship. The Prophet, peace be upon him, stressed the rights of the neighbour so emphatically that the companions thought they might be given a share of inheritance. Next are “close friends” (al-ṣāḥib bi al-janb), the companion at one’s side, whether in travel, work, or daily life. Then “needy travellers” (ibn al-sabīl), the wayfarer stranded far from home. Finally, “those bondspeople in your possession,” the slaves and servants whose humane treatment is a religious duty. The verse ends with a warning against the internal attitude that destroys all these kindnesses: “Surely Allah does not like whoever is arrogant, boastful.” Arrogance makes a person feel superior to others and unwilling to extend the kindness that this verse commands. Boastfulness pollutes any good deed with self-display. This verse thus provides a complete map of a compassionate society, held together by devotion to Allah and humble service to all layers of human connection.
37 | those who are stingy, promote stinginess among people, and withhold Allah’s bounties. We have prepared for the disbelievers a humiliating punishment. | This verse continues the description of those whom Allah does not love, linking closely to the previous mention of the arrogant and boastful. “Those who are stingy” refers to people who hoard their wealth and refuse to spend it in the ways that Allah has commanded, such as supporting relatives, neighbours, and the poor as mentioned in the previous verse.
