Their stinginess is not a passive trait; it is active and contagious: “promote stinginess among people.” They not only withhold themselves but also encourage others to be miserly, using arguments like “charity will make you poor” or “you have worked hard, no one else deserves it.” This spreading of a stingy mindset poisons the community’s spirit of generosity. The verse then adds: “and withhold Allah’s bounties.” This is the core of their crime. The wealth they possess is not truly theirs; it is a bounty from Allah, entrusted to them for a purpose. Withholding it is therefore a form of denying Allah’s right and failing the test of gratitude. The punishment is severe: “We have prepared for the disbelievers a humiliating punishment.” The use of “disbelievers” here is significant. Such deep-seated stinginess, combined with promoting it and arrogantly withholding God’s gifts, is not merely a moral failing but a sign of disbelief in the Hereafter and in the accountability before Allah. The punishment is described as “humiliating” because the miser who thought his wealth gave him honour and security will find that it has led him to the deepest disgrace. This verse, together with the next, paints a portrait of a character type that is utterly opposed to the spirit of the surah’s earlier commands about caring for orphans, giving dowries, and sharing inheritance with kindness.
38 | Likewise for those who spend their wealth to show off and do not believe in Allah or the Last Day. And whoever takes Satan as an associate —what an evil associate they have! | The previous verse condemned stinginess; this verse condemns its opposite when done for the wrong reason. “Likewise for those who spend their wealth to show off.” The term “show off” (riʾāʾ) refers to spending not for Allah’s pleasure but to be seen and praised by people. Outwardly, the generous person and the show-off may appear identical; both give money away. But the inner intention makes one act a path to Paradise and the other a path to destruction. The verse then links this hypocrisy directly to a deficiency in faith: “and do not believe in Allah or the Last Day.” If a person truly believed in Allah, they would seek His approval alone; if they truly believed in the Last Day, they would await the reward from Allah rather than the fleeting applause of people. The show-off essentially trades eternal reward for a momentary reputation, which is a catastrophic bargain. The verse then delivers a chilling statement: “And whoever takes Satan as an associate —what an evil associate they have!” The person who spends to show off has, in effect, chosen Satan as a close companion. Satan whispered the temptation to make the deed about human recognition rather than divine acceptance, and the person complied. The “associate” (qarīn) is a constant companion, whispering and influencing. The rhetorical question “what an evil associate” highlights the tragedy: the human being could have had Allah as their patron and guide, but instead they have shackled themselves to a companion whose only goal is to lead them to ruin. This verse serves as a profound warning to examine the intentions behind all charitable acts, ensuring that the praise of people is never the hidden motivation.
39 | What harm could have come to them if they had believed in Allah and the Last Day and donated from what Allah has provided for them? And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of them. | The surah now poses a rhetorical question that invites reflection on the irrationality of the behaviours just condemned. “What harm could have come to them if they had believed in Allah and the Last Day and donated from what Allah has provided for them?” This question dismantles any possible excuse for stinginess, show-off spending, or disbelief. Would belief in Allah have harmed their reputation? Would faith in the Last Day have diminished their wealth? Would giving from their provisions have impoverished them? The obvious answer is no. On the contrary, belief would have brought peace, and charity would have increased their blessings. The verse exposes the groundlessness of the fears that drive miserliness and hypocrisy. People hoard wealth because they fear poverty, yet the Lord who provided the wealth in the first place can certainly replenish it and add to it. People show off because they crave human approval, yet human approval is fickle and fleeting while divine approval is eternal. The verse then concludes: “And Allah has perfect knowledge of them.” He knows their inner calculations, their secret fears, their hidden stinginess, and their ostentation. This knowledge is a warning to the hypocrite and a comfort to the sincere believer. The hypocrite should tremble, because no act of show can be hidden from Allah. The sincere believer should feel secure, because Allah knows the genuine intention behind every quietly given charity. The verse thus gently invites the reader to reconsider their priorities and to recognise that faith and generosity can never harm them; it is the lack of them that leads to loss.
40 | Indeed, Allah never wrongs ˹anyone˺—even by an atom’s weight. And if it is a good deed, He will multiply it many times over and will give a great reward out of His grace. | This verse stands as one of the most hope-filled declarations in the Qur’an, establishing the absolute justice and immense generosity of Allah. “Indeed, Allah never wrongs anyone—even by an atom’s weight.” The Arabic mithqāl dharratin refers to the weight of a tiny ant or a speck of dust, the smallest imaginable thing. Allah’s justice is so precise that no soul will have its good deeds diminished by even that minuscule amount, and no soul will be punished for a sin it did not commit. This guarantee of perfect justice is the foundation upon which the believer’s peace of mind rests. But the verse does not stop at justice; it moves to extraordinary grace. “And if it is a good deed, He will multiply it many times over.” The smallest act of kindness, charity, or piety is not merely recorded as one unit; it is multiplied, in some cases by ten, in others by seven hundred, and in some by an uncountable multiple, depending on the sincerity and the circumstances. The verse then adds: “and will give a great reward out of His grace.” This “great reward” (ajran ʿaẓīman) is something beyond the multiplication of the deed itself; it is an additional, unbounded gift from Allah’s own bounty, unearned and purely from His generosity. The theological message is profound: entry into Paradise is not by a strict accounting of deeds alone, but by Allah’s mercy and grace, which far exceed what any human could merit. This verse directly counters any notion that Allah is harsh or ungenerous. It encourages the believer to never belittle any good deed, knowing that its ultimate value in Allah’s sight may be immeasurably greater than it appears. The verse concludes the section on financial and social ethics with a surge of optimism, lifting the heart from the fear of punishment to the hope of reward, and from the anxiety of accounting to the trust in a Lord whose mercy envelops all things.
41 | So how will it be when We bring a witness from every faith-community and bring you ˹O Prophet˺ as a witness against yours? | The surah now pivots to the scene of the Day of Judgment, a powerful lens through which all the preceding commands about justice, inheritance, and integrity are to be viewed. The opening phrase, “So how will it be,” is a rhetorical question intended to stir profound reflection and a sense of awe. It is not asking for a literal description but exclaiming about the enormity of that moment. The verse then describes the event: “when We bring a witness from every faith-community.” The consensus of commentators is that this witness is the prophet sent to that particular nation. Every messenger will be brought forward to testify about his people, either in their favour if they believed and obeyed, or against them if they rejected and disobeyed. This truth is echoed in Sūrat al-Naḥl, where Allah declares that on the Day of Resurrection, He will raise up a witness from every community. The second part of the verse is intensely personal for the early Muslim community and for all who claim to follow the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him: “and bring you O Prophet as a witness against yours.” The Prophet will be called to testify about his own ummah. He will bear witness that he delivered the message fully, and he will confirm who followed it and who turned away. The idea that the merciful Prophet, who was so eager for the guidance of his people, will testify against those who rejected him adds a sobering gravity to every moral and legal instruction revealed in this surah. It reminds every believer that the Prophet’s teaching is not merely advice but a trust that will be accounted for. The verse implicitly asks each reader: on that Day, what will your Prophet witness concerning you? Will he witness your faithful adherence to the trusts and limits set by Allah, or will your actions provide testimony against you? This forward-looking perspective aims to instil a constant sense of accountability, transforming the abstract commands of the law into a living relationship with the one who brought it.
42 | On that Day, those who denied ˹Allah˺ and disobeyed the Messenger will wish they were reduced to dust. And they will never be able to hide anything from Allah. | Building directly on the previous verse’s image of the Prophet as a witness, this verse describes the psychological state of those who failed the test. “On that Day” refers to the same Day of Judgment when the witnesses are brought forward. The subjects are those who committed two grievous wrongs: they “denied Allah” and they “disobeyed the Messenger.” The pairing indicates that disobedience to the Messenger is essentially linked to a denial of Allah, because the Messenger speaks by divine authority. Their reaction is one of unimaginable despair: they “will wish they were reduced to dust.” This is a profoundly evocative image. Humans were originally created from dust, and throughout life they tend to forget their humble origin. Yet on that Day, when the reality of their deeds crashes upon them, they will long to return to that state of nothingness, to be scattered dust, erased from existence, rather than face the consequences of their denial and disobedience. This wish for non-existence is the ultimate expression of regret and terror. The verse then adds a final, crushing clarification: “And they will never be able to hide anything from Allah.” In this world, people can conceal their sins, disguise their hypocrisy, or hide their rejection behind clever words. But on that Day, all veils are torn away. Not a single deed, word, or intention will remain hidden. This directly connects to earlier verses about the Watchful Lord and the final Reckoner, and it serves as a powerful deterrent against the secret sins and hidden plots of the hypocrites and disbelievers who are a recurring theme throughout the surah. The believer who internalises this verse will live with an acute awareness that every private action is fully known and will one day be laid bare before the Lord, the Prophet, and all of creation.
43 | O believers! Do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you are aware of what you say, nor in a state of ˹full˺ impurity—unless you merely pass through ˹the mosque˺—until you have bathed. But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth, wiping your faces and hands. And Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving. | This verse addresses the prerequisites of prayer and establishes the rulings on purity in a graduated, merciful manner. It begins with a direct address: “O believers! Do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you are aware of what you say.” This was an early step in the prohibition of intoxicants. Before the complete ban was revealed, the believers were first instructed not to pray while drunk, because prayer requires conscious, reverent communication with Allah. If a person does not know what they are uttering, their prayer is invalid and disrespectful. The restriction of drinking to non-prayer times was a practical step that prepared hearts for the final, absolute prohibition. The verse then addresses another state that bars one from prayer: “nor in a state of full impurity—unless you merely pass through the mosque—until you have bathed.” Full impurity, or janābah, refers to the state following sexual intimacy or a nocturnal emission. A person in this state must not pray or remain in the mosque, but may pass through if necessary, until they perform the full ritual bath (ghusl). The verse then introduces the concession of dry purification (tayammum): “But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth, wiping your faces and hands.” The situations mentioned are those where using water is either harmful (illness) or inaccessible (travel, lack of water). The permission to use clean earth, striking it with the hands and wiping the face and hands, is a hallmark of Islam’s practicality and ease. The narration from ʿĀʾishah about a necklace being lost on a journey and the subsequent revelation of this verse illustrates how divine law responds with mercy to human predicaments. The verse closes with two divine names: “Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving.” This softens any potential feeling of strictness and reminds believers that Allah’s law is not designed to entrap them but to provide them with merciful alternatives, always opening the door to forgiveness for any shortcomings.
44 | Have you ˹O Prophet˺ not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures yet trade it for misguidance and wish to see you deviate from the ˹Right˺ Path? | This verse draws the Prophet’s attention, and through him the believers’ attention, to a tragic spiritual condition. “Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures” refers to certain Jewish scholars and leaders of Medina who possessed knowledge of the Torah and the prophecies regarding a final messenger. They had a “portion” of divine revelation, which should have been sufficient to guide them to the truth. Instead of using that knowledge to recognise and follow the Prophet, they “trade it for misguidance.” The verb “trade” (yashtarūna) is economic; they bartered away the priceless treasure of divine guidance for the cheap price of worldly gain, social prestige, or racial pride. They chose error over truth in a conscious transaction. The verse then reveals the depth of their corruption: they “wish to see you deviate from the Right Path.” Their own deviation is not enough for them; they actively hope and plot to drag the believers into error with them. This exposes a psychological reality: those who have knowingly rejected the truth often cannot tolerate seeing others steadfast upon it. The followers of truth become a living reproach to them, so they try to create doubt, spread confusion, and cause deviation. The verse is a warning to the Prophet and the believers to be aware of this hidden malice. It also serves as a timeless caution against those who, despite possessing some scriptural knowledge, allow arrogance or worldly attachment to turn them into enemies of the guidance they claim to honour. The verse fits perfectly into the surah’s larger narrative of confronting hypocrisy and the scheming of the People of the Book against the Muslim community.
45 | Allah knows best who your enemies are! And Allah is sufficient as a Guardian, and He is sufficient as a Helper. | In the face of hidden animosity described in the previous verse, Allah provides profound reassurance to the Prophet and the believers. “Allah knows best who your enemies are!” Human perception can be deceived. Enemies may wear the mask of friendship, speak pleasant words, or outwardly profess faith while harbouring destructive intentions. The believers might not fully grasp the depth of the hostility arrayed against them, nor accurately identify every source of danger. But Allah’s knowledge is complete and unfailing. He knows the hypocrites who sit among the believers, the plotting neighbours, and the hostile tribes. There is immense comfort in this; the believers do not need to become paranoid or rely solely on their own intelligence-gathering. Their Lord knows every hidden chest and every secret meeting. The verse then adds two declarations that form a powerful shield: “And Allah is sufficient as a Guardian, and He is sufficient as a Helper.” The word “Guardian” (walī) implies a protecting ally, a patron who manages one’s affairs. “Helper” (naṣīr) is one who grants victory and support against enemies. If Allah is the Guardian, no enemy can ultimately harm the believers without His permission. If Allah is the Helper, no force can defeat them. This verse calls the believers to place their complete trust in Allah, not in their own strength or in the numbers of their allies. It teaches a balanced mindset: one must be aware of enmity and exercise prudence, but the heart must rest in the knowledge that Allah’s guardianship is absolute and His help is ever near. This spiritual anchoring was crucial for the early Muslim community surrounded by powerful foes, and it remains equally vital for believers navigating a world of hidden agendas and open hostility.
46 | Some Jews take words out of context and say, “We listen and we disobey,” “Hear! May you never hear,” and “Râ’ina!” [Herd us!]—playing with words and discrediting the faith. Had they said ˹courteously˺, “We hear and obey,” “Listen to us,” and “Unẓurna,” [Tend to us!] it would have been better for them and more proper. Allah has condemned them for their disbelief, so they do not believe except for a few. | This verse exposes a specific form of verbal mockery employed by some Jews against the Prophet. It first states that they “take words out of context,” distorting the meaning of their own scriptures and twisting the words addressed to the Prophet. They would say, “We listen and we disobey,” instead of the proper “We hear and obey.” This was an open declaration of defiance disguised as a response. They would also say, “Hear! May you never hear,” an insult wrapped in a superficially attentive phrase. The third example, “Râ’inā,” was particularly insidious. The believers had been taught to say “Unẓurnā,” meaning “Tend to us” or “Wait for us, so we may understand.” The Jews twisted this to “Râ’inā,” which in their usage carried a derogatory connotation derived from a Hebrew word for evil or foolishness, or from the Arabic root meaning “herd us,” implying the Prophet was a shepherd of fools. They were “playing with words and discrediting the faith,” deriving malicious pleasure from this verbal trickery. The verse then contrasts this with the beautiful alternative: “Had they said courteously, ‘We hear and obey,’ ‘Listen to us,’ and ‘Unẓurnā,’ it would have been better for them and more proper.” Politeness, sincerity, and straightforward speech would have brought them good and earned them divine pleasure. Instead, their mockery revealed the disease in their hearts. The verse concludes, “Allah has condemned them for their disbelief, so they do not believe except for a few.” The root cause is identified not as mere linguistic mischief but as deep-seated disbelief (kufr). Because of this, their hearts were sealed, and only a small number among them were sincere enough to eventually embrace faith, such as ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām. The verse teaches the paramount importance of respectful speech, especially concerning matters of religion, and the danger of allowing clever mockery to harden into rejection.
47 | O you who were given the Book! Believe in what We have revealed— confirming your own Scriptures—before We wipe out ˹your˺ faces, turning them backwards, or We condemn the defiant as We did to the Sabbath-breakers. And Allah’s command is always executed! | The surah now issues a direct, urgent call to the People of the Book. “O you who were given the Book!” addresses the Jews and Christians who have received prior revelation. They are commanded to “believe in what We have revealed,” meaning the Qur’an, which is described as “confirming your own Scriptures.” This confirmation is a powerful argument: the Qur’an does not ask them to abandon their sacred heritage; it asks them to recognise the truth that is consistent with what is already in their books, and to accept the final, uncorrupted revelation that authenticates the core message of Moses and Jesus. The verse then follows with a stern warning: “before We wipe out your faces, turning them backwards.” The image is terrifying. Faces, the seat of identity and honour, will be obliterated and literally turned to the back of the head, a punishment that signifies total humiliation and the reversal of their moral direction. This fate can be understood as occurring in this world before death, or as a description of their state on the Day of Judgment; both interpretations carry profound weight. The alternative is “or We condemn the defiant as We did to the Sabbath-breakers.” This refers to the community among the Children of Israel who violated the Sabbath and were transformed into apes, as mentioned in Sūrat al-Baqarah. The reference reminds them that divine punishment for open defiance is not merely a theoretical future event; it has historical precedents. The verse closes with the decisive statement, “And Allah’s command is always executed!” His will is not a possibility; it is an inevitability. This verse is a final, urgent plea mixed with a dire warning, reflecting the surah’s broader effort to invite the People of the Book to the truth before the door of opportunity closes and punishment becomes certain.
48 | Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating others with Him ˹in worship˺, but forgives anything else of whoever He wills. And whoever associates others with Allah has indeed committed a grave sin. | This verse is one of the most theologically decisive statements in the entire Qur’an. It first declares a boundary that divine mercy will not cross for those who die unrepentant: “Indeed, Allah does not forgive associating others with Him in worship.” Shirk, the act of setting up partners, rivals, or intermediaries in worship with Allah, is the ultimate crime because it violates the very foundation of existence: the absolute oneness and uniqueness of the Creator. It is an injustice (ẓulm) of cosmic proportions, as it directs the rights of the Creator to created things. However, the verse immediately opens a vast horizon of hope: “but forgives anything else of whoever He wills.” Every other sin—lying, theft, adultery, even murder—falls within the potential scope of divine forgiveness, conditioned upon Allah’s will. This does not mean that these sins are light or that one can be complacent about them. Rather, it means that no other sin is inherently unforgivable. The door of repentance and mercy remains open until death, and Allah, in His absolute sovereignty, may forgive whom He wills, either through sincere repentance or purely out of His grace. The verse closes by emphasising the magnitude of shirk: “And whoever associates others with Allah has indeed committed a grave sin.” It is a tremendous falsehood, a fabrication of the highest order, because it ascribes to creatures what belongs solely to the Almighty. This teaching serves both as a dire warning against all forms of shirk, whether blatant idol-worship or subtler forms like showing off, and as a source of immense comfort to the sinner who has not fallen into shirk, assuring them that Allah’s mercy is vast enough to encompass their failings if He so wills.
49 | Have you ˹O Prophet˺ not seen those who ˹falsely˺ elevate themselves? It is Allah who elevates whoever He wills. And none will be wronged ˹even by the width of˺ the thread of a date stone. | The verse turns to another flaw of those who claim scriptural authority: self-righteousness. “Have you not seen those who falsely elevate themselves?” This refers to the Jews and others who claimed they were the chosen people of God, the children of God, and the beloved of God, insisting that they were guaranteed Paradise regardless of their deeds. They engaged in self-praise, boasting of their lineage and their religious status while neglecting the substance of faith and obedience. The verse immediately cuts down this self-elevation: “It is Allah who elevates whoever He wills.” True honour, rank, and spiritual elevation are not achieved by human proclamation or by birthright. They are gifts bestowed by Allah according to His wisdom and based on genuine piety and righteousness. No amount of self-congratulation can lift a person in Allah’s sight. The verse then adds a guarantee of perfect justice: “And none will be wronged even by the width of the thread of a date stone.” The “thread of a date stone” (fatīl) is the tiny filament found in the cleft of a date pit, an image of the smallest conceivable thing. On the Day of Judgment, no soul will be dealt with unjustly, not even by that minuscule measure. The self-elevated will be brought low, and the humble will be raised. This verse is a powerful antidote to religious arrogance. It reminds believers that their standing with Allah is a matter of His grace and their own sincerity, not of group identity or ancestral claims. It teaches that true self-assessment lies in humility, self-examination, and the fear that one’s deeds may fall short, rather than in the loud declaration of one’s own righteousness.
50 | See how they fabricate lies against Allah—this alone is a blatant sin. | This short verse is a direct continuation and a forceful exclamation. “See how they fabricate lies against Allah” refers back to the claims of the self-elevated, including their assertions of being God’s chosen ones to the exclusion of others, their corruption of scripture, and their declarations that they would only touch the Fire for a few days. The verb “fabricate” (yaftarūna) implies deliberate invention, crafting a falsehood and attributing it to God. The verse then pronounces a severe verdict: “this alone is a blatant sin.” The word “alone” (kafā bihi) means that this single crime is sufficient to condemn them, without needing to list any other. Attributing falsehood to Allah is the peak of transgression because it misguides people about the nature of God, His mercy, His justice, and His commands. It creates a counterfeit religion that serves human desires and distorts the path of salvation. A “blatant sin” (ithman mubīnan) is one that is clear and manifest, leaving no room for ambiguity. The verse serves as a warning to all who speak about religion without knowledge, who issue rulings from their own whims, or who claim divine favour based on unsubstantiated assumptions. In a surah filled with precise legal rulings, this verse grounds all authority in truthful transmission from Allah, condemning any deviation from that truth as a monstrous offense. It reinforces the gravity of the preceding warnings and calls believers to anchor their faith solely in what Allah has revealed, not in inherited fabrications.
51 | Have you ˹O Prophet˺ not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures yet believe in idols and false gods and reassure the disbelievers that they are better guided than the believers? | This verse exposes a shocking spiritual alliance that unfolded in Medina. “Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures” again points to certain Jewish tribes or leaders. Despite possessing the Torah, with its uncompromising monotheism and its condemnation of idolatry, they “believe in idols and false gods.” This is not necessarily a belief in the theological truth of idols; rather, it refers to their practical endorsement and alliance with the idolatrous Quraysh. Some Jewish leaders went to Mecca, expressed sympathy with the pagan Arabs, and even participated in their idolatrous practices to cement a political and military alliance against the Muslims. The word “false gods” (al-jibt wa al-ṭāghūt) encompasses all objects of worship and systems of authority that rival Allah. Their most egregious act, however, was that they would “reassure the disbelievers that they are better guided than the believers.” They told the Quraysh that their pagan religion, rooted in ancestral custom, was superior to the monotheism of Muhammad, peace be upon him. This was a complete inversion of the truth. They used their scriptural knowledge, ironically, to prop up the very polytheism their own books condemned. The psychological and political motivation was clear: hatred and envy of the emerging Muslim community drove them to prefer the company and ideology of idolaters over the followers of the final prophet they were expecting. This verse is a timeless warning against the abandonment of principle for worldly alliance. It demonstrates that when a person’s heart is corrupted by envy, they can descend into supporting falsehood so blatantly that it contradicts their own professed sources of guidance.
52 | It is they who have been condemned by Allah. And whoever is condemned by Allah will have no helper. | Having described their shameful conduct in the previous verse, this verse delivers the divine verdict. “It is they who have been condemned by Allah.” The Arabic laʿnah, often translated as curse, means expulsion from divine mercy, a state of being deprived of Allah’s grace and favour. Those who allied with the idolaters and declared the pagans better guided than the believers have placed themselves under this divine condemnation. It is not a hasty human judgment but a decree from the All-Knowing. The verse then draws a universal, terrifying conclusion: “And whoever is condemned by Allah will have no helper.” On the Day of Judgment, or even in the trials of this world, a person whom Allah has cursed will find no ally, no intercessor, no protector who can shield them from the consequences of that curse. All the alliances they forged in this world—with other tribes, with powerful leaders, with their own wealth—will disintegrate into nothingness. This verse is a severe warning against the path described in verse 51. It underscores a key Qur’anic theme: the only true source of help and honour is Allah. When a person sells their faith for a temporary advantage, they might gain a fleeting ally, but they lose the only Ally that truly matters. The verse serves as a consolation to the believers who might have been dismayed by the alliance of scriptural people with pagans; their ultimate failure is sealed, and the believers remain under Allah’s guardianship, as stated earlier. The balance between warning and consolation is finely maintained.
53 | Do they have control over shares of the kingdom? If so, they would not have given anyone so much as the speck on a date stone. | This verse addresses the underlying arrogance of those who rejected the Prophet and envied the believers. It poses a rhetorical question: “Do they have control over shares of the kingdom?” The “kingdom” refers to Allah’s dominion over all creation, including the distribution of prophethood, wealth, power, and honour. The question is meant to expose the absurdity of their pretensions. Do they have any partnership in Allah’s sovereignty that would entitle them to decide who receives divine gifts? The obvious answer is no. The verse then delivers a sharp psychological insight into their character: “If so, they would not have given anyone so much as the speck on a date stone.” The “speck on a date stone” (naqīr) is the tiny dot on the back of a date pit, even smaller than the thread mentioned earlier. The verse declares that if these people truly had any share in the distribution of divine bounties, they would be so miserly and filled with stinginess that they would not give away even the most insignificant thing. This exposes their moral bankruptcy. They are not objecting to the Prophet’s mission out of a sincere concern for truth; their objection is rooted in jealousy, selfishness, and a desire to hoard spiritual privilege. Had they been in control, they would have denied all good to others. This verse simultaneously mocks their false claim to spiritual authority and reveals the ugly reality of their hearts. It teaches the believers that those who grudge them the mercy of Allah do so from a place of deep-seated envy, not from any legitimate right, and it reinforces the theme that all favour is in Allah’s hands alone.
54 | Or do they envy the people for Allah’s bounties? Indeed, We have given the descendants of Abraham the Book and wisdom, along with great authority. | The previous verse diagnosed stinginess; this verse names the disease directly. “Or do they envy the people for Allah’s bounties?” The “people” here, according to the overwhelming majority of commentators, refers to the Prophet Muhammad and his followers, the Muslim ummah. The Arabs, specifically the descendants of Ishmael, were now receiving the divine gifts of prophethood, scripture, and a unified community—gifts that had long been exclusive to the Children of Israel. This shift provoked intense envy (ḥasad) among some of the Jewish tribes. They could not bear that the final prophet emerged from a different branch of Abraham’s family. The verse responds by placing the matter in its proper historical and theological context: “Indeed, We have given the descendants of Abraham the Book and wisdom, along with great authority.” This is a reminder that the entire edifice of prophethood, scripture, and righteous rule had always been within the Abrahamic household. The gifts given to Moses, David, Solomon, and now Muhammad are all manifestations of this ancient divine promise. The “great authority” refers to the spiritual and temporal kingdom bestowed upon figures like David and Solomon, and by extension, the authority given to the Prophet Muhammad. The verse thus reframes the situation: the coming of the Prophet is not an anomaly to be envied but the fulfillment of a continuous divine plan. Envy is shown to be a rejection of God’s sovereign choice, a disease that eats away at faith and leads to the destructive behaviours described earlier. The verse calls upon the envious to recognize that Allah grants His favour as He wills, and the only appropriate response is humble acceptance, not bitter resentment.
55 | Yet some believed in him while others turned away from him. Hell is sufficient as a torment! | This verse traces the response to the long chain of divine favours mentioned in the previous verse. The pronoun “him” is interpreted by many to refer to Abraham or, more broadly, to the message and the messengers sent to humanity. Among the descendants of Abraham and those who received the Book, “some believed in him while others turned away from him.” This is the eternal pattern of revelation. Every prophet, from Abraham to Moses to Jesus, and finally to Muhammad, was met with a divided response. Some hearts opened in faith, while others closed in rejection. The verse then delivers a stark conclusion about the latter group: “Hell is sufficient as a torment!” The phrase “is sufficient” (kafā bi) implies that Hell, as a punishment, is more than enough. It needs no supplement; its horror is complete and overwhelming. For those who have knowingly turned away from the truth despite receiving clear signs and scriptures, Hell is the fitting and just abode. This verse serves multiple purposes within the surah. It connects the historical rejection of past prophets to the current rejection of Muhammad, comforting him by showing that this is a well-worn path. It also serves as a warning to the fence-sitters and the hypocrites within Medina: turning away from the truth after it has been clearly presented leads to a destination so terrible that no additional punishment could be imagined. The brief, emphatic statement leaves a heavy silence, compelling the listener to consider their own position: are they among those who believed, or among those who turned away?
56 | Surely those who reject Our signs, We will cast them into the Fire. Whenever their skin is burnt completely, We will replace it so they will ˹constantly˺ taste the punishment. Indeed, Allah is Almighty, All-Wise. | The surah now transitions into a series of verses contrasting the ultimate fates of the disbelievers and the believers. This verse describes the punishment of those “who reject Our signs.” Rejection of divine signs—whether the verses of the Qur’an or the signs in creation and conscience—is the core crime that earns this fate. The first statement, “We will cast them into the Fire,” is a direct, terrifying promise. The verse then provides a uniquely vivid detail about the nature of the punishment: “Whenever their skin is burnt completely, We will replace it so they will constantly taste the punishment.” The skin is the organ of sensation; when it burns, the nerve endings are destroyed, and the acute pain subsides. But divine justice and power decree that as soon as the skin is consumed, it is renewed, fresh and fully sensitive, so that the agony does not diminish but is perpetually experienced anew. This continuous renewal of the capacity to feel pain is an answer to the question of how an eternal punishment remains eternally severe. The verse then closes with two divine names that justify this reality: “Indeed, Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.” The Almighty has absolute power to execute such a punishment without any fatigue or limitation. The All-Wise ensures that this punishment is perfectly suited to the crime. Rejection of the signs was a persistent, stubborn state; the punishment mirrors it with its persistence. Far from being arbitrary cruelty, it is exact, measured justice from the One who knows the true weight of the sin. This verse serves as a fearsome deterrent, urging immediate repentance before the fire whose fuel is men and stones becomes an inescapable reality.
57 | As for those who believe and do good, We will admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever. There they will have pure spouses, and We will place them under a vast shade. | The severe warning of the previous verse is immediately followed by the soothing promise of reward, a Qur’anic pattern that balances fear with hope. “As for those who believe and do good” combines internal faith with external righteous action, the inseparable twins of salvation. Their reward is described with vivid, sensory imagery that resonated deeply with the Arabian audience. First, “We will admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever.” The phrase “under which rivers flow” evokes a picture of lush, shaded orchards with streams running beneath the trees, an image of perfect, effortless abundance. The permanence is doubly emphasised: “for ever and ever” (khālidīna fīhā abadan), leaving no room for any fear of the bliss ending. Then, an additional blessing is mentioned: “There they will have pure spouses.” The spouses (azwāj) are purified from all physical impurities such as menstruation or defecation, and from all moral flaws such as jealousy, spite, or deception. This represents the perfection of marital companionship, free from every cloud that dims relationships in this world. Finally, “We will place them under a vast shade.” In the scorching desert climate, shade is one of the greatest luxuries. The “vast shade” (ẓillan ẓalīlan) is an extended, deep, permanent shade that never moves or shrinks, a metaphor for complete comfort, protection, and peace. This entire description is not a comprehensive catalog of Paradise but a selection of images that appeal to the deepest human longings for security, beauty, companionship, and rest. It serves as a powerful motivation for the believers to remain steadfast in the face of the tests and seductions described throughout the surah.
58 | Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners; and when you judge between people, judge with fairness. What a noble commandment from Allah to you! Surely Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing. | After the contrasting destinies of the Hereafter, the surah returns to the foundational principles of a just society. This verse is considered one of the most comprehensive ethical commands in the Qur’an. It begins, “Indeed, Allah commands you to return trusts to their rightful owners.” The term “trusts” (amānāt) is vast in its scope. It includes material deposits and financial obligations, but it extends to responsibilities, secrets, positions of authority, and the rights that one person holds over another. The duty to return a trust means that every entrusted item, duty, or confidence must be given back to the one who is entitled to it, intact and in the proper manner. The specific historical occasion mentioned by some commentators involves the keys of the Kaʿbah, which the Prophet returned to ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥah, its hereditary custodian, despite having the power to keep them. This act demonstrated that even in victory, trusts are not violated. The second command is “and when you judge between people, judge with fairness.” This addresses all forms of arbitration, from a formal court ruling to a simple dispute between two friends or family members. Justice must be the unwavering standard, unaffected by wealth, status, friendship, or enmity. The verse then pauses to admire the command: “What a noble commandment from Allah to you!” The Arabic niʿimmā yaʿiẓukum bihi is an expression of high praise for this instruction; it is an excellent, beautiful exhortation. The verse closes by linking the command to two divine attributes: “Surely Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing.” Every word spoken in judgment, every silent betrayal of a trust, every hidden bias—all of it is fully perceived by Allah. This awareness transforms the fulfillment of trusts and the administration of justice from social duties into acts of worship, performed under the gaze of the Divine.
59 | O believers! Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. Should you disagree on anything, then refer it to Allah and His Messenger, if you ˹truly˺ believe in Allah and the Last Day. This is the best and fairest resolution. | This verse establishes the hierarchy of obedience and the mechanism for conflict resolution in the Muslim community. It begins with an address to the believers: “Obey Allah and obey the Messenger.” The repetition of the verb “obey” before “the Messenger” indicates that obedience to the Prophet holds its own weight; he must be obeyed because Allah commands it, and his instructions are binding. Then the verse adds, “and those in authority among you” (ulī al-amr). This refers to the rightful leaders, rulers, and scholars who govern the affairs of the community in accordance with Islamic law. Their obedience is conditional upon their own obedience to Allah and His Messenger; it is not absolute. The verse then anticipates a natural human reality: “Should you disagree on anything, then refer it to Allah and His Messenger.” Disagreements will arise between people, and even between the community and its leaders. The resolution is not to split, fight, or follow personal whims, but to return to the original sources: the Qur’an (“Allah”) and the Sunnah (“the Messenger”). This is the duty of those who “truly believe in Allah and the Last Day.” This clause links adherence to this method directly to the genuineness of one’s faith. A person who claims to believe but then insists on his own opinion over the clear texts of revelation is contradicting his claim. The verse concludes, “This is the best and fairest resolution.” The Arabic khayrun wa aḥsanu taʾwīlan means it is better in its immediate outcome and more beautiful in its ultimate consequence. Returning to divine guidance brings the most beneficial results and the most harmonious conclusions. This verse is the constitutional bedrock of Islamic governance and personal piety, ensuring that all authority is ultimately subordinated to the revealed will of Allah.
60 | Have you ˹O Prophet˺ not seen those who claim they believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you? They seek the judgment of false judges, which they were commanded to reject. And Satan ˹only˺ desires to lead them farther away. | This verse exposes a specific category of hypocrites whose actions betray their professed faith. “Have you not seen those who claim they believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you?” These are individuals who verbally affirm both the Qur’an and the earlier scriptures, presenting themselves as believers in all divine revelation. Yet their behaviour shatters this claim: “They seek the judgment of false judges, which they were commanded to reject.” The term “false judges” translates al-ṭāghūt, which encompasses any source of judgment, law, or arbitration that is based on principles contrary to divine revelation. Instead of bringing their disputes to the Prophet for resolution according to Allah’s law, they sought out pagan soothsayers or tribal chieftains who ruled by custom and whim. This was a direct violation of the command in the previous verse to refer disagreements to Allah and His Messenger. According to a well-known occasion of revelation, a hypocrite named Bishr had a dispute with a Jewish man. The Jewish man suggested they take the matter to the Prophet, knowing he would judge justly, but the hypocrite insisted on going to a pagan judge, Kaʿb ibn al-Ashraf. This incident revealed the hypocrisy of those who outwardly professed Islam but inwardly preferred the judgment of jāhiliyyah. The verse concludes with a diagnosis: “And Satan only desires to lead them farther away.” Their choice was not a small misstep; it was a step onto a path that Satan continuously uses to drag them further and further from the truth, until they are completely lost. The verse serves as a timeless criterion: true belief in revelation is demonstrated not by verbal claims but by the consistent desire to seek judgment from that revelation, not from systems that reject it.
61 | When it is said to them, “Come to Allah’s revelations and to the Messenger,” you see the hypocrites turn away from you stubbornly. | This verse paints a vivid picture of the hypocrites’ reaction when invited to the very source of guidance. “When it is said to them, ‘Come to Allah’s revelations and to the Messenger’” refers to the call to bring their disputes before the Qur’an and the Prophet for a just resolution. The invitation is clear, noble, and linked directly to salvation. Yet the response is not hesitation or polite refusal; it is a visible, physical turning away: “you see the hypocrites turn away from you stubbornly.” The Arabic word ṣudūd conveys not just turning aside but doing so with aversion and pride, while iʿrāḍ adds the sense of deliberate, persistent avoidance. They literally swivel away, refusing to face the Prophet or hear the words of Allah. This bodily reaction exposes the state of their hearts. If they truly believed that the Qur’an was Allah’s revelation, they would rush toward it; their turning away proves that their claim of faith is hollow. The verse also speaks to a deep psychological truth: those who are insincere in their faith find the presence of truth and the company of the righteous uncomfortable. Being near the Prophet meant being confronted with their own hypocrisy, so they fled. This scene must have been painful for the Prophet, who desired guidance for all, and for the sincere believers, who witnessed people they considered part of the community reject the very foundation of the faith. The verse serves as a lesson that mere verbal profession of Islam is not enough; the test comes when one is called to submit one’s personal disputes to divine judgment. The stubborn turning away is a diagnostic sign of a diseased heart.
62 | How ˹horrible˺ will it be if a disaster strikes them because of what their hands have done, then they come to you swearing by Allah, “We intended nothing but goodwill and reconciliation.” | Having described their stubborn turning away, the verse now imagines a future moment of crisis. “How horrible will it be if a disaster strikes them because of what their hands have done.” The “disaster” (muṣībah) refers to the natural consequence of their sins and schemes—perhaps exposure, punishment in this world, or defeat. The phrase “because of what their hands have done” ties the suffering directly to their own actions, which is a fundamental Qur’anic principle: Allah does not wrong people; they wrong themselves. The disaster shatters their facade. In desperation, “they come to you swearing by Allah, ‘We intended nothing but goodwill and reconciliation.’” Now that calamity has hit, they return to the Prophet, the one they earlier avoided, and they swear the most solemn oaths that their past behaviour was well-intentioned. They claim that their avoidance of his judgment was not out of defiance but out of a desire to promote peace and mend relations. This is the height of hypocrisy: to disguise treachery as diplomacy. The verse exposes the hollow excuses that hypocrites manufacture when their misdeeds catch up with them. It also highlights a recurring pattern: when they are safe, they mock and turn away; when trouble strikes, they run to the believers, invoking Allah’s name to cover their lies. The rhetorical question “How horrible will it be” (fa-kayfa) expresses the utter repulsiveness of this state. The verse prompts the believers to recognise that such oaths are worthless if not backed by consistent, sincere obedience. True goodwill and reconciliation would have been demonstrated by coming to the Messenger willingly from the start.
63 | ˹Only˺ Allah knows what is in their hearts. So turn away from them, caution them, and give them advice that will shake their very souls. | This verse provides the divine response to the hypocrites’ deceptive oaths. “Only Allah knows what is in their hearts.” The believers are not required to probe hidden intentions or judge the unseeable. Allah alone is fully aware of the hypocrisy, the secrets, and the lies that reside in the chests. This is a reassurance to the Prophet: you do not need to prove their insincerity; Allah knows. It also serves as a warning to the hypocrites that their inner state, no matter how well concealed from people, is an open book to the Almighty. The verse then gives the Prophet a threefold instruction on how to deal with them. “So turn away from them” means do not engage them in endless argument, do not grant them the intimacy of true friends, and do not let their schemes occupy your heart. This turning away is not one of neglect in delivering the message, but a refusal to be affected or manipulated by their games. “Caution them” means warn them of the severe consequences of their hypocrisy, both in this world and the Hereafter. “Give them advice that will shake their very souls” translates the Arabic qawlan balīghan, a word that penetrates deeply into the heart, an eloquent, powerful admonition that reaches the core of their being. The advice should be so clear and impactful that it leaves no room for excuses. The combination of these three commands—detachment, warning, and profound counsel—forms a comprehensive strategy for dealing with unrepentant hypocrites: protect your own soul from their toxicity, alert them to their danger, and deliver the truth with piercing clarity, leaving the ultimate judgment to Allah.
64 | We only sent messengers to be obeyed by Allah’s Will. If only those ˹hypocrites˺ came to you ˹O Prophet˺—after wronging themselves—seeking Allah’s forgiveness and the Messenger prayed for their forgiveness, they would have certainly found Allah ever Accepting of Repentance, Most Merciful. | This verse lays down a fundamental principle about the role of messengers, then opens a door of immense mercy. “We only sent messengers to be obeyed by Allah’s Will.” The whole purpose of sending prophets is not merely to deliver a theoretical message but to establish a practical framework of obedience. A messenger is not just an informant; he is a leader whose commands, by Allah’s permission, must be followed. This reinforces the preceding critique of the hypocrites who refused to refer their disputes to the Messenger. The verse then shifts to a deeply compassionate tone: “If only those hypocrites came to you O Prophet—after wronging themselves—seeking Allah’s forgiveness and the Messenger prayed for their forgiveness, they would have certainly found Allah ever Accepting of Repentance, Most Merciful.” The wording outlines a specific path of repentance that involves four steps: they must first acknowledge that they have wronged themselves; they must then physically come to the Prophet, a sign of genuine humility and reversal of their earlier avoidance; they must seek Allah’s forgiveness; and finally, the Messenger himself must pray for their forgiveness. If this sequence were fulfilled, the result would be certain: they would find Allah to be Tawwāb, the One who constantly turns back in mercy, and Raḥīm, the Most Merciful. According to authentic narrations, this verse was demonstrated in a profound incident: after the Prophet’s passing, Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, in their caliphates, would instruct those who came seeking forgiveness for serious errors to go to the Prophet’s grave, as if seeking the intercession implied in the verse, but this interpretation is debated. The core lesson, however, is that no matter how severe the hypocrisy or wrongdoing, the door of repentance is wide open if approached with sincerity and proper respect for the Messenger’s station.
65 | But no! By your Lord, they will never be ˹true˺ believers until they accept you ˹O Prophet˺ as the judge in their disputes, and find no resistance within themselves against your decision and submit wholeheartedly. | This verse issues one of the most emphatic oaths in the Qur’an, directly addressing the Prophet. “But no! By your Lord, they will never be true believers until they accept you O Prophet as the judge in their disputes.” The opening “But no!” (fa-lā) is a forceful negation of any claim to faith that is not accompanied by full acceptance of the Prophet’s judicial authority. Allah swears by His own Lordship over the Prophet, highlighting the seriousness of this condition. True faith, the verse declares, is not established merely by performing prayers or paying zakāt. It requires that in all disputes, large or small, the believer willingly turns to the Prophet as the ultimate arbiter. At the time of revelation, this meant direct recourse to the Prophet himself; for all later generations, it means recourse to his Sunnah. The verse then adds two further conditions. First, they must “find no resistance within themselves against your decision.” The word “resistance” (ḥaraj) implies any feeling of tightness, discomfort, or resentment. A person might outwardly accept a ruling but inwardly feel it is unfair, too strict, or against their interests. True faith demands that the heart be at peace with the divine judgment. Second, they must “submit wholeheartedly.” The Arabic taslīman emphasizes a complete, total, and peaceful surrender, without any reservation or reluctance. This verse stands as a permanent criterion for genuine faith. It teaches that Islam is not a buffet from which one picks and chooses; it is a comprehensive submission of the mind and heart to the judgment of the Messenger, rooted in the conviction that his judgment is ultimately Allah’s judgment.
66 | If We had commanded them to sacrifice themselves or abandon their homes, none would have obeyed except for a few. Had they done what they were advised to do, it would have certainly been far better for them and more reassuring, | After the previous verse’s absolute requirement of submission, this verse offers a realistic assessment of human weakness and contrasts it with what sincere commitment would produce. “If We had commanded them to sacrifice themselves or abandon their homes, none would have obeyed except for a few.” This is a hypothetical scenario. If Allah had imposed the extreme obligation of self-sacrifice (like the Israelites’ command to kill themselves in repentance) or mass emigration from their homes, only a very small number of sincere believers would have carried it out. The majority would have faltered. This statement serves as a rebuke to those whose obedience is weak even in lighter matters. It also acknowledges the mercy of Allah in not burdening the community with such crushing commands. The verse then reflects on what could have been: “Had they done what they were advised to do, it would have certainly been far better for them and more reassuring.” The phrase “what they were advised to do” refers to the sincere obedience and recourse to the Messenger’s judgment previously emphasised. If they had simply complied with the attainable commands they were given, the outcome would have been “far better” (khayran lahum) in terms of worldly and spiritual benefit, and “more reassuring” (wa ashadda tathbītan), meaning firmer in establishing their hearts upon the truth. Obedience breeds inner stability and conviction. The hypothetical demonstrates the principle that Allah’s commands are not arbitrary burdens but pathways to profound good. The verse thus encourages the believers to seize the commands they already have, recognising that their fulfilment leads to strength and certainty, while their neglect leads to spiritual weakness and turmoil.
67 | and We would have granted them a great reward by Our grace | This verse continues the chain of promised blessings that would have followed obedience. “And We would have granted them a great reward by Our grace.” The reward is described as “great” (ajran ʿaẓīman), signifying immeasurable, eternal good. The crucial phrase is “by Our grace” (min ladunnā). This indicates that the reward is not a transactional payment but a gift from the special treasuries of divine bounty. Ladunnā conveys a sense of closeness and exclusivity; it is a reward directly from Allah’s presence, not one mediated or calculable by human scales. The use of “grace” (faḍl) underscores that no human deed can ever truly earn the rewards of Paradise; they come through Allah’s immense generosity. This verse, though brief, serves a powerful function. It shows that what the hypocrites and weak believers forfeit by their disobedience is not a small thing. They lose the chance to receive this direct, magnificent gift from the Lord. The verse also encourages believers to have high hopes in Allah’s generosity. Obedience may sometimes feel burdensome in the moment, but the hidden reward awaiting it is beyond imagination. The connection between this verse and the previous ones is clear: the command to accept the Prophet’s judgment and to submit wholeheartedly is not a demand that diminishes people; it is an invitation to a path that culminates in a direct and glorious bestowal of divine grace.
68 | and guided them to the Straight Path. | The chain of blessings concludes with the most comprehensive of all gifts. “And guided them to the Straight Path.” The Straight Path (al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm) is the path of Islam, the clear way that leads to Allah’s pleasure and to Paradise. This verse states that sincere obedience to the commands, especially the central command to refer disputes to the Messenger and submit wholeheartedly, is itself the means to receive increased guidance. Guidance is not a static possession; it grows, deepens, and becomes firmer through acts of obedience. Allah promises that if they had done what they were advised, He would have guided them further along the path, increasing their knowledge, their certainty, and their ability to discern right from wrong. This is a profound principle: guidance begets guidance. Every act of compliance illuminates the heart and makes the next step of obedience easier and clearer. Conversely, turning away begets misguidance. The hypocrites, by refusing to come to the Prophet, were not merely rejecting a single ruling; they were closing off the channel through which Allah grants increased guidance. The Straight Path mentioned here is the same one that believers ask for in every rakʿah of prayer: “Guide us to the Straight Path.” The verse reveals that the answer to that prayer lies in practical submission to the Messenger. The more one submits, the more one is guided. This verse thus integrates beautifully into the surah’s theme: the social and legal commands are not peripheral to spirituality; they are the very mechanism by which a soul progresses along the path to its Lord.
69 | And whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger will be in the company of those blessed by Allah: the prophets, the people of truth, the martyrs, and the righteous—what honourable company! | After the conditional blessings of the previous verses, this verse opens the promise to all who meet the condition. “And whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger” establishes the universal criterion. This obedience is the practical expression of the submission demanded earlier. The reward is breath-taking in its beauty and motivation: they “will be in the company of those blessed by Allah.” The phrase “blessed by Allah” (anʿama Allāhu ʿalayhim) recalls the verse of Sūrat al-Fātiḥah that every Muslim recites daily: “the path of those upon whom You have bestowed favour.” The verse then specifies four categories of this exalted company. “The prophets” (al-nabiyyīn) are the highest rank of humanity, the chosen recipients of revelation. “The people of truth” (al-ṣiddīqīn) are those whose faith is so deep and actions so truthful that they are the foremost confirmers of the truth. “The martyrs” (al-shuhadāʾ) are those who gave their lives in the path of Allah, witnessing to the truth with their blood. “The righteous” (al-ṣāliḥīn) are those whose entire lives are lived in moral rectitude. The believer who obeys Allah and His Messenger is promised not just entry into Paradise but the specific, intimate companionship of these spiritual giants. The verse then exclaims, “what honourable company!” (wa ḥasuna ulāʾika rafīqan). The word rafīq means a close companion with whom one shares a journey. The company is honourable, noble, and delightful. This verse has been a source of immense hope for believers throughout the ages. It teaches that the goal of obedience is not merely to avoid Hell but to ascend to the highest circles of nearness to Allah, in the company of the most beloved of His creation. It also provides a powerful social incentive: one should choose one’s earthly companions wisely, because the ultimate aim is to be in the company of the prophets and the righteous.
70 | This is Allah’s favour, and Allah fully knows ˹who deserves it˺. | The verse acknowledges that the magnificent reward described in the previous verse is not a wage earned by sheer effort but a divine bounty. “This is Allah’s favour” (dhālika al-faḍlu min Allāh). The demonstrative pronoun “this” points to the promised companionship and reward. It is a faḍl, an overflowing grace, that Allah bestows upon the obedient not because their deeds are proportionally equal to the reward, but because He is infinitely generous. This prevents any sense of self-righteousness or spiritual arrogance in the believer. One enters Paradise and its noble company not by one’s own merit alone but by the encompassing mercy of Allah. The verse then adds a profound qualifier: “and Allah fully knows who deserves it.” The Arabic kafā bi-Allāhi ʿalīman means “sufficient is Allah as the All-Knowing.” He knows perfectly who is truly eligible for this favour. Outward obedience might be mimicked, and hidden intentions might remain concealed from human observers, but Allah’s knowledge penetrates every layer of the heart. He alone knows whose faith is genuine, whose submission is wholehearted, and whose deeds are pure. Thus, no unworthy person can accidentally slip into that noble company, and no sincere person will be unjustly excluded. This verse provides both comfort and caution. It comforts the humble believer who feels unworthy, reminding them that it is Allah’s favour, not their own self-assessment, that is the gate to Paradise. It cautions the ostentatious that their outward show will not fool the All-Knowing. The perfect knowledge of Allah is the ultimate guarantor of perfect justice and mercy in the distribution of eternal ranks.
71 | O believers! Take your precautions and go forth either in groups or together. | The surah now shifts its thematic focus to the practicalities of striving in the cause of Allah, particularly the military struggle that the early Muslim community in Medina was facing. The address opens with “O believers!” drawing a clear line between the faithful and the hypocrites whose behaviour was just dissected. The first command is “Take your precautions.” The Arabic ḥidhr means being fully alert, prepared, and armed against the enemy. It implies strategic thinking, intelligence gathering, and material readiness. The believers are not to be recklessly naive or fatalistic. Then comes the instruction on how to mobilise: “and go forth either in groups or together.” The phrase “in groups” (thubātin) refers to smaller, separate squadrons or detachments that move in a dispersed manner for tactical reasons. “Together” (jamīʿan) refers to marching as a unified, massed army when the situation demands it. This verse establishes the principle of flexible military strategy. The community is ordered to assess the threat and respond appropriately, using guerrilla-style detachments or a full-scale expedition as needed. The command also has a spiritual dimension: the believers are to go forth in the path of Allah, not hiding at home. The verse marks the beginning of a new phase in the surah where the focus moves from social and legal reform to the external struggle for the survival and propagation of the faith. It shows that the protection of the just society whose foundations were laid in the earlier verses requires the community to be prepared to defend itself physically. The juxtaposition is powerful: the same surah that safeguards orphans and women also calls the men to take up arms in defence of those very values.
72 | There will be some among you who will lag behind so that if you face a disaster, they will say, “Allah has blessed us for not being there among them.” | This verse exposes the mentality of the hypocrites and the weak-hearted when the call to arms is issued. “There will be some among you who will lag behind.” The verb “lag behind” (la-yubṭiʾanna) conveys a deliberate slowness, a dragging of feet, an intentional failure to join the expedition. These are not people with valid excuses; they are those who stay back out of cowardice, doubt, or disloyalty. Their true colours emerge when news returns: “so that if you face a disaster, they will say, ‘Allah has blessed us for not being there among them.’” If the Muslim force suffers a defeat or heavy casualties, they do not grieve or feel solidarity. Instead, they smugly attribute their survival to divine favour, interpreting their cowardice as a blessing. They see the martyrs and the wounded not as honoured in the sight of Allah but as fools who walked into disaster. This is a chilling inversion of values. The very thing that the believers consider a trial and a means to Paradise, the hypocrites view as a calamity to be avoided at all costs. Their statement, “Allah has blessed us,” is a blasphemous misuse of religious language to justify their treachery. This verse serves as a warning to the believers that among them will be those who view every setback of the community as a vindication of their own inaction. It prepares the sincere believers not to be surprised or demoralised by such attitudes. It also highlights the theme of hidden hearts; the battlefield becomes a revealer of true faith, separating those who trust in Allah and His Messenger from those who trust only in their own calculations of safety.
73 | But if you return with Allah’s bounties, they will say—as if there had been no bond between you—“We wish we had been there with them to share the great gain!” | This verse completes the portrait of the laggards by showing their reaction when the outcome is favourable. “But if you return with Allah’s bounties” refers to a victorious expedition where the believers return with spoils of war and honour. The response of those who stayed behind is entirely different from their earlier smugness. “They will say—as if there had been no bond between you—‘We wish we had been there with them to share the great gain!’” Now they express regret, not over their lack of faith or their failure to support the cause, but over missing out on the material spoils. The parenthetical phrase “as if there had been no bond between you” (ka-an lam takun baynakum mawaddah) is piercing. It means they speak as if there was never any affection, loyalty, or brotherhood between them and the believers. Their relationship is purely transactional. When the believers were in danger, they severed the bond to protect themselves; when the believers triumph, they wish to reconnect to claim a share of the wealth. This exposes the hypocrisy at its core: the faith of such people is attached to worldly gain, not to Allah. When the cause requires sacrifice, they are absent; when it yields profit, they want a cut. The verse teaches the believers to recognise this pattern and not to be emotionally manipulated by the belated expressions of regret from such people. It also serves as a timeless mirror for individuals to examine their own motivations for supporting religious causes: is it for the love of Allah and the brotherhood, or is it for the potential worldly returns?
74 | Let those who would sacrifice this life for the Hereafter fight in the cause of Allah. And whoever fights in Allah’s cause—whether they achieve martyrdom or victory—We will honour them with a great reward. | After exposing the hypocrites, the surah now issues a stirring call to the true believers. “Let those who would sacrifice this life for the Hereafter fight in the cause of Allah.” The description is precise: those who are fit for this fight are not simply the brave or the strong, but those who have made a fundamental transaction, selling the temporary pleasures and securities of the worldly life in exchange for the eternal bliss of the Hereafter. The verb “sacrifice” (yashrūna) is commercial: they are traders, but their trade is with Allah. This echoes the verse in Sūrat al-Tawbah where Allah has purchased the lives and wealth of the believers in return for Paradise. The command to fight is thus framed not as a grim duty but as the natural expression of a faith that values the next world over this one. The verse then provides an unmatched guarantee: “And whoever fights in Allah’s cause—whether they achieve martyrdom or victory—We will honour them with a great reward.” The fighter in Allah’s cause is in a win-win situation, a true no-lose proposition. If they are killed, they attain shahādah, a state of being a living witness in the presence of their Lord, receiving provision and joy. If they survive and are victorious, they gain the spoils and honour of this world along with the divine reward. Both outcomes are good because the intention is purely for Allah. “We will honour them” (sawfa nuʾtīhi ajran ʿaẓīman) uses the future emphatic, promising a reward that is great, certain, and directly from Allah. This verse has historically been a tremendous source of courage for Muslim armies, instilling the understanding that death in this path is not loss but the highest gain.
75 | And what is it with you? You do not fight in the cause of Allah and for oppressed men, women, and children who cry out, “Our Lord! Deliver us from this land of oppressors! Appoint for us a saviour; appoint for us a helper—all by Your grace.” | This verse delivers a powerful emotional and moral argument for fighting. “And what is it with you?” (wa mā lakum) is a rhetorical question expressing amazement and reproach. What excuse can the believers possibly have? They are being called to fight for two intertwined causes: “in the cause of Allah and for oppressed men, women, and children.” The cause of Allah is the highest abstraction, the establishment of His word and law. But it is immediately given concrete, human flesh in the form of the oppressed. The verse gives voice to the weak who cannot defend themselves. They are “men, women, and children” suffering under tyranny. The verse even quotes their desperate prayer: “Our Lord! Deliver us from this land of oppressors! Appoint for us a saviour; appoint for us a helper—all by Your grace.” This prayer encapsulates their complete helplessness and their sole reliance on Allah. The mention of “oppressed children” is particularly heart-wrenching, designed to stir the protective instincts of any honourable person. The believers are being told that their military struggle is not a war of aggression or tribal revenge; it is a rescue mission, a response to the cries of the downtrodden. This verse establishes a core principle of Islamic just war theory: fighting is legitimate not only for self-defence but also to rescue the oppressed, regardless of their identity. The faith of the oppressed is not specified, but their humanity and their cry to the Lord of all beings are sufficient to obligate the believers to act. The verse shames the able-bodied who remain sitting while children and elderly cry out under persecution.
76 | Believers fight for the cause of Allah, whereas disbelievers fight for the cause of the Devil. So fight against Satan’s ˹evil˺ forces. Indeed, Satan’s schemes are ever weak. | This verse clarifies the fundamental difference in motivation between the two sides in any conflict between faith and disbelief. “Believers fight for the cause of Allah.” Their purpose is to make Allah’s word supreme, to defend justice, and to protect the weak, all for the sake of divine pleasure. Their fight is principled and bounded by ethical rules. “Whereas disbelievers fight for the cause of the Devil” (ṭāghūt). The disbelievers, regardless of their stated political or economic reasons, are ultimately fighting for false gods, for tyranny, for personal ambition, for tribal pride, or for material gain—all of which fall under the umbrella of ṭāghūt, the forces of rebellion against Allah. This does not mean every disbelieving soldier is consciously fighting for Satan, but the system and ultimate objective of the disbelieving side serve Satan’s goal of spreading corruption, injustice, and the rejection of Allah. The verse then issues the direct command: “So fight against Satan’s evil forces.” The believers are to see the battle as a spiritual one as much as a physical one. Then a profound reassurance is given: “Indeed, Satan’s schemes are ever weak.” Satan’s plots, his alliances, his military strength—all of it is ultimately frail and destined to collapse when confronted with true faith and reliance on Allah. This is not a naive underestimation of enemy power; it is a theological truth that falsehood has no inherent, lasting strength. It can appear formidable for a while, but it is built on a foundation of deception and will crumble when the believers stand firm. This verse infuses the believers with confidence and frames their struggle within the cosmic battle between truth and falsehood.
77 | Have you ˹O Prophet˺ not seen those who had been told, “Do not fight! Rather, establish prayer and pay alms-tax.”? Then once the order came to fight, a group of them feared those ˹hostile˺ people as Allah should be feared—or even more. They said, “Our Lord! Why have You ordered us to fight? If only You had delayed ˹the order for˺ us for a little while!” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “The enjoyment of this world is so little, whereas the Hereafter is far better for those mindful ˹of Allah˺. And none of you will be wronged ˹even by the width of˺ the thread of a date stone. | The verse describes a dramatic shift in religious obligation and the reaction of those with weak faith. “Have you not seen those who had been told, ‘Do not fight! Rather, establish prayer and pay alms-tax.’?” In Mecca, the Muslims were severely persecuted, yet they were commanded to refrain from armed retaliation and to focus on spiritual purification through prayer and charity. This was a training period to build their faith and discipline. But in Medina, the command changed: “Then once the order came to fight, a group of them feared those hostile people as Allah should be feared—or even more.” When the divine legislation progressed to permit and then obligate fighting in self-defence, some of those who had been content with the peaceful rituals suddenly panicked. Their fear of human enemies equalled or exceeded the fear they should have had for Allah alone. This is a profound diagnostic: their previous religious practice was not rooted in unconditional submission to Allah’s command, but in the comfort of a command that required no risk. The moment the command demanded sacrifice, their true colours showed. Their complaint is quoted: “Our Lord! Why have You ordered us to fight? If only You had delayed the order for us for a little while!” They did not refuse outright but asked for postponement, hoping to enjoy life a bit longer. The Prophet is then instructed to reply: “Say, ‘The enjoyment of this world is so little, whereas the Hereafter is far better for those mindful of Allah.’” The fleeting pleasures they cling to are paltry compared to the eternal bliss awaiting the God-conscious. The verse closes with a guarantee of perfect justice: “And none of you will be wronged even by the width of the thread of a date stone.” No sacrifice, no delay in receiving worldly comforts, will go unrewarded. This verse teaches that stages of divine legislation are a test, and the believer must be ready to move from easier duties to harder ones when Allah wills.
78 | Wherever you may be, death will overcome you—even if you were in fortified towers.” When something good befalls them, they say, “This is from Allah,” but when something evil befalls them, they say, “This is from you.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Both have been destined by Allah.” So what is the matter with these people? They can hardly comprehend anything! | This verse dismantles the psychology of those who feared fighting by confronting them with the inevitability of death. “Wherever you may be, death will overcome you—even if you were in fortified towers.” The Arabic burūj mushayyadah evokes towering, lofty fortresses built with immense strength. The point is clear: no amount of fortification, no avoidance of the battlefield, can delay the appointed moment of death by a single second. Hiding from the fight does not extend life; it merely changes the location and circumstance of an event that is absolutely certain. The second half of the verse exposes a deep inconsistency in the worldview of the hypocrites and weak-hearted. “When something good befalls them, they say, ‘This is from Allah.’” They are quick to attribute blessings, victory, and prosperity to Allah. “But when something evil befalls them, they say, ‘This is from you.’” They blame the Prophet and the believers for their misfortunes, as if adherence to Islam brought them bad luck.
