This is a superstitious and ungrateful mindset. The Prophet is ordered to correct them: “Say, O Prophet, ‘Both have been destined by Allah.’” Everything, good and evil in the sense of fortune and misfortune, occurs by Allah’s decree and permission. This is a core doctrine of qadar. The verse then expresses divine exasperation at their intellectual failure: “So what is the matter with these people? They can hardly comprehend anything!” (fa-mā li-hāʾulāʾi al-qawmi lā yakādūna yafqahūna ḥadīthan). They fail to grasp the simplest truths about death and divine decree. Their thinking is so shallow that they cannot string together a coherent understanding of causality and fate. This verse trains the believers to have a consistent theology of qadar, acknowledging Allah as the ultimate source of all events, while still recognising human responsibility, a balance that will be further clarified in the next verse.
79 | Whatever good befalls you is from Allah and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself. We have sent you ˹O Prophet˺ as a messenger to ˹all˺ people. And Allah is sufficient as a Witness. | At first glance, this verse may seem to contradict the previous one, which attributed both good and evil to Allah’s decree. However, the two verses address different dimensions. The previous verse speaks about events, fortune, and misfortune in terms of creation and divine decree: everything that happens does so by Allah’s will. This verse speaks in terms of causality and responsibility. “Whatever good befalls you is from Allah.” All good things—blessings, guidance, success—are ultimately from Allah’s grace. “And whatever evil befalls you is from yourself.” The evils that afflict a person, particularly as consequences of their actions, are the result of their own sins and poor choices. This is a well-established Qur’anic principle: “Whatever misfortune strikes you is because of what your hands have earned.” The hypocrites who blamed the Prophet for their misfortunes are thus corrected. Their losses, defeats, and troubles were not caused by the Prophet’s presence but by their own hypocrisy, sins, and refusal to follow commands. The verse does not deny Allah’s ultimate decree over evil but clarifies that the immediate cause and moral responsibility lie with the human being. After this profound theological point, the verse turns to the Prophet’s mission: “We have sent you O Prophet as a messenger to all people.” This universal scope underscores that his message and the guidance he brings are for all of humanity, not just a particular tribe. The verse then concludes: “And Allah is sufficient as a Witness.” He witnesses the Prophet’s delivery of the message and the people’s response. For the Prophet, this is a reassurance; for the people, it is a warning that their rejection or acceptance is fully recorded. The balance in these two verses (78-79) is a masterclass in Islamic theology, affirming both comprehensive divine decree and genuine human accountability without contradiction.
80 | Whoever obeys the Messenger has truly obeyed Allah. But whoever turns away, then ˹know that˺ We have not sent you ˹O Prophet˺ as a keeper over them. | After the theological clarification, the surah returns to the theme of obedience that runs through the entire passage. “Whoever obeys the Messenger has truly obeyed Allah.” This is a statement of immense gravity. It erases any possible separation between obedience to Allah and obedience to His Messenger. To obey the Prophet is not a separate religious act; it is the very embodiment of obedience to Allah. The commands the Prophet gives in matters of religion are not his own invention; they are based on revelation. Therefore, one cannot claim to love Allah, obey Allah, or follow Allah while disregarding the words and example of the Messenger. This principle applies to every era. Then the verse addresses the painful reality of rejection: “But whoever turns away, then know that We have not sent you O Prophet as a keeper over them.” The Prophet’s duty is to convey the message clearly, to warn, and to call people to the truth. He is not a ḥafīẓ, a guardian appointed to force people into belief or to record and punish their every disobedience. The guidance is offered, but the choice ultimately rests with each individual. If they turn away, the Prophet is not held accountable for their rejection; his responsibility is discharged by the delivery. This verse is a source of relief for the Prophet, who was deeply pained by the rejection of his people. It frees him from the psychological burden of feeling personally responsible for their salvation. It also serves as a warning to those who turn away: their rejection does not harm the Prophet or Allah; it only harms themselves, and they will meet the consequences. This verse completes a circle of themes: the call to obedience, the clarification of judgment, the acceptance of human free will, and the limits of prophetic responsibility.
81 | And they say, “We obey,” but when they leave you, a group of them would spend the night contradicting what they said. Allah records all their schemes. So turn away from them, and put your trust in Allah. And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs. | This verse exposes another layer of hypocritical behaviour, directly following the principle that obeying the Messenger is obeying Allah. It begins with the hypocrites’ verbal declaration: “And they say, ‘We obey.’” In the presence of the Prophet, they mouth the words of loyalty and compliance. But the verse immediately reveals the hidden reality: “but when they leave you, a group of them would spend the night contradicting what they said.” Under the cover of darkness, away from the Prophet’s gaze, they gather and plot, speaking words that are the opposite of their daytime affirmations. The Arabic yubayyitūna conveys the idea of secretly scheming during the night. This is not a mere change of opinion; it is a deliberate, conspiratorial contradiction. The verse then provides a chilling reassurance: “Allah records all their schemes.” The word “records” (yaktubu) indicates a meticulous, comprehensive documentation. Every whispered word, every hidden plot, every secret meeting is inscribed in the divine record. Nothing escapes. The hypocrites may think they have successfully deceived the Prophet and the believers, but they are utterly exposed before Allah. Given this reality, the Prophet is instructed: “So turn away from them, and put your trust in Allah.” The turning away is not a dismissal of the duty to warn, but a refusal to become consumed by their scheming or to seek revenge personally. The command to “put your trust in Allah” (tawakkul) is the spiritual antidote to the anxiety caused by hidden enemies. The verse closes with the powerful affirmation: “And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs.” Allah takes full responsibility for the affairs of those who trust in Him. He will manage the consequences of the hypocrites’ treachery, protect the believers, and ultimately bring justice. This verse teaches believers that when faced with duplicity and secret plotting, their primary response is not paranoia or counter-scheming, but a calm entrusting of the matter to Allah, coupled with a wise disengagement from the toxic influence of the deceivers.
82 | Do they not then reflect on the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have certainly found in it many inconsistencies. | After detailing the hypocrites’ secret schemes, the surah turns the focus to the very source of guidance they are neglecting. The rhetorical question “Do they not then reflect on the Quran?” is a piercing call to intellectual and spiritual engagement. The Arabic yatadabbarūn comes from dubur, meaning the back or end of something, implying a deep, careful consideration of the meanings, aims, and consequences of the Qur’anic verses. It is not a superficial recitation but a profound pondering. The hypocrites and those who waver are challenged to examine the Book with honesty. The verse then presents a logical test for the divine origin of the Qur’an: “Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have certainly found in it many inconsistencies.” This is a rational argument. Any human production, especially one as lengthy and wide-ranging as the Qur’an, would inevitably contain contradictions, errors, or discrepancies when addressing diverse topics over many years. Yet the Qur’an, revealed over twenty-three years in varying circumstances, remains perfectly consistent in its theology, its legal principles, its narratives, and its style. There is no contradiction between its promise of mercy and its threat of punishment, between its call to fight and its command for peace, or between its various historical accounts. Every part harmonises with the whole. The verse implies that the failure to find such inconsistencies is a clear sign of its divine authorship. The hypocrites’ problem is not a lack of evidence but a lack of genuine reflection. Their hearts are sealed because they do not approach the Qur’an with sincerity. This verse stands as a permanent challenge to all doubters and an invitation to believers to deepen their engagement with the Qur’an through tadabbur. It teaches that a superficial reading may lead to confusion, but deep, holistic reflection guided by sound knowledge always leads to the clarity of its divine unity.
83 | And when they hear news of security or fear, they publicize it. Had they referred it to the Messenger or their authorities, those with sound judgment among them would have validated it. Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy, you would have followed Satan—except for a few. | This verse addresses a dangerous social behaviour that threatens community security: the irresponsible spread of information. “And when they hear news of security or fear, they publicize it.” The context is the fragile security situation in Medina, where rumours of enemy movements, victories, or defeats could cause panic or false confidence. The hypocrites and those with weak faith would take any rumour and broadcast it without verification. This recklessness could demoralise the believers, aid the enemy’s psychological warfare, or disrupt military planning. The verse then provides the correct protocol: “Had they referred it to the Messenger or their authorities, those with sound judgment among them would have validated it.” Information should be channelled to those in command and those with knowledge and wisdom, who can analyse it, verify its truth, and decide on the appropriate response. The phrase “those with sound judgment” (alladhīna yastanbiṭūnahu minhum) refers to people capable of extracting the true implications of a report. This is a foundational principle of media literacy and responsible communication in an Islamic society. Not every piece of information is meant for public consumption. The verse then turns to a profound spiritual reality: “Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy, you would have followed Satan—except for a few.” The human tendency to spread rumours, to panic, and to be manipulated by sensational news is a satanic inclination. Only Allah’s grace and mercy protect the believers from falling into these traps on a large scale. The “except for a few” acknowledges that some truly steadfast believers would resist even without that grace, but the majority are saved by divine intervention. This verse is remarkably relevant in the age of social media, where unverified information spreads instantly. It teaches the obligation to verify before sharing, to refer matters to qualified authorities, and to recognise that the discipline of the tongue and the keyboard is a matter of divine grace.
84 | So fight in the cause of Allah ˹O Prophet˺. You are accountable for none but yourself. And motivate the believers ˹to fight˺, so perhaps Allah will curb the disbelievers’ might. And Allah is far superior in might and in punishment. | The surah returns to the theme of military struggle with a direct command to the Prophet. “So fight in the cause of Allah O Prophet.” This is a personal obligation upon him, regardless of the hesitancy of others. The command is then immediately qualified with a profound statement of individual responsibility: “You are accountable for none but yourself.” The Prophet is not held responsible for those who refuse to fight, who lag behind, or who harbour hypocrisy in their hearts. His duty is to fulfil the command himself and deliver the message. The salvation of others is ultimately their own concern. This is a restatement of the principle that no soul bears the burden of another, and a comfort to the Prophet that the failures of his followers do not diminish his own standing with Allah if he has done his part. However, personal accountability does not absolve him of the duty to lead and encourage: “And motivate the believers to fight.” He must inspire, exhort, and rally the faithful to join the struggle. The purpose of this fighting is then stated: “so perhaps Allah will curb the disbelievers’ might.” The Arabic baʾs means strength, power, and military might. The believers are a tool through which Allah suppresses the aggressive power of those who oppose His cause. The verse closes with the ultimate source of confidence: “And Allah is far superior in might and in punishment.” The disbelievers may have worldly strength, but Allah’s might is infinitely greater. His punishment for the aggressors is more severe than any harm they can inflict. This verse balances the realism of individual responsibility with the necessity of communal effort and the reassurance that the final outcome rests in the hands of the Almighty. It teaches that leaders must lead by example, must not be paralysed by the failings of followers, and must constantly redirect hearts toward reliance on Allah’s superior power.
85 | Whoever intercedes for a good cause will have a share in the reward, and whoever intercedes for an evil cause will have a share in the burden. And Allah is Watchful over all things. | This verse lays down a general principle that governs the moral responsibility of influence and mediation. “Whoever intercedes for a good cause” translates the Arabic man yashfaʿ shafāʿatan ḥasanatan. Shafāʿah literally means intercession or mediation; it refers to any act where a person uses their influence, words, or efforts to help someone else obtain a benefit or avoid a harm. If this intercession is for a “good cause”—something that is right, just, and pleasing to Allah—then the intercessor “will have a share in the reward.” They are not merely a neutral facilitator; they become a partner in the good deed and receive a portion of its divine reward. This is a tremendous encouragement for believers to actively help others in matters of charity, reconciliation, justice, and all forms of righteousness. Conversely, “whoever intercedes for an evil cause will have a share in the burden.” If a person uses their influence to secure something sinful, unjust, or harmful—whether it is helping someone obtain an illegal gain, evade punishment, or oppress another—they bear a portion of the sin. The word “burden” (kifl) implies a heavy load of guilt. This principle was directly applied to the hypocrites who interceded for one another in their schemes against the believers. The verse closes with “And Allah is Watchful over all things.” The Arabic muqīt is rich in meaning: it can mean Watchful, All-Powerful, and the Guardian who provides sustenance. Allah monitors every act of intercession, knowing the intentions behind it and the consequences that flow from it. Nothing escapes His observation. This verse is a cornerstone of Islamic social ethics, making every believer conscious that their influence—whether in a courtroom, a workplace, a family dispute, or a government office—is a trust for which they will be held accountable.
86 | And when you are greeted, respond with a better greeting or at least similarly. Surely Allah is a ˹vigilant˺ Reckoner of all things. | This short verse establishes a beautiful rule of social conduct. “And when you are greeted, respond with a better greeting or at least similarly.” The greeting referred to is the universal Islamic salutation of peace: “Al-salāmu ʿalaykum” (Peace be upon you). The verse commands that when someone offers this greeting, the recipient must respond. The minimum response is to return it equally: “Wa ʿalaykum al-salām” (And upon you be peace). However, the verse encourages a higher standard: respond with a better greeting. For example, if someone says “Peace be upon you,” one could respond “And upon you be peace and the mercy of Allah,” adding an extra blessing. This principle of returning a greeting with an increase or at least its equivalent is an expression of generosity, goodwill, and the strengthening of social bonds. The historical context also includes an incident where some Jews greeted the Prophet by twisting the words to say “al-sāmu ʿalaykum” (Death be upon you). The Prophet taught the believers to reply simply “Wa ʿalaykum” (And upon you), thereby neutralising the evil intent without stooping to their level. The verse’s command applies to the standard greeting of peace and, by extension, to all forms of courteous salutation among believers. The verse closes with a reminder: “Surely Allah is a vigilant Reckoner of all things.” Even the exchange of greetings, a seemingly small social courtesy, is recorded and accounted for by Allah. The quality of one’s response, the warmth in one’s voice, the choice to add a beautiful extra blessing—all of it falls under divine scrutiny. This elevates everyday politeness from a mundane habit to an act of worship. It teaches that a believer’s entire social demeanour should be marked by generosity, even in words, and that no act of kindness is too small to be noticed by Allah.
87 | Allah, there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. He will certainly gather ˹all of˺ you together on the Day of Judgment—about which there is no doubt. And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s? | After a series of social and military commands, the surah refocuses the heart on the core of faith and the ultimate accountability. “Allah, there is no god worthy of worship except Him.” This is the foundational declaration of tawḥīd, negating divinity from all else and affirming it exclusively for Allah. It is a reminder that every command discussed—from greeting to fighting—is rooted in the worship of the One God. The verse then immediately connects this monotheistic belief to the Day of Judgment: “He will certainly gather all of you together on the Day of Judgment—about which there is no doubt.” The gathering (jāmiʿ) is a direct consequence of His oneness. Because He is the sole Creator and Sovereign, He has the absolute right and power to summon all humanity, from the first to the last, to stand before Him for judgment. The emphatic language “certainly” (la-yajmaʿannakum) and “no doubt” (lā rayba fīhi) leaves no room for scepticism. The diverse and scattered deeds of a lifetime will be brought together in a single, inescapable court. The verse then poses a rhetorical question that seals the argument: “And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s?” The Arabic ṣidq means truthfulness and veracity. If Allah, the most truthful of all speakers, has made this promise, then it is more certain than any fact perceived by the senses. Any denial of the Day of Judgment is effectively an accusation of falsehood against God, which is an absurdity. This verse serves as a powerful anchor within the surah, reminding the believers that every instruction about honesty, inheritance, fighting, and greeting will be weighed on that Day. It both comforts the oppressed, assuring them that ultimate justice awaits, and warns the oppressor that no evasion is possible.
88 | Why are you ˹believers˺ divided into two groups regarding the hypocrites while Allah allowed them to regress ˹to disbelief˺ because of their misdeeds? Do you wish to guide those left by Allah to stray? And whoever Allah leaves to stray, you will never find for them a way. | The verse addresses a specific confusion that arose among the believers regarding a group of hypocrites. “Why are you believers divided into two groups regarding the hypocrites?” Some believers were inclined to treat them with leniency and hope for their reformation, while others insisted on firmness and even military action against them. The verse reveals the divine perspective: “while Allah allowed them to regress to disbelief because of their misdeeds.” The hypocrites had committed sins and followed a path of deception until Allah, in His just decree, caused them to revert to a state of open disbelief. They were not victims of circumstance but architects of their own spiritual ruin. Their actions caused their hearts to be overturned. Given this divine judgment, the verse asks a penetrating question: “Do you wish to guide those left by Allah to stray?” It is a gentle rebuke to those believers who were perhaps too optimistic about reforming people whom Allah, in His knowledge, had already recorded as astray because of their persistent, unrepentant choices. The verse does not discourage calling people to faith in general, but it addresses a specific group whose deep-rooted hypocrisy had been sealed by Allah’s decree. The final statement is absolute: “And whoever Allah leaves to stray, you will never find for them a way.” No human effort, no wisdom, no emotional appeal can guide a person whom Allah has willed to leave in misguidance, because that divine will is the consequence of their own free, persistent rejection of the truth. The door of guidance is open for those who seek it, but for those who have turned their backs irreversibly, no path to salvation remains. This verse teaches the believers a sober realism about spiritual states, balancing the duty to invite to truth with the recognition that ultimate guidance is solely in Allah’s hands.
89 | They wish you would disbelieve as they have disbelieved, so you may all be alike. So do not take them as allies unless they emigrate in the cause of Allah. But if they turn away, then seize them and kill them wherever you find them, and do not take any of them as allies or helpers, | After clarifying the state of the hypocrites, the verse reveals their deep-seated desire. “They wish you would disbelieve as they have disbelieved, so you may all be alike.” The hypocrites are not content with their own disbelief; they actively desire to drag the believers down to their level. There is a psychological comfort in seeing others share one’s moral decline; it validates the choice and reduces the reproach of a guilty conscience. This is a warning to the believers to be vigilant against the subtle influence of such people, who may not openly invite to disbelief but create an atmosphere of doubt and compromise. The verse then issues a clear command: “So do not take them as allies unless they emigrate in the cause of Allah.” The condition for any form of alliance or close friendship is that they prove their sincerity by undertaking hijrah, leaving the environment of hostility and joining the community of faith in a sacrificial move. If they refuse this condition and instead “turn away,” the command is severe: “then seize them and kill them wherever you find them, and do not take any of them as allies or helpers.” This ruling applied to those hypocrites who stayed among the disbelievers and actively worked against the Muslim community, effectively becoming combatants. It is not a blanket command against all hypocrites; those who lived peacefully within Medina, even with known hypocrisy, were not subjected to this treatment, as the Prophet’s own conduct demonstrates. The verse, read in context, addresses a wartime situation where loyalty to the enemy camp had been clearly established. It underscores the principle that passive or active support for forces at war with the Muslim community constitutes a breach of the social contract and warrants the severest response. The severity aims to protect the community from internal sabotage.
90 | except those who are allies of a people you are bound with in a treaty or those wholeheartedly opposed to fighting either you or their own people. If Allah had willed, He would have empowered them to fight you. So if they refrain from fighting you and offer you peace, then Allah does not permit you to harm them. | This verse immediately carves out two vital exceptions to the preceding command to seize and kill the hostile hypocrites. The first exception is “except those who are allies of a people you are bound with in a treaty.” If the hypocrites have joined a tribe that has a formal peace treaty with the Muslims, they are protected under that treaty. The sanctity of the covenant overrides their individual hypocrisy. The second exception is even more nuanced: “or those wholeheartedly opposed to fighting either you or their own people.” This refers to individuals who come to the Muslims genuinely conflicted, their hearts constricted (ḥaṣirat ṣudūruhum) by the idea of fighting against the believers or against their own tribe. They are in a state of psychological distress, unwilling to shed blood on either side. These people are not to be harmed. The verse then provides a theological reflection: “If Allah had willed, He would have empowered them to fight you.” Their reluctance to fight is itself a divine mercy. If Allah had decreed, they could have been made eager fighters against the Muslims. Their neutrality is a gift. The verse then states a clear principle: “So if they refrain from fighting you and offer you peace, then Allah does not permit you to harm them.” Peaceful conduct and a genuine desire to avoid bloodshed must be met with acceptance. This verse is a cornerstone of Islamic rules of engagement. It demonstrates that hostility is not assumed based on group identity; it is based on actual aggression. The default posture toward those who offer peace is to accept it. The legal and military rigour of the previous verse is thus humanised and balanced by a profound commitment to honouring treaties and protecting those who genuinely seek to avoid conflict.
91 | You will find others who wish to be safe from you and their own people. Yet they cannot resist the temptation ˹of disbelief or hostility˺. If they do not keep away, offer you peace, or refrain from attacking you, then seize them and kill them wherever you find them. We have given you full permission over such people. | The surah now describes a third, more dangerous category of people. “You will find others who wish to be safe from you and their own people.” These are the ultimate opportunists. When they are among the Muslims, they profess Islam to enjoy safety. When they return to their disbelieving people, they revert to disbelief to enjoy safety there. They are spiritual chameleons, belonging fully to neither side. “Yet they cannot resist the temptation of disbelief or hostility.” The Arabic fitnah here refers to the trial of being drawn back into active hostility and persecution. Whenever they are tested, they fall back into fighting against the believers. Their desire for safety is not matched by any principled commitment to peace. The verse then sets a clear condition: “If they do not keep away, offer you peace, or refrain from attacking you.” Three things are demanded: they must physically withdraw, they must formally offer peace, and they must stop their aggressive actions. If they fail to do all three, the command is “then seize them and kill them wherever you find them.” Their perpetual instability and eventual reversion to hostility make them an ongoing threat. The verse closes by asserting the divine sanction for this action: “We have given you full permission over such people.” This is not an unjust aggression but a divinely authorised response to a pattern of treachery that undermines the security of the community. The verse demonstrates that while Islam is always open to genuine peace, it does not tolerate serial treachery that uses the mask of peace to enable repeated attacks. The permission is given to neutralise a clear and present danger, not to persecute people for their inner beliefs.
92 | It is not lawful for a believer to kill another except by mistake. And whoever kills a believer unintentionally must free a believing slave and pay blood-money to the victim’s family—unless they waive it charitably. But if the victim is a believer from a hostile people, then a believing slave must be freed. And if the victim is from a people bound with you in a treaty, then blood-money must be paid to the family along with freeing a believing slave. Those who are unable, let them fast two consecutive months—as a means of repentance to Allah. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | This verse addresses the sanctity of a believer’s life and the legal consequences of accidental killing. It opens with a fundamental declaration: “It is not lawful for a believer to kill another except by mistake.” Intentional killing of a believer is a crime of the highest order, as will be addressed in the next verse. The current verse focuses on unintentional killing, which, although a grave tragedy, does not carry the same legal and spiritual weight as murder. The verse then outlines the required expiation and compensation: “And whoever kills a believer unintentionally must free a believing slave and pay blood-money to the victim’s family—unless they waive it charitably.” The expiation is twofold. First, the freeing of a believing slave, a profound act that served the dual purpose of emancipating a human being and acting as a spiritual purification for the killer. Second, the payment of blood-money (diyah) to the family of the deceased, a fixed sum specified in the Sunnah, which provides material recompense for the loss. The verse adds that the family may charitably waive the blood-money, an act of immense virtue. The verse then differentiates two other scenarios. “But if the victim is a believer from a hostile people,” meaning a Muslim living among enemies at war with the Islamic state, then only the freeing of a believing slave is required, and no blood-money is paid to the hostile family. “And if the victim is from a people bound with you in a treaty,” a non-Muslim from a tribe with whom the Muslims have a covenant, then blood-money must be paid to the family along with freeing a believing slave. This shows that the right to compensation is tied to the treaty relationship. Finally, for those who cannot afford to free a slave, the verse prescribes a spiritual alternative: “let them fast two consecutive months—as a means of repentance to Allah.” This two-month fast is a severe discipline that purifies the soul and fosters deep reflection on the sanctity of life. The verse ends by reminding that Allah is All-Knowing of intentions and All-Wise in legislation.
93 | And whoever kills a believer intentionally, their reward will be Hell—where they will stay indefinitely. Allah will be displeased with them, condemn them, and will prepare for them a tremendous punishment. | After the detailed rulings on accidental killing, the verse addresses the crime of intentional murder of a believer with terrifying severity. “And whoever kills a believer intentionally, their reward will be Hell—where they will stay indefinitely.” The phrase “their reward” (jazāʾuhu) is used ironically; the only recompense they will receive for their heinous act is Hell. The description of staying in Hell “indefinitely” (khālidan fīhā) has been the subject of careful scholarly discussion. The overwhelming majority of Ahl al-Sunnah hold that a Muslim who commits murder does not become an eternal denizen of Hell like a disbeliever. Rather, the verse indicates a very long, prolonged stay, or it refers to a person who kills a believer while considering that killing lawful, thereby committing an act of disbelief. Alternatively, it is an expression of severe threat (waʿīd) that Allah may choose to execute or forgive based on His will, especially if the murderer sincerely repents. The verse then intensifies the gravity by listing Allah’s direct reactions: “Allah will be displeased with them, condemn them, and will prepare for them a tremendous punishment.” The displeasure (ghaḍib) of Allah is the worst possible state for a human being. To have the Creator of the universe angry with you is a catastrophe beyond imagination. The condemnation (laʿanahum) means being cast far from divine mercy, cursed and rejected. The “tremendous punishment” is an additional, explicit preparation of torment. This verse, alongside numerous hadith, underscores that the taking of an innocent life is one of the most monstrous sins in Islam. It is second only to shirk in its gravity. The sanctity of a believer’s blood is established here with a force that was meant to completely transform a society where tribal vengeance and bloodshed were rampant. It teaches that life belongs to Allah, and no human has the right to deliberately extinguish it without a just, divinely sanctioned cause.
94 | O believers! When you struggle in the cause of Allah, be sure of who you fight. And do not say to those who offer you ˹greetings of˺ peace, “You are no believer!”—seeking a fleeting worldly gain. Instead, Allah has infinite bounties ˹in store˺. You were initially like them then Allah blessed you ˹with Islam˺. So be sure! Indeed, Allah is All-Aware of what you do. | This verse addresses a specific incident that occurred during a military expedition and sets a profound ethical principle for combat. A man from a hostile tribe, shepherding his flock, encountered a group of Muslim soldiers. When he greeted them with the salutation of peace (salām), indicating his acceptance of Islam, they killed him and took his sheep, assuming his greeting was merely a ruse to save his life and property. The verse was revealed to correct this grave error. “O believers! When you struggle in the cause of Allah, be sure of who you fight.” The Arabic tabayyanū means to verify, to ascertain the truth before acting. The command is to exercise caution and not to act on mere suspicion or appearance. “And do not say to those who offer you greetings of peace, ‘You are no believer!’” A person who utters the Islamic greeting is, on the face of it, declaring their faith. To reject that declaration and kill them is a tremendous sin. The verse exposes the potential corrupt motive: “seeking a fleeting worldly gain.” The soldiers’ desire for the spoils of war (the sheep) led them to dismiss the clear sign of faith. The verse then redirects their focus: “Instead, Allah has infinite bounties in store.” The spoils of this world are nothing compared to what Allah provides to those who fear Him. The verse further appeals to their own past: “You were initially like them then Allah blessed you with Islam.” They themselves were once disbelievers, and it was Allah’s grace that guided them. They should not be the ones who now deny that same grace to others. The command “So be sure!” is repeated for emphasis. The verse closes with a reminder that Allah is fully aware of all actions, inner and outer. This verse establishes the absolute prohibition of treating a person who appears to be a Muslim as an enemy, and it safeguards the integrity of the declaration of faith against cynical, worldly calculations.
95 | Those who stay at home—except those with valid excuses—are not equal to those who strive in the cause of Allah with their wealth and their lives. Allah has elevated in rank those who strive with their wealth and their lives above those who stay behind ˹with valid excuses˺. Allah has promised each a fine reward, but those who strive will receive a far better reward than others— | This verse establishes the spiritual hierarchy between those who struggle in jihad and those who remain behind. It begins with a clear inequality: “Those who stay at home—except those with valid excuses—are not equal to those who strive in the cause of Allah with their wealth and their lives.” The crucial exception is “those with valid excuses” (ulī al-ḍarar), referring to people with genuine disabilities, blindness, chronic illness, or other hindrances that prevent them from participating in combat. Their inability is not a moral failing, and the verse exempts them from the comparison. For those without such excuses, however, the mujāhid who sacrifices both wealth and life holds a rank far above the one who sits comfortably at home. The verse then specifies: “Allah has elevated in rank those who strive with their wealth and their lives above those who stay behind with valid excuses.” Even those with valid excuses, though not blamed, do not attain the same rank as the strivers. The elevation is a matter of spiritual degree, reward, and honour with Allah. However, the verse immediately balances this by adding, “Allah has promised each a fine reward.” Both the strivers and those with valid excuses who sincerely believe and do what they can are promised al-ḥusnā, the best reward, meaning Paradise. The mercy of Allah ensures that those with genuine constraints are not deprived of His bounty. Yet, the verse concludes by emphasising the distinction: “but those who strive will receive a far better reward than others.” The “far better reward” (ajran ʿaẓīman) is a greater, more magnificent recompense that reflects the magnitude of their sacrifice. This verse was revealed, according to some narrations, to honour the blind companion Ibn Umm Maktūm and others like him, while also spurring the able-bodied. It teaches that while physical inability is excused, the sacrifice of life and wealth in the path of truth carries an incomparable spiritual value.
96 | far superior ranks, forgiveness, and mercy from Him. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | This verse completes the description of the “far better reward” promised to those who strive. It begins by specifying the nature of this reward: “far superior ranks.” The Arabic darajāt means degrees or stations of honour in Paradise. The strivers are elevated to heights that others, even the forgiven, may not reach. Their special closeness to Allah is a direct fruit of their sacrifice. Next is “forgiveness” (maghfirah). Their sins are not just overlooked; they are completely covered and erased. The act of struggling, which involves leaving behind comfort and facing death, serves as a profound expiation for past misdeeds. The Prophet confirmed that being wounded or suffering in the path of Allah causes sins to fall away. Then the verse adds “and mercy from Him” (raḥmah). Mercy here is a positive bestowal of divine tenderness, care, and blessing beyond the removal of sin. It is the embrace of Allah’s loving-kindness. The verse closes with the pairing of two divine names: “And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” This closing is deeply significant. After describing the severe obligations of fighting and the sacrifices demanded, the verse reminds the believers that the One commanding them is not a harsh taskmaster but the Oft-Forgiving, the Most Merciful. He knows the weaknesses and fears of His servants, and He is ready to forgive their shortcomings and shower them with mercy when they strive. The pairing of these names also implies that the rank and reward given to the strivers are not merely a wage but a manifestation of Allah’s forgiving and merciful nature. This verse, together with the previous one, creates a comprehensive picture of the divine reward system: justice in acknowledging different levels of effort, compassion in excusing the genuinely unable, and boundless generosity in rewarding sacrifice with exalted stations, complete pardon, and enveloping mercy.
97 | When the angels seize the souls of those who have wronged themselves—scolding them, “What do you think you were doing?” they will reply, “We were oppressed in the land.” The angels will respond, “Was Allah’s earth not spacious enough for you to emigrate?” It is they who will have Hell as their home—what an evil destination! | This verse describes a tragic category of people at the moment of death. It begins with a terrifying scene: “When the angels seize the souls of those who have wronged themselves.” The “wrong” here is their failure to emigrate from a place where they could not freely practice their faith. The angels of death come to them “scolding them,” a severe and harsh reprimand. The question they are asked is, “What do you think you were doing?” It is a question of condemnation, exposing their complacency and the flimsiness of their excuses. In response, they will plead: “We were oppressed in the land.” They will argue that they were weak and unable to practice their religion due to the persecution of the disbelievers. This is their justification for remaining among the enemies of Islam. But the angels will reject this plea with a devastating counter-question: “Was Allah’s earth not spacious enough for you to emigrate?” Allah’s earth is vast. If one place makes it impossible to worship Allah, the duty is to move to a place where it is possible. The argument of being “oppressed” is not accepted when emigration was a viable option. The verse then pronounces their fate: “It is they who will have Hell as their home—what an evil destination!” This is a stark warning against the complacency that leads a person to accept living in an environment where their faith is constantly compromised, when a way out exists. The verse addresses those who had the physical and financial ability to emigrate from Mecca but chose to stay, often collaborating with the Quraysh against the Muslims. It establishes the principle that a believer must not willingly remain in a state of subjugation that prevents the practice of Islam, if migration is possible. It is a powerful call to prioritise faith over homeland, comfort, or family when a choice must be made.
98 | Except helpless men, women, and children who cannot afford a way out— | This brief verse immediately follows the severe condemnation of those who failed to emigrate, providing the divinely ordained exception. “Except helpless men, women, and children who cannot afford a way out.” The Arabic word mustaḍʿafūn means those who are deemed weak and are oppressed, having no means or strength to change their situation. The verse specifically includes men, women, and children, covering all who are genuinely unable to migrate. The phrase “cannot afford a way out” (lā yastaṭīʿūna ḥīlatan wa lā yahtadūna sabīlan) is beautifully encompassing. They cannot devise any plan (ḥīlah), meaning they lack the financial resources, the physical strength, the cunning, or the connections to escape. They also do not find a path (sabīl), meaning they know of no route, no guide, and no opportunity to leave. This is a state of complete, objective helplessness. The verse acknowledges that not everyone has the capacity to simply pack up and leave a hostile environment. The divine law is perfectly just, and it does not burden a soul beyond its capacity. This exception is a profound mercy, distinguishing between those who are content with oppression and those who are trapped by it. The sincere but weak believers who remained in Mecca, such as some of the early converts who were prevented by their families or by poverty from making the hijrah, were covered by this verse. Their helplessness was a shield from the condemnation of the preceding verse. This clause teaches that judgment of individuals is not based on surface-level circumstances but on their genuine ability and effort. Allah knows the constraints of every soul and judges accordingly.
99 | it is right to hope that Allah will pardon them. For Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving. | This verse completes the statement of the exception for the helpless. “It is right to hope that Allah will pardon them.” The Arabic ʿasā Allāh conveys a sense of hopeful expectation. For these genuinely oppressed and incapable individuals, there is a strong and well-founded hope that Allah will overlook whatever lapses or compromises they were forced into by their helplessness. The use of “hope” rather than a definitive promise is understood by scholars to reflect the proper humility of a believer; one never presumes upon Allah’s forgiveness but remains in a state of hopeful fear. However, many commentators state that this “hope” from Allah is a form of gentle promise, as Allah’s generosity would not disappoint such sincere hope. The verse then grounds this hope in two divine names: “For Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving.” The Ever-Pardoning (ʿAfuww) is the One who completely erases the traces of sins and does not even hold the servant to an accounting for them out of His magnanimity. The All-Forgiving (Ghafūr) is the One who covers and conceals sins abundantly. These names assure that the helpless, who genuinely desired to follow their faith but were prevented, are under the vast canopy of divine pardon. Their forced confessions or outward accommodations to the oppressors will not be held against them if their hearts were firm in faith. This verse, together with the preceding one, provides a timeless comfort to believers who live in circumstances of severe restriction and persecution. It assures them that Allah sees their hearts, knows their inability, and will deal with them with pardon and forgiveness, far beyond what their outward circumstances might suggest.
100 | Whoever emigrates in the cause of Allah will find many safe havens and bountiful resources throughout the earth. Those who leave their homes and die while emigrating to Allah and His Messenger—their reward has already been secured with Allah. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | After the severe warning and the merciful exception, the surah now highlights the immense spiritual reward and promise attached to emigration. “Whoever emigrates in the cause of Allah will find many safe havens and bountiful resources throughout the earth.” The verb “emigrates” (yuhājir) means to leave one’s homeland purely for the sake of Allah, abandoning attachment to place and tribe. The promise is that Allah’s earth is vast and filled with “safe havens” (murāghaman kathīran), a word that implies a place of refuge where one’s enemies cannot reach, and “bountiful resources” (saʿatan), meaning spaciousness in provision and freedom of worship. This is a reassurance to the hesitant that migration will not lead to destitution but to new and abundant opportunities provided by Allah. The second part of the verse elevates the reward even further for those who take the step but do not live to reach their destination: “Those who leave their homes and die while emigrating to Allah and His Messenger—their reward has already been secured with Allah.” The word “secured” (waqaʿa) indicates that their reward is a settled, guaranteed matter, as if it has already taken effect. They did not complete the physical journey, but their intention and their initial action are sufficient for the full reward of emigration. An authentic hadith recounts the story of a man who set out for Medina and died on the way. When some suggested he missed the reward, the verse was revealed to show that his reward was already divinely fixed. The verse closes with “And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful,” comforting all emigrants that whatever sins or shortcomings accompanied their journey are enveloped in divine forgiveness. This verse has inspired generations of believers to make difficult choices for their faith, trusting that Allah’s provision and reward are infinitely greater than what they leave behind.
81 | And they say, “We obey,” but when they leave you, a group of them would spend the night contradicting what they said. Allah records all their schemes. So turn away from them, and put your trust in Allah. And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs. | This verse draws back the curtain on a particular form of hypocrisy that deeply troubled the early Muslim community. It begins by quoting the hypocrites’ verbal declaration: “And they say, ‘We obey.’” In the presence of the Prophet, they mouth the words of loyalty and compliance, assuring him that they have heard the command and will follow it. Yet the verse immediately exposes the reality that follows these meetings: “but when they leave you, a group of them would spend the night contradicting what they said.” The darkness of night becomes the cover for their true sentiments. They gather in secret, plotting and saying things that completely reverse their daytime affirmations. They might mock the commands, question the Prophet’s wisdom, or conspire to disobey. The phrase “a group of them” indicates that not all who claim to be believers engage in this; it is a specific faction whose hearts are diseased. The verse then delivers a powerful warning: “Allah records all their schemes.” The word “schemes” translates the Arabic mā yubayyitūn, which literally refers to what they plan and contrive during the night. Every whispered word, every plotted defiance, every secret mockery is being meticulously documented by the Lord from whom nothing is hidden. This is a terrifying prospect for the hypocrites, who thought their nocturnal gatherings were private and safe. The Prophet is then instructed on how to handle this: “So turn away from them, and put your trust in Allah.” He is not to be consumed by their treachery, not to waste his energy in fruitless confrontation. Turning away means not allowing their hypocrisy to disturb his peace or distract him from his mission. Instead, he must place his complete trust (tawakkul) in Allah, the ultimate Trustee of Affairs (Wakīl). The verse closes with the reassuring declaration: “And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs.” Allah will handle their scheming; He will protect His Messenger and His religion; and He will bring the hypocrites to account. For the believer, this verse teaches that when faced with duplicity and secret plotting, the correct response is not paranoia or obsession, but a dignified detachment combined with a profound reliance on Allah to manage what is beyond one’s control.
82 | Do they not then reflect on the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have certainly found in it many inconsistencies. | The surah now issues a profound intellectual challenge. After exposing the hypocrites who plot in the night and contradict what they hear, the verse asks, “Do they not then reflect on the Quran?” The Arabic word yatadabbarūn comes from dubur, meaning the back or end of something. Tadabbur means to ponder deeply, to consider the consequences and deeper meanings, to turn a matter over in one’s mind. It is not a superficial reading but a deep, contemplative engagement. The question is rhetorical and accusatory: if these hypocrites and skeptics would only seriously reflect on the Qur’an, they would be forced to recognise its divine origin. The verse then gives the criterion for this recognition: “Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have certainly found in it many inconsistencies.” The Arabic ikhtilāfan kathīran refers to manifold contradictions, discrepancies, and disharmonies. The argument is as elegant as it is conclusive. The Qur’an was revealed over twenty-three years, in different circumstances, addressing a vast array of topics—law, theology, history, morality, and eschatology. A human author, especially one who could neither read nor write, would inevitably contradict himself, forget earlier statements, or be influenced by changing moods and interests. Yet the Qur’an is marked by an astonishing internal consistency. Its themes interlock perfectly; later revelations expand and complement earlier ones without contradiction; its account of past prophets aligns without copying known sources; its legal rulings form a coherent system despite being revealed piecemeal. This absence of contradiction is presented as evidence of its divine authorship. The verse is also an invitation to every reader of every era. It calls for an honest, rational examination of the text. Doubts and questions are not forbidden; they are to be investigated through deep reflection. If someone claims to find an inconsistency, the call is to engage in careful tadabbur, because thorough study guided by sound principles will resolve apparent discrepancies. This verse thus establishes the Qur’an’s internal harmony as a standing miracle and the act of deep reflection as a primary duty of every person who encounters the Book.
83 | And when they hear news of security or fear, they publicize it. Had they referred it to the Messenger or their authorities, those with sound judgment among them would have validated it. Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy, you would have followed Satan—except for a few. | This verse addresses a dangerous social flaw that can destabilise a community: the hasty spread of unverified information. “And when they hear news of security or fear, they publicize it.” The hypocrites and weak-hearted, upon hearing any rumour—whether it be a report of impending attack (fear) or a rumour of a peace treaty (security)—immediately spread it far and wide without checking its truth. In a state of war and tension, such behaviour can cause panic, demoralise the army, give intelligence to the enemy, or create false hope that leads to complacency. The verse then prescribes the proper procedure: “Had they referred it to the Messenger or their authorities, those with sound judgment among them would have validated it.” The correct channel for sensitive information is to bring it to those in authority—the Prophet and, by extension, the leaders and scholars of the community—who have the knowledge and wisdom to assess its veracity and determine the appropriate response. The phrase “those with sound judgment” (alladhīna yastanbiṭūnahu) refers to those capable of deduction, analysis, and extracting the truth from ambiguous reports. This is the Qur’anic foundation for the principle of verifying news before disseminating it, a principle that is tragically neglected in the age of instant social media. The verse then reflects on the human condition: “Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy, you would have followed Satan—except for a few.” Left to their own devices, most people would naturally be drawn to spreading gossip, reacting emotionally, and being led astray by misinformation. It is only by Allah’s grace and mercy—through sending the Messenger, revealing guidance, and strengthening the believers—that the majority are saved from falling into these traps. The “few” are those rare individuals with innate wisdom and steadfastness. This verse remains a timeless instruction for the Muslim community: to verify, to refer to knowledgeable authorities, and to resist the temptation to publicise every unconfirmed piece of news, recognising that such restraint is a mercy from Allah.
84 | So fight in the cause of Allah ˹O Prophet˺. You are accountable for none but yourself. And motivate the believers ˹to fight˺, so perhaps Allah will curb the disbelievers’ might. And Allah is far superior in might and in punishment. | After exposing the hypocrisy and the rumour-mongering, the verse turns to the Prophet with a direct and personal command. “So fight in the cause of Allah O Prophet.” The singular imperative emphasises that regardless of the reluctance or failure of others, the Prophet himself must continue the struggle. His duty is not contingent on the full support of a completely faithful army. Then comes a statement that is both a weighty responsibility and a profound release: “You are accountable for none but yourself.” The Prophet is not held responsible for the hypocrites who stay behind, the cowards who flee, or the rumour-mongers who sow discord. His personal accountability before Allah concerns his own actions: his obedience to the command to fight, his sincerity, and his effort. He will not be asked about the failures of those who were supposed to follow him. This does not negate the duty of leadership or the call to advise others; rather, it sets a boundary around the Prophet’s ultimate liability. He is then instructed: “And motivate the believers to fight.” While not accountable for their choices, he must still exhort, encourage, and rally them. The goal is stated: “so perhaps Allah will curb the disbelievers’ might.” The Arabic baʾs is the strength, courage, and military ferocity of the enemy. The Prophet’s fighting and his motivation of the believers are the means through which Allah, by His will, will break that strength. The verse closes with a comparison that dwarfs all earthly power: “And Allah is far superior in might and in punishment.” The might of the disbelievers, which seems so formidable, is nothing compared to the might of Allah. His punishment is more severe than any earthly defeat. This verse instils in the Prophet and the believers a reliance not on their own numbers but on the superior power of Allah. It is a call to action, a clarification of personal responsibility, and a source of courage rooted in the understanding that the ultimate outcome is in the hands of the Almighty.
85 | Whoever intercedes for a good cause will have a share in the reward, and whoever intercedes for an evil cause will have a share in the burden. And Allah is Watchful over all things. | This verse establishes a general principle of moral causality regarding influence and intercession. “Whoever intercedes for a good cause will have a share in the reward.” Intercession (shafāʿah) here means using one’s position, words, or efforts to help someone else achieve something. If the cause is good—such as helping an oppressed person, reconciling between people, supporting a charitable project, or encouraging someone to do a righteous deed—the intercessor earns a portion of the reward of that good action, even if they did not perform it directly. This is a profound incentive. It means that one’s capacity to do good is not limited to one’s own hands; by encouraging, facilitating, or advocating for goodness, one multiplies one’s reward. The converse is equally true: “and whoever intercedes for an evil cause will have a share in the burden.” If someone uses their influence to help someone commit wrongdoing, to secure a job for an unqualified person through nepotism, to advocate for an unjust policy, or to facilitate a sin, they carry a share of the sin and its consequences. The word “burden” (kifl) is like a heavy load of sin. This principle, often called “the one who points to a good deed is like the one who does it,” is well-established in the Sunnah. The verse ends with a terse and powerful reminder: “And Allah is Watchful over all things.” The Arabic Muqīt is translated as Watchful but also carries the meaning of the Overseer, the One who provides sustenance and power, and the One who records and preserves. Every intercession, whether whispered in secret or spoken openly, is under the watchful observation of Allah. No advocacy for good goes unrewarded, and no facilitation of evil goes unrecorded. This verse shapes the social conscience of the believer, making them mindful of the far-reaching consequences of their influence and words, and encouraging them to be active agents of goodness in their communities.
86 | And when you are greeted, respond with a better greeting or at least similarly. Surely Allah is a ˹vigilant˺ Reckoner of all things. | This verse addresses a seemingly simple matter of social etiquette and elevates it to an act of worship governed by divine law and accountability. “And when you are greeted, respond with a better greeting or at least similarly.” The greeting referred to is the Islamic greeting of peace, “al-salāmu ʿalaykum” (peace be upon you). The verse commands that when someone offers this greeting, the recipient must respond. The minimum acceptable response is to return it equally: “wa ʿalaykum al-salām.” However, the preferred and recommended action is to respond with a better greeting: for instance, if someone says “al-salāmu ʿalaykum,” one should say “wa ʿalaykum al-salām wa raḥmatu Allāh,” adding “and the mercy of Allah.” If they add “wa raḥmatu Allāh,” one adds “wa barakātuhu,” and so on. This instruction fosters an atmosphere of generosity, warmth, and mutual love. It turns a routine social exchange into a competition in kindness. The Prophet, peace be upon him, exemplified this in his interactions, always returning the greeting in a more complete form. This etiquette also applies to greetings that come via a third party or in writing; the same principle of equal or better response applies. The verse then closes with a deeply significant statement: “Surely Allah is a vigilant Reckoner of all things.” The word Ḥasīban means that Allah keeps a meticulous account. Even an act as small as returning a greeting is recorded and will be brought to account. This reframes the greeting from a mere custom to a binding right. If someone greets you, they have placed a right upon you to respond. Neglecting this right is not just rudeness but a matter that Allah has noted and will question. Conversely, the beautiful response is a source of reward. This brief verse is a masterpiece of Islamic social teaching, showing how the divine law penetrates every corner of daily life, infusing it with justice, kindness, and a constant awareness of the final Reckoning.
87 | Allah, there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. He will certainly gather ˹all of˺ you together on the Day of Judgment—about which there is no doubt. And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s? | This verse is a powerful anchor of faith placed in the midst of the surah’s varied legal and social instructions. It begins with the fundamental declaration of tawḥīd: “Allah, there is no god worthy of worship except Him.” This is the core of the Islamic creed, the basis upon which every command and prohibition in the surah rests. It reminds the believers that the One legislating for their families, wealth, and warfare is the only true deity. The verse then immediately connects this monotheistic belief to eschatology: “He will certainly gather all of you together on the Day of Judgment—about which there is no doubt.” The gathering (jāmiʿ) of all humanity from the first to the last is a necessary consequence of Allah’s oneness and justice. If there is only one God, and He is just and all-powerful, then there must be a day when He judges every soul for its deeds. The phrase “about which there is no doubt” (lā rayba fīhi) emphasises the absolute certainty of this event. The scepticism of the hypocrites and the carelessness of the sinful will not alter the reality that this Day is coming. The verse then delivers a rhetorical question that silences all argument: “And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s?” The answer, obvious to every rational mind, is no one. Allah’s word is absolute truth. If He says the Day of Judgment is coming, it is coming. This question challenges the reader to reflect: if you truly believe that Allah’s word is the most truthful, how can you doubt His promise of resurrection and His promise of reward and punishment? This verse functions as a powerful conclusion to the preceding discussions about hypocrisy, obedience, and fighting. It brings the reader’s attention back to the ultimate reality that gives meaning to all the regulations: the oneness of Allah and the certainty of standing before Him. It is a condensed, powerful injection of faith that re-centers the heart amidst the details of law.
88 | Why are you ˹believers˺ divided into two groups regarding the hypocrites while Allah allowed them to regress ˹to disbelief˺ because of their misdeeds? Do you wish to guide those left by Allah to stray? And whoever Allah leaves to stray, you will never find for them a way. | The surah now turns to a specific dispute that had arisen among the believers concerning the hypocrites. “Why are you believers divided into two groups regarding the hypocrites?” In the early Medinan period, the believers were unsure how to deal with those who outwardly professed Islam but secretly opposed it. One group argued that they should be treated as enemies; another group was inclined to make excuses for them or to try to win them over with patience. The verse rebukes this division: the matter should not be a source of internal conflict because Allah’s judgment on them is clear. The verse then explains the reality of the hypocrites’ condition: “while Allah allowed them to regress to disbelief because of their misdeeds.” The Arabic arkasahum literally means to turn them upside down or to cause them to revert. The misdeeds of the hypocrites—their lies, their plots, their mockery—led Allah to abandon them and to allow them to sink back into the disbelief from which they had superficially emerged. Their regression is both a punishment and a natural consequence of their choices. The verse then asks a penetrating question: “Do you wish to guide those left by Allah to stray?” This is a profound theological statement. Guidance is in Allah’s hands. When Allah, based on His knowledge of a person’s persistent rejection and evil, decrees to leave them astray, no human effort can override that decree. The believers are being told that their emotional desire to save these hypocrites must not blind them to the reality that some hearts have become so sealed that they will not respond to guidance. The verse concludes with an absolute principle: “And whoever Allah leaves to stray, you will never find for them a way.” No path, no strategy, no method of dawah will succeed for a person whom Allah has willed to leave in misguidance as a just consequence of their own deep-seated choice. This teaching does not discourage calling to Allah, but it defines the limits of human responsibility and protects the believers from being emotionally devastated by the refusal of the hard-hearted.
89 | They wish you would disbelieve as they have disbelieved, so you may all be alike. So do not take them as allies unless they emigrate in the cause of Allah. But if they turn away, then seize them and kill them wherever you find them, and do not take any of them as allies or helpers, | The previous verse clarified the hopeless spiritual state of the hard-core hypocrites. This verse now reveals their hidden desire and the practical measures to be taken. “They wish you would disbelieve as they have disbelieved, so you may all be alike.” The hypocrites are not content with their own state of internal disbelief. They actively wish for the sincere believers to abandon their faith and sink to the same level of hypocrisy and rejection. This wish stems from a psychological dynamic: the presence of genuine faith is a silent reproach to their own falseness, so they seek to extinguish it by converting the faithful to their own ranks. They desire a uniformity of disbelief. Given this hostile and corrosive intent, the believers are given clear instructions: “So do not take them as allies unless they emigrate in the cause of Allah.” The condition for any kind of close alliance or trust is that they prove their sincerity by emigrating, making a physical break from their previous environment and demonstrating their commitment to the cause of Allah through sacrifice. Mere verbal profession is insufficient. The verse then delivers a severe command: “But if they turn away, then seize them and kill them wherever you find them, and do not take any of them as allies or helpers.” This refers to those hypocrites who not only refuse to emigrate but also demonstrate active opposition, potentially aiding the enemy. In a state of war and existential threat, such individuals become a fifth column that must be dealt with decisively. It is crucial to read this command within the historical context of Medina under siege and the specific legal parameters of dealing with treason, not as a general rule for all times and places. The command underscores that there is no comfortable middle ground for those who have made their enmity clear: they cannot enjoy the protection of the Muslim community while working for its downfall.
90 | except those who are allies of a people you are bound with in a treaty or those wholeheartedly opposed to fighting either you or their own people. If Allah had willed, He would have empowered them to fight you. So if they refrain from fighting you and offer you peace, then Allah does not permit you to harm them. | Immediately after the severe command, the verse carves out critical exceptions that demonstrate the justice and restraint inherent in Islamic law. “Except those who are allies of a people you are bound with in a treaty.” If a hypocrite or an enemy seeks refuge with a tribe that has a peace treaty with the Muslims, they are protected by the terms of that treaty. The sanctity of international agreements is upheld, even when dealing with internal enemies. The second exception is “those wholeheartedly opposed to fighting either you or their own people.” The Arabic ḥaṣirat ṣudūruhum beautifully conveys the image of hearts that are constricted, that feel an inner aversion and reluctance to engage in combat. There are individuals who are caught in a difficult position: they do not want to fight the Muslims, but they also do not want to fight their own tribe. They are psychologically torn and genuinely desire peace. Such people are not to be harmed. The verse then provides the theological reasoning: “If Allah had willed, He would have empowered them to fight you.” Allah could have given them the strength and the inclination to be your open enemies, but He did not. Therefore, you must not act as if they are enemies when Allah has not made them so. The concluding instruction is clear and merciful: “So if they refrain from fighting you and offer you peace, then Allah does not permit you to harm them.” The conditions for protection are simple: they stop fighting, and they extend peace. When that occurs, the believers have no divine permission to attack. This verse is a foundational text for the Islamic law of war, establishing that the default state is peace, that treaties must be honoured, and that those who genuinely desire peace must be granted it. It tempers the harshness of the preceding command with a broad and principled mercy.
91 | You will find others who wish to be safe from you and their own people. Yet they cannot resist the temptation ˹of disbelief or hostility˺. If they do not keep away, offer you peace, or refrain from attacking you, then seize them and kill them wherever you find them. We have given you full permission over such people. | This verse describes a third, more dangerous category of hypocrites and vacillators. “You will find others who wish to be safe from you and their own people.” These are the ultimate opportunists. When they are with the Muslims, they profess Islam to gain safety. When they are with their disbelieving tribes, they profess loyalty to them for the same reason. They are two-faced in the most literal sense, playing both sides for personal security and gain. The verse then exposes their fatal flaw: “Yet they cannot resist the temptation of disbelief or hostility.” The Arabic expression means that whenever fitnah (trial, temptation, or the call to revert to hostility) arises, they fall headlong into it. They lack the backbone to commit to peace. When their own tribe calls them to fight the Muslims, they join in. Their desire to be safe from everyone does not translate into a principled neutrality; it translates into treacherous oscillation. The verse then lays out the conditions for their immunity: “If they do not keep away, offer you peace, or refrain from attacking you.” Three things are required: they must physically withdraw from hostile action, they must extend a genuine offer of peace, and they must cease all aggression. If they fail to do all three—if they continue to dwell among you while maintaining ties with the enemy and participating in attacks—then the command is “seize them and kill them wherever you find them.” The final phrase, “We have given you full permission over such people,” grants a clear, divine mandate for dealing with this specific type of treacherous individual whose pattern of behaviour proves that they are combatants hiding behind a mask of neutrality. This verse, alongside the two before it, forms a nuanced legal framework. It is not a blanket licence but a highly specific ruling for a highly specific threat. It teaches that the Muslim community must be clear-eyed and firm in the face of persistent treachery, while remaining open to genuine peace.
92 | It is not lawful for a believer to kill another except by mistake. And whoever kills a believer unintentionally must free a believing slave and pay blood-money to the victim’s family—unless they waive it charitably. But if the victim is a believer from a hostile people, then a believing slave must be freed. And if the victim is from a people bound with you in a treaty, then blood-money must be paid to the family along with freeing a believing slave. Those who are unable, let them fast two consecutive months—as a means of repentance to Allah. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | Having addressed fighting against enemies and hypocrites, the surah now turns to the sanctity of life within the community and beyond. The opening statement is absolute: “It is not lawful for a believer to kill another except by mistake.” Intentional killing of a believer is an enormity that will be addressed in the next verse. This verse focuses on unintentional killing, or manslaughter. The phrase “except by mistake” acknowledges that such tragedies can occur, especially in the heat of battle or through accident. The verse then prescribes a detailed set of expiations and compensations. First, for the general case of killing a believer by mistake, the killer must “free a believing slave and pay blood-money to the victim’s family—unless they waive it charitably.” The freeing of a believing slave is an act of worship and atonement; it liberates a human soul from bondage. The blood-money (diyah) is a financial compensation to the victim’s family for their loss. The family has the right to waive this money as an act of charity, which is encouraged. Then, a specific case is addressed: “But if the victim is a believer from a hostile people.” This refers to a believer who lives among a tribe at war with the Muslims and has not emigrated. In such a case, the diyah is not paid to the hostile tribe, because that money could be used against the Muslims. The only expiation is the freeing of a believing slave. The third case: “And if the victim is from a people bound with you in a treaty, then blood-money must be paid to the family along with freeing a believing slave.” The treaty partner, even if not Muslim, is entitled to full compensation, demonstrating the sanctity of covenants. Finally, for the one who cannot afford to free a slave, an alternative is provided: “Those who are unable, let them fast two consecutive months—as a means of repentance to Allah.” This is a rigorous spiritual discipline. The verse closes by reminding that “Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise,” ensuring that these differing rulings are based on perfect knowledge of circumstances and perfect wisdom in their application. This comprehensive legal framework shows the immense value Islam places on human life and the seriousness with which it treats even unintentional harm.
93 | And whoever kills a believer intentionally, their reward will be Hell—where they will stay indefinitely. Allah will be displeased with them, condemn them, and will prepare for them a tremendous punishment. | This verse stands as one of the most severe warnings in the entire Qur’an. After the detailed discussion of unintentional killing in the previous verse, the text now confronts the horror of intentional murder. “And whoever kills a believer intentionally, their reward will be Hell—where they will stay indefinitely.” The word “reward” (jazāʾ) is used here with bitter irony; what awaits the murderer is not a reward but a recompense of the worst kind. The phrase “indefinitely” (khālidan fīhā) indicates an extremely long, protracted stay, though major scholarly discussion exists on whether a repentant murderer who dies as a believer will eventually be extracted from Hell by Allah’s mercy. The weight of evidence indicates that this verse applies to the one who kills a believer deeming that killing lawful or without repentance. But the warning itself is deliberately unqualified to instil the maximum deterrent effect. The verse then piles on further divine reactions: “Allah will be displeased with them.” This is the loss of divine pleasure, the opposite of the ultimate goal of every believer. “Condemn them” means they will be cursed and expelled from divine mercy. “And will prepare for them a tremendous punishment.” This is a description that leaves the magnitude unquantified, to be filled in by the imagination with the worst possible suffering. The severity of this verse is directly proportional to the sanctity of the life it protects. The life of a believer is a vessel of faith, a bearer of the knowledge of Allah, and its unjust destruction is an assault on what Allah loves most. The verse serves as an absolute barrier against the normalization of violence among believers. It also provides profound comfort to the families of victims: even if the murderer escapes human justice, he can never escape this divine promise. The verse is a permanent, thunderous declaration that the blood of the believers is sacred, and its spilling invites a wrath from which there is no refuge except through sincere repentance before death and, ultimately, Allah’s unfathomable mercy.
94 | O believers! When you struggle in the cause of Allah, be sure of who you fight. And do not say to those who offer you ˹greetings of˺ peace, “You are no believer!”—seeking a fleeting worldly gain. Instead, Allah has infinite bounties ˹in store˺. You were initially like them then Allah blessed you ˹with Islam˺. So be sure! Indeed, Allah is All-Aware of what you do. | This verse provides critical ethical guidelines for the conduct of battle, specifically addressing a tragic mistake that could occur. It begins by commanding caution: “O believers! When you struggle in the cause of Allah, be sure of who you fight.” Soldiers must exercise due diligence to identify their targets. Rashness and killing based on mere suspicion are forbidden. The immediate context, as narrated in authentic hadiths, involves an incident where a Muslim troop encountered a man herding sheep. The man uttered the shahādah or offered the greeting of peace, but one of the Muslims, assuming the man said it only out of fear for his life and wealth, killed him and took his flock. When the matter reached the Prophet, he was deeply distressed and the killer expressed regret. The verse rebukes this action: “And do not say to those who offer you greetings of peace, ‘You are no believer!’—seeking a fleeting worldly gain.” The outward expression of peace or the declaration of faith must be accepted at face value. The hidden state of the heart is known only to Allah; humans judge by what is apparent. The motivation exposed is the desire for booty, the “fleeting worldly gain.” The verse then redirects their desires: “Instead, Allah has infinite bounties in store.” The spoils of war are trivial compared to the rewards Allah can give.
