Then comes a humbling reminder: “You were initially like them then Allah blessed you with Islam.” The believers themselves were once disbelievers, or they were unknown to one another, and it was only through Allah’s grace that they entered Islam. They should not forget their own past and become arrogant. The verse closes with a double exhortation to caution and a warning: “So be sure! Indeed, Allah is All-Aware of what you do.” Every act of killing, every ignored greeting of peace, every hidden intention for wealth is fully known to Allah. This verse establishes the principle that the sanctity of life is not violated based on suspicion, and that a profession of faith or an offer of peace acts as a shield until proven otherwise.
95 | Those who stay at home—except those with valid excuses—are not equal to those who strive in the cause of Allah with their wealth and their lives. Allah has elevated in rank those who strive with their wealth and their lives above those who stay behind ˹with valid excuses˺. Allah has promised each a fine reward, but those who strive will receive a far better reward than others— | This verse deals with the immense disparity in rank and reward between those who strive in jihad and those who sit at home. It begins by establishing the inequality: “Those who stay at home—except those with valid excuses—are not equal to those who strive in the cause of Allah with their wealth and their lives.” The immediate context of revelation highlights the importance of the exception. When this verse was first revealed without the exception, a blind companion, Ibn Umm Maktūm, came to the Prophet and asked if his disability excused him. The verse was then supplemented with “except those with valid excuses” (ulī al-ḍarar), referring to the blind, the lame, the chronically ill, and those with genuine impediments. This inclusion is a hallmark of divine justice; it ensures that those who physically cannot fight are not spiritually diminished. The verse then declares: “Allah has elevated in rank those who strive with their wealth and their lives above those who stay behind with valid excuses.” The elevation is in rank (darajah), a matter of honour, spiritual station, and closeness to Allah. Striving with both wealth and life is the highest form of sacrifice. The verse then tempers any potential discouragement for those excused: “Allah has promised each a fine reward.” Even those who stay behind with valid excuses are promised ḥusnā, a beautiful reward, provided their faith and intentions are sound. However, “those who strive will receive a far better reward than others.” The Arabic emphasizes a tremendous, magnified reward. This verse creates a balanced psychology within the community: it motivates the able to strive and honours them for their sacrifice, while comforting and honouring the excused, assuring them that Allah’s mercy does not overlook their sincere disability. It establishes a hierarchy of merit based on effort and sacrifice, not on mere desire or abstract faith.
96 | far superior ranks, forgiveness, and mercy from Him. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | The previous verse mentioned the better reward; this verse details what that reward comprises. “Far superior ranks” translates darajātin minhu, which are elevated degrees of honour and closeness directly from Allah. These ranks are not merely nominal; they represent real, experienced levels of bliss, knowledge, and nearness to the Divine in Paradise. Then comes “forgiveness” (maghfirah). This is significant: the striver who sacrifices life and wealth is in need of forgiveness, because no human action is perfect or purely for Allah. The reward includes a comprehensive pardon for their shortcomings. “And mercy” (raḥmah) is the encompassing divine compassion that brings every good. The striver is enveloped in a special mercy that protects them in this world and the next. The verse then closes with the two divine names that make all of this possible: “And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” The juxtaposition is beautiful. The one who strives has shown immense effort and sacrifice, yet their entry into these ranks is not by their deeds alone but by the forgiveness and mercy of Allah. This eliminates any sense of self-righteousness. At the same time, the verse reassures the striver that the Lord they are sacrificing for is not a harsh taskmaster but the All-Forgiving, Most Merciful, who sees their effort and envelops them in His grace. For those who stayed behind with valid excuses, these names are also a comfort: the same Lord who exalted the strivers is the Lord who forgives and shows mercy to those who were unable. The entire section, from verses 95 to 96, masterfully balances the duty to strive with the reality of human limitation, all under the canopy of divine mercy. It motivates, honours, comforts, and points all believers—the strivers and the excused—back to the source of all reward: a Lord who is infinitely forgiving and merciful.
97 | When the angels seize the souls of those who have wronged themselves—scolding them, “What do you think you were doing?” they will reply, “We were oppressed in the land.” The angels will respond, “Was Allah’s earth not spacious enough for you to emigrate?” It is they who will have Hell as their home—what an evil destination! | This verse shifts to a terrifying scene of the angels of death confronting a specific category of wrongdoers at the moment of death. “When the angels seize the souls of those who have wronged themselves.” The “wrong” referred to is remaining in a state of surrender to a hostile, disbelieving environment and failing to emigrate when they had the ability. The angels scold them with a sharp question: “What do you think you were doing?” It is a question of accountability and rebuke. What was their state? What was their religious condition? The sinners try to offer an excuse: “They will reply, ‘We were oppressed in the land.’” They claim they were weak and unable to practice their faith freely, implying they had no choice. But the angels, who know the truth by divine permission, reject this excuse with a devastating question: “Was Allah’s earth not spacious enough for you to emigrate?” The earth is vast. If one land is oppressive, a believer is obligated to move to a land where they can worship Allah freely. The excuse of being “oppressed” collapses when emigration was a viable option. The verse then delivers the verdict: “It is they who will have Hell as their home—what an evil destination!” This is a stark and sobering judgment. The verse was revealed concerning a group of Muslims in Mecca who did not emigrate to Medina and were subsequently forced to fight alongside the disbelievers at Badr, some being killed. However, its principle is timeless. It places a profound responsibility on believers: when they cannot practice their faith in a particular place, they must strive to leave. Staying and passively accepting the erosion of one’s religion, when one has the means to move, is a form of wronging one’s own soul that can lead to a dreadful end. The verse simultaneously serves as a warning and a call to take agency for one’s spiritual well-being.
98 | Except helpless men, women, and children who cannot afford a way out— | The severity of the previous verse is immediately lightened by this crucial exception. “Except helpless men, women, and children who cannot afford a way out.” The Arabic mustaḍʿafīn paints a picture of those who are truly weak, whose strength and resources have been completely drained. The verse lists the most vulnerable categories: men who are old, infirm, or otherwise incapacitated; women who have no male guardian to facilitate their travel; and children who are entirely dependent. These individuals genuinely wish to emigrate but “cannot afford a way out.” The word ḥīlah means any stratagem, resource, or means. They lack the physical strength, the financial means, the knowledge of routes, or the support network to make the journey. Their helplessness is not feigned; it is real and inescapable. For them, the excuse of “We were oppressed” is valid because they exhausted any possible avenue of escape. This verse is a magnificent demonstration of divine justice and mercy. Allah does not hold people accountable for what is genuinely beyond their capacity. The principle, derived from this and similar verses, is that “Allah does not burden a soul beyond its scope.” While those who could emigrate but clung to their homes out of comfort or fear of losing their property are condemned, the truly powerless are granted full exemption. The verse ends without a predicate, leaving the sentence hanging, which in Arabic rhetoric draws the listener to reflect on what awaits these helpless ones. The next verse will complete the thought, revealing the hope and pardon that Allah extends to them. This exception serves as a profound comfort to all believers who find themselves in difficult circumstances from which they see no exit; their sincere struggle and helplessness are fully seen and will be fully compensated by a merciful Lord.
99 | it is right to hope that Allah will pardon them. For Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving. | This verse completes the exception begun in the previous verse, offering a message of immense hope. “It is right to hope that Allah will pardon them.” The phrase “it is right to hope” (fa-ulāʾika ʿasā Allāhu an yaʿfuwa ʿanhum) is a divine expression that leans strongly toward the certainty of forgiveness. When Allah Himself says “it is right to hope” for something, the hope is practically a promise. The helpless ones who could not emigrate are under the canopy of divine pardon (ʿafw), which means their sins related to their inability to fulfill the full obligations of hijrah will be erased and overlooked. The verse then grounds this pardon in two of Allah’s most beautiful names: “For Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving.” Al-ʿAfuww means the One who wipes away sins completely, as if they never existed. Al-Ghafūr means the One who covers and conceals sins, protecting the sinner from the consequences and disgrace. The pairing of these names assures the helpless that not only will they not be punished, but their record will be cleansed. This verse, along with the previous two, forms a comprehensive theology of responsibility, capacity, and mercy. It sets a high standard of obligation: believers must actively seek to live in an environment where they can practice their faith, and failure to do so when capable carries grave consequences. Yet it simultaneously wraps the incapable in a gentle and certain hope of divine forgiveness. The heart of the believer is thus suspended between a healthy fear that drives them to action and a profound hope that comforts them when action is impossible. This balance is at the core of Islamic spirituality.
100 | Whoever emigrates in the cause of Allah will find many safe havens and bountiful resources throughout the earth. Those who leave their homes and die while emigrating to Allah and His Messenger—their reward has already been secured with Allah. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | After the warning to those who refuse to emigrate and the exception for the helpless, this verse turns to glorify the act of emigration itself. “Whoever emigrates in the cause of Allah will find many safe havens and bountiful resources throughout the earth.” The Arabic murāghaman kathīran wa saʿah literally means many places of refuge and an expansive, abundant provision. The verse addresses the deep-seated human fear of leaving one’s homeland: the fear of being homeless, without resources, and at the mercy of strangers. Allah promises that the one who leaves for His sake will not be abandoned. The earth is vast, and Allah will open doors of safety and sustenance in unexpected places. This is both a practical observation and a spiritual guarantee. History testifies to it repeatedly: the early emigrants who left everything in Mecca found in Medina and Abyssinia a new life of dignity and provision. The verse then elevates the status of the emigrant further by addressing a specific, poignant scenario: “Those who leave their homes and die while emigrating to Allah and His Messenger—their reward has already been secured with Allah.” The journey of emigration was perilous; many died on the way from exhaustion, illness, or attacks. This verse declares that the moment a person steps out with the intention of reaching Allah and His Messenger, and death overtakes them before they arrive, their reward is already guaranteed and fixed. They are counted as having completed the hijrah. The phrase “their reward has already been secured” (waqaʿa ajruhu ʿalā Allāh) means it becomes an obligatory recompense upon Allah, out of His grace. The verse closes with the familiar and comforting “And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” This covers any shortcomings, any mixed intentions, and any sins that might have accompanied the emigrant. The entire verse is a ringing endorsement of sacrifice for faith, a comfort for those who fear the unknown, and a crown of honour for those who die in the pursuit of a life pleasing to Allah.
101 | When you travel through the land, it is permissible for you to shorten the prayer—˹especially˺ if you fear an attack by the disbelievers. Indeed, the disbelievers are your sworn enemies. | This verse introduces the important concession of shortening the prayer during travel. “When you travel through the land, it is permissible for you to shorten the prayer.” The Arabic word qaṣr means to reduce the four-rakʿah obligatory prayers—Ẓuhr, ʿAṣr, and ʿIshāʾ—to two rakʿahs. This is a merciful alleviation from Allah, making worship manageable during the hardships of journeying. The phrase “especially if you fear an attack by the disbelievers” is often understood as describing the most common circumstance of travel in that era, rather than a strict condition. The authentic Sunnah clarifies that shortening the prayer is a general concession for any legitimate travel, whether fear is present or not. The Prophet, peace be upon him, consistently shortened his prayers on all journeys, and when some companions remarked that they were now safe, he replied that it was a charity from Allah that they should accept. The explicit mention of fear, however, serves to reinforce the connection between prayer and divine protection. Even in a state of imminent danger, when the instinct might be to abandon all ritual, the believer is commanded to maintain prayer, albeit in a simplified form. The verse then adds, “Indeed, the disbelievers are your sworn enemies.” This statement is not a call to hatred of individuals but a sober assessment of the hostile environment the early Muslims faced. The idolaters of Quraysh and other tribes were actively seeking to destroy the nascent Muslim community. Recognizing this enmity explains the context of the rulings that follow regarding the prayer of fear. The believer must remain spiritually connected to Allah while also being acutely aware of worldly dangers. The concession of shortening prayer is a perfect example of how Islamic law balances the soul’s need for worship with the body’s need for safety and the practical realities of life. It demonstrates that Islam is not a religion of rigid burdens but one of dynamic, realistic guidance that bends with genuine hardship while always keeping the believer tied to the remembrance of Allah.
102 | When you ˹O Prophet˺ are ˹campaigning˺ with them and you lead them in prayer, let one group of them pray with you—while armed. When they prostrate themselves, let the other group stand guard behind them. Then the group that has not yet prayed will then join you in prayer—and let them be vigilant and armed. The disbelievers would wish to see you neglect your weapons and belongings, so they could launch a sweeping assault on you. But there is no blame if you lay aside your weapons when overcome by heavy rain or illness—but take precaution. Indeed, Allah has prepared a humiliating punishment for the disbelievers. | This verse details the specific manner of performing the “prayer of fear” (ṣalāt al-khawf), a unique form of congregational prayer in the midst of battle. The instruction is given directly to the Prophet: “When you O Prophet are campaigning with them and you lead them in prayer, let one group of them pray with you—while armed.” The army is divided. One contingent begins the prayer behind the Prophet while retaining their weapons, vigilant against any surprise attack. “When they prostrate themselves, let the other group stand guard behind them.” The first group completes a portion of the prayer, and as they move into the vulnerable position of prostration, the second group stands guard, facing the enemy, protecting the praying congregation. The verse then explains the rotation: “Then the group that has not yet prayed will then join you in prayer—and let them be vigilant and armed.” The second group now comes forward to perform their prayer with the Prophet, again carrying their weapons. The detailed variations of how this prayer is performed are documented in the authentic hadith, with different scenarios described for different battle conditions. The wisdom behind this rigorous discipline is then revealed: “The disbelievers would wish to see you neglect your weapons and belongings, so they could launch a sweeping assault on you.” The enemy constantly looks for a moment of distraction or vulnerability. If the entire army were to engage in prayer simultaneously and unarmed, it would present an irresistible target for a devastating ambush. The command to keep weapons during prayer is a practical military precaution. The verse then provides merciful exceptions: “But there is no blame if you lay aside your weapons when overcome by heavy rain or illness—but take precaution.” When circumstances make carrying weapons genuinely burdensome, such as drenching rain that soaks the gear or illness that weakens the body, the soldiers may put them aside, but they must still remain on high alert and take other precautions. The verse concludes by reminding the believers that the real enemy faces a far worse fate: “Indeed, Allah has prepared a humiliating punishment for the disbelievers.” This final note serves as a spiritual morale boost; the believers’ vigilance is undergirded by the knowledge that ultimate victory and justice belong to Allah.
103 | When the prayers are over, remember Allah—whether you are standing, sitting, or lying down. But when you are secure, establish regular prayers. Indeed, performing prayers is a duty on the believers at the appointed times. | After the intense, weapon-laden prayer of fear, this verse expands the believer’s spiritual horizon. “When the prayers are over, remember Allah—whether you are standing, sitting, or lying down.” The completion of the formal ritual prayer is not the end of worship. The believer is commanded to transition immediately into dhikr, the continuous remembrance of Allah with the tongue and heart. The three postures—standing, sitting, lying down—encompass all the normal human states. The instruction indicates that no physical condition is an obstacle to remembering Allah. A soldier standing guard after the battle prayer can do dhikr. A wounded soldier lying on the ground can move his lips in remembrance. This practice transforms the aftermath of a terrifying combat situation into an oasis of spiritual connection, calming the heart and reinforcing the purpose for which they fought. The verse then returns to the normal state of affairs: “But when you are secure, establish regular prayers.” The Arabic fa-idhā iṭmaʾnantum conveys a deep sense of settled security, when the fear has departed. In times of peace and safety, the believers are to return to the complete, established form of prayer (aqīmū al-ṣalāh), performing it with all its essential elements, tranquillity, and at its proper times, without the concessions of shortening or the battle formation. The verse then delivers a foundational principle: “Indeed, performing prayers is a duty on the believers at the appointed times.” The word “duty” (kitāban mawqūtan) indicates a written, fixed obligation, bound to specific times that must be observed. Prayer is not a voluntary act that can be performed whenever convenient; it is a timed prescription. This concluding sentence anchors the entire discussion. Even in war, prayer is not abandoned but adapted. Even in peace, it is not a loose suggestion but a disciplined, timed obligation. The verse seamlessly weaves together the flexibility of Islamic law in extremis and its unbending structure in normalcy, showing that the essence of the faith is constant connection to Allah, expressed appropriately according to circumstance.
104 | Do not falter in pursuit of the enemy—if you are suffering, they too are suffering. But you can hope to receive from Allah what they can never hope for. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | After the physical and spiritual exertions of battle and prayer, a natural human fatigue can set in. This verse addresses that state directly. “Do not falter in pursuit of the enemy.” The Arabic lā tahinū means do not weaken, do not lose your resolve, do not slacken in your effort to chase down and neutralise the threat they pose. The pursuit after a battle is often as critical as the battle itself to prevent the enemy from regrouping. The verse then provides a powerful psychological argument to combat the fatigue: “if you are suffering, they too are suffering.” The believers are not unique in their pain. The enemy is also exhausted, wounded, and demoralized. War inflicts suffering on both sides. Recognizing this creates a sense of shared burden; the believers are not being asked to endure what their adversaries are not also enduring. The difference, however, is monumental, and this is the heart of the verse: “But you can hope to receive from Allah what they can never hope for.” The believers have a source of strength, comfort, and motivation that is completely inaccessible to the disbelievers. They hope for Allah’s reward, His pleasure, forgiveness, and the eternal bliss of Paradise. They hope for divine help in this world and a meeting with their Lord in the next. The disbelievers, fighting for worldly gain or tribal pride, have no such hope. Their struggle ends in annihilation or a fleeting victory with no eternal consequence. This hope is a spiritual fuel that can overcome physical exhaustion. A believer can endure more because their horizon extends beyond the visible battlefield into the unseen realms of divine mercy. The verse closes with “And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.” He knows the true state of both armies, the fatigue in every heart, and the precise moment when perseverance will yield victory. His command to persist is not arbitrary; it is rooted in perfect wisdom that calculates what the soldiers on the ground may not see. This verse teaches the believer that in any struggle, the key differentiator is not the absence of hardship but the presence of a hope that transcends it.
105 | Indeed, We have sent down the Book to you ˹O Prophet˺ in truth to judge between people by means of what Allah has shown you. So do not be an advocate for the deceitful. | The surah now shifts to a specific historical incident that demonstrates the paramount importance of justice. “Indeed, We have sent down the Book to you O Prophet in truth.” The Qur’an is described as having been revealed bi al-ḥaqq, with truth, embodying truth, and for the purpose of truth. Its function is immediately stated: “to judge between people by means of what Allah has shown you.” The Prophet is not a mere reciter of the Book; he is an active judge, an arbiter who uses the divine revelation to resolve disputes and establish justice among humanity. The phrase “by means of what Allah has shown you” indicates that the Prophet’s judgments are not based on his personal opinion but on the divinely granted insight and the explicit rulings of the Qur’an. The verse then delivers a sharp, direct command: “So do not be an advocate for the deceitful.” This introduces the background of the revelation. A man from the Anṣār named Ṭuʿmah ibn Ubayriq stole a coat of mail from his neighbor and hid it with a Jewish man. When the theft was discovered and the evidence pointed toward Ṭuʿmah, his clan came to the Prophet to passionately defend him and argue that the Jewish man must be the thief. They sought to use the Prophet’s authority to falsely condemn an innocent person and absolve their guilty relative. The verse instructs the Prophet not to become a defender (khaṣīm) for those who are treacherous and deceptive. This is a timeless warning to all who sit in positions of judgment: the emotional pleas of family, the status of the accused, or the persuasive arguments of eloquent advocates must never sway a judge from the truth. The justice demanded by the Qur’an is blind to everything except the facts and the divine law. The verse condemns the very idea of a legal system where the powerful or the well-connected can manipulate the process to escape accountability.
106 | And seek Allah’s forgiveness—indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | This brief verse, placed immediately after the command not to advocate for the deceitful, holds a deep and multi-layered significance. “And seek Allah’s forgiveness.” The command to the Prophet to ask for forgiveness, despite his own infallibility from sin, is understood by scholars in several complementary ways. It serves as a constant act of humility and worship, acknowledging that all success in judgment and all guidance come from Allah alone. For the Prophet to seek forgiveness before his Lord is an expression of his absolute servitude and his recognition that ultimate perfection belongs only to Allah. It also serves as a protection: the matter of judgment is so delicate, and the risk of an unintended, minute error in the process—though not a sin for the Prophet—is met with a seeking of divine cover and pardon. Additionally, the verse acts as a subtle, powerful admonition to the believers who were advocating for the guilty Ṭuʿmah. While the Prophet is commanded to seek forgiveness as an act of devotion, those who were passionately defending a thief are in desperate need of actual repentance for their misguided efforts. Their sin requires them to turn back to Allah. The verse then reminds them of the divine attributes that make this possible: “indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” The door of repentance is wide open. Even the grave error of attempting to pervert justice can be washed away by sincere tawbah. This verse is a masterclass in spiritual leadership. It combines the leader’s personal devotion, the call for the community to correct its moral compass, and the overwhelming mercy of Allah as the solution to every failure. It reminds every judge, lawyer, and witness that they must constantly seek Allah’s forgiveness for the immense responsibility they bear and for any hidden biases that may cloud their pursuit of justice.
107 | Do not advocate for those who wrong themselves. Surely Allah does not like those who are deceitful, sinful. | The rebuke continues and intensifies, now broadening from the Prophet to the wider community. “Do not advocate for those who wrong themselves.” The act of stealing and then lying about it is a form of wronging one’s own soul (khāʾin), a self-inflicted spiritual harm. The verse explicitly forbids any form of advocacy, legal defense, or intercession that is aimed at covering up the guilt of such a person. The clan of Ṭuʿmah came to the Prophet to argue his case, presenting their defense with passion and perhaps with oaths. This verse tells them and all believers that such advocacy is itself a sin. To defend someone you know to be guilty, or to actively work to shift the blame onto an innocent person, is to participate in the wrongdoing. The principle is that justice demands standing with the truth, not with one’s kin or tribe. The verse then provides the reason for this prohibition by revealing Allah’s attitude toward such people: “Surely Allah does not like those who are deceitful, sinful.” The description applies both to the one who committed the original theft (the deceitful, sinful) and to those who help him in his deception. Allah’s love and pleasure are removed from a person who makes a practice of betraying trusts and then compounding that betrayal with lies and manipulation. This is a powerful moral deterrent. In a tribal society where loyalty to one’s clan was considered the highest virtue, this verse was revolutionary. It reorients loyalty from the tribe to the truth. Your brother is not the one who stands with you when you are an oppressor; your brother is the one who restrains you from oppression and insists that you face the consequences of your actions, thereby saving your soul in the Hereafter. The verse teaches that misplaced advocacy, rooted in tribalism, is not kindness but a form of complicity in sin that earns divine displeasure.
108 | They try to hide ˹their deception˺ from people, but they can never hide it from Allah—in Whose presence they plot by night what is displeasing to Him. And Allah is Fully Aware of what they do. | The previous verse condemned advocacy for the deceitful. This verse now strips away the layers of their secrecy and exposes their psychology. “They try to hide their deception from people.” The thief and his defenders worked in the shadows, planting the stolen item with an innocent Jewish man, fabricating a story, and hoping their scheme would go undetected by the community. They felt shame before human eyes and feared the social consequences of being discovered. They took elaborate precautions to maintain their reputation among their peers. “But they can never hide it from Allah.” This is the devastating truth that their scheming minds ignored. While they were so concerned with their public image, they forgot the gaze that penetrates every darkness. They can deceive a prophet, they can deceive a community, but they cannot deceive the All-Seeing. The verse then paints a vivid picture of their nocturnal scheming: “in Whose presence they plot by night what is displeasing to Him.” The night, which they thought provided cover, is actually the very time when they are directly in the presence of Allah, weaving plans that anger Him. The phrase “in Whose presence” is profoundly ironic. They think they are alone, but they are in the court of the Almighty, where their every whisper is recorded. The verse concludes with a comprehensive statement: “And Allah is Fully Aware of what they do.” The Arabic muḥīṭ conveys the image of complete, encircling knowledge. Nothing of their plot falls outside His awareness: the initial theft, the planting of evidence, the false testimony, the passionate defense. This verse serves as a powerful corrective to the human tendency to fear public exposure more than divine observation. It teaches the believer that true integrity is not merely about maintaining a clean image before people but about being profoundly aware that every act, especially those done in secret, is witnessed by the One whose judgment truly matters.
109 | Here you are! You ˹believers˺ are advocating for them in this life, but who will ˹dare to˺ advocate for them before Allah on the Day of Judgment? Or who will come to their defence? | Having exposed the hypocrisy of the deceitful, the verse now directly addresses those believers who were misled into defending the guilty party. “Here you are! You believers are advocating for them in this life.” The tone is one of strong reproach. It is as if Allah is saying, “Look at what you are doing.” In this temporary, fleeting world, they have taken up the role of lawyers, passionately arguing the case of the guilty, trying to secure a favorable human verdict. But the verse immediately shatters the myopia of their efforts with two devastating rhetorical questions. The first: “but who will dare to advocate for them before Allah on the Day of Judgment?” In the divine court, the rules are completely different. There, no eloquent argument, no false oath, no tribal pressure will avail. The advocates who were so bold in the Prophet’s assembly will be utterly silent and powerless. Who will stand up and plead their case before the Judge of all judges? The implied answer is: no one. The second question intensifies the sense of abandonment: “Or who will come to their defence?” The word wakīl means a guardian, a protector, a legal representative who takes charge of one’s affairs. On that Day, every soul will stand alone, naked before its Lord, with only its deeds as its companion. The clan members who defended the thief in this world will be of no help; they will be fleeing from one another, each consumed by their own reckoning. The verse essentially tells the misguided advocates that their worldly defense is not only sinful but also catastrophically short-sighted. By helping the guilty escape earthly justice, they are enabling him to carry his unrepented sin to a court where no human advocacy can save him. True love and true loyalty would have been to guide him to confession, repentance, and making amends, thereby lightening his burden on that inevitable Day. This verse is a timeless call to weigh every worldly action against its eternal consequence.
110 | Whoever commits evil or wrongs themselves then seeks Allah’s forgiveness will certainly find Allah All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | After the severe warning to the advocates and the guilty, the surah now opens the door of mercy wide. The previous verses described a web of theft, deceit, and false accusation, a truly dark set of actions. A sinner hearing those verses might feel that they are beyond hope. This verse provides the immediate remedy. “Whoever commits evil or wrongs themselves then seeks Allah’s forgiveness will certainly find Allah All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” The verse uses a general formula, covering anyone who does any kind of evil (sūʾ) or who commits any act of self-wronging (ẓulm al-nafs). The path back is clearly marked: “seeks Allah’s forgiveness.” This seeking (yastaghfir) involves sincere regret in the heart, a cessation of the sin, a firm resolve not to return to it, and the verbal supplication for pardon. It is not a passive wish but an active turning. The result is stated with absolute certainty: he “will certainly find Allah All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” The Arabic la-yajidanna emphasizes the definite, experiential nature of this discovery. The repentant sinner does not just hope; they find Allah’s forgiveness and mercy as a tangible reality. The two names Ghafūr and Raḥīm encompass the complete erasure of the sin and the subsequent bestowal of blessings. This verse is a direct application to the case of Ṭuʿmah ibn Ubayriq and his defenders. If Ṭuʿmah had confessed his theft, returned the stolen item, and sincerely repented, he would have found Allah forgiving and merciful. The path was not closed to him. The tragedy was that he chose instead to compound his theft with lies and deception, and his clan chose to support his falsehood, thereby shutting themselves out from the mercy described in this verse. The lesson for all believers is that no sin, however grave, places a person beyond the reach of divine pardon, provided they turn back sincerely. The verse is a beacon of hope in the midst of a passage that deals with some of the ugliest human behavior, reminding everyone that the mercy of Allah is vaster than any transgression.
111 | And whoever commits a sin—it is only to their own loss. Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | This verse follows the promise of forgiveness with a fundamental principle of moral accountability. “And whoever commits a sin—it is only to their own loss.” The Arabic verb yaksib means to earn or to acquire. The sinner is engaged in a terrible transaction: they are “earning” a sin, and that earning works only “against their own soul” (ʿalā nafsihi). The consequences of sin are entirely self-contained. A sin does not harm Allah in the slightest; He is Self-Sufficient, above all need, and immune to any damage. It does not ultimately harm the angels, the prophets, or the universe. The damage, both spiritual and potentially physical, recoils entirely upon the perpetrator. This is a profound psychological and theological insight. When a person steals, as in the incident with Ṭuʿmah, they think they are gaining a piece of wealth. But in reality, they are accumulating a burden that will weigh down their own soul on the Day of Judgment, a debt that will have to be repaid from their own good deeds. The immediate material gain is an illusion; the real loss is to the self. The verse then closes with “Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.” He knows the true nature and full extent of every sin, even those aspects hidden from the sinner’s own consciousness. His wisdom is manifest in the very system of accountability He has established. He does not forbid sins out of a tyrannical desire to restrict human pleasure; He forbids them because they are, by their very nature, harmful to the human soul that commits them. The prohibition is a mercy, and the punishment is a just consequence. This verse teaches the believer to reframe their understanding of sin. It is not merely the breaking of an arbitrary rule; it is an act of self-sabotage. This realization fosters a deeper, more internalized motivation to avoid sin: not just fear of external punishment, but a protective love for one’s own soul and a desire not to earn what will only bring oneself loss in the truest and most lasting sense.
112 | And whoever commits an evil or sinful deed then blames it on an innocent person, they will definitely bear the guilt of slander and blatant sin. | This verse captures the exact nature of the crime committed in the incident of the stolen coat of mail. “And whoever commits an evil or sinful deed then blames it on an innocent person.” The phrase “evil or sinful deed” (khaṭīʾatan aw ithman) can refer to a mistake or a deliberate sin. The person first commits the original act—the theft, the betrayal, the wrongdoing. Then, instead of owning it and repenting, they take a further, more sinister step: they “blame it on an innocent person.” They transfer the accusation to someone who is entirely blameless (barīʾ). This is precisely what Ṭuʿmah did: he stole the armor and then, with the help of his clan, accused a Jewish man of the theft. The verse then declares the double punishment for this double crime: “they will definitely bear the guilt of slander and blatant sin.” The word buhtān means a false accusation, a slander that is particularly outrageous because it involves a complete fabrication. The “blatant sin” (ithman mubīn) is the compounded nature of the entire act, a sin that is clear and manifest in its ugliness. The perpetrator is not only carrying the burden of the original theft but also the burden of falsely accusing an innocent person, a crime that destroys reputations, divides communities, and perverts the course of justice. The verse emphasizes that the attempt to deflect blame does not remove the original guilt; it adds a new, heavier layer of guilt. The liar is crushed under a double load. This teaching is crucial for social justice. It warns that the legal and moral system takes the crime of false accusation with the utmost seriousness. It also serves as a powerful deterrent: if you have committed a sin, do not dig yourself deeper by dragging an innocent person into it. The only way out is the path of verse 110: repentance, not blame-shifting. The verse is a timeless condemnation of scapegoating and a guarantee that the innocent will ultimately be vindicated, and the slanderer will be fully exposed and punished.
113 | Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy, a group of them would have sought to deceive you ˹O Prophet˺. Yet they would deceive none but themselves, nor can they harm you in the least. Allah has revealed to you the Book and wisdom and taught you what you never knew. Great ˹indeed˺ is Allah’s favour upon you! | This verse addresses the Prophet directly, highlighting the immense divine favour that protected him from being misled by the scheming hypocrites. The specific reference is to the clan of Ṭuʿmah, who came with a carefully constructed, emotionally charged argument to convince the Prophet that their man was innocent and the Jewish man was guilty. From a purely human perspective, their case might have seemed plausible. The verse states, “Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy, a group of them would have sought to deceive you O Prophet.” They “sought to deceive” (hammat ṭāʾifatun minhum an yuḍillūka), meaning they had the intention and made the attempt. But Allah’s grace intervened. Allah revealed the truth of the matter to the Prophet, exposing the real thief. “Yet they would deceive none but themselves, nor can they harm you in the least.” Their attempt to deceive the Prophet was, in the end, a failure that only deepened their own spiritual ruin. They could not cause him to make a wrong judgment, and they could not diminish his prophethood or his standing with Allah. The verse then widens the scope of divine favour: “Allah has revealed to you the Book and wisdom and taught you what you never knew.” This is a magnificent enumeration of gifts. The Book (the Qur’an) provides the ultimate criterion for judgment. The wisdom (al-ḥikmah) is the profound understanding and the Sunnah that enables correct application. And beyond the knowledge contained in revelation, Allah directly taught the Prophet, through inspiration and experience, things he could never have known by his own human effort. The verse concludes with an exclamation: “Great indeed is Allah’s favour upon you!” This immense favour is the reason the Prophet was protected. The verse teaches the believers that the immunity of the Prophet from error in conveying the message and judging is not his own achievement but a pure gift from Allah. It also assures them that the truth will always prevail, no matter how clever the deception, because Allah’s grace is with His Messenger and the truth He revealed.
114 | There is no good in most of their secret talks—except those encouraging charity, kindness, or reconciliation between people. And whoever does this seeking Allah’s pleasure, We will grant them a great reward. | The surah now pivots from the specific conspiracy of the hypocrites to a general principle about private conversations. “There is no good in most of their secret talks.” The pronoun “their” refers to people in general, or specifically to the type of people who engage in the secret scheming described earlier. The statement is a sweeping moral observation: the majority of whispered conferences, hidden conversations, and private meetings among people are devoid of real goodness. They are often filled with gossip, plotting harm, vanity, or idle talk. However, the verse immediately establishes three shining exceptions. “Except those encouraging charity.” When people gather privately to plan or encourage acts of ṣadaqah—charitable giving to the needy—their secret talk is blessed. “Kindness” (maʿrūf) covers every recognized act of goodness and decency. “Or reconciliation between people.” The third exception is iṣlāḥ, making peace between estranged parties, a deed so noble that certain rules about truth-telling are even relaxed to allow for conciliatory speech that bridges hearts. These three exceptions share the quality of being other-focused, altruistic, and aimed at increasing good and decreasing harm in the community. The verse then adds the crucial condition for the reward: “And whoever does this seeking Allah’s pleasure, We will grant them a great reward.” The outward act, however good, is only valuable if the intention behind it is purely for Allah’s pleasure. If one gives charity to be seen, or reconciles people for worldly status, the deed loses its spiritual merit. This verse provides a comprehensive ethical filter for the believer’s social life. When you find yourself in a private conversation, you are to ask: is this talk encouraging charity, promoting good, or mending relationships? If so, and if your intention is pure, it is a gathering surrounded by angels and divine reward. If not, it is likely a waste of time or a source of sin. The verse transforms the way Muslims view their social interactions, making them conscious architects of their speech, ensuring that even their most private moments are oriented towards what pleases Allah and benefits His creation.
115 | And whoever defies the Messenger after guidance has become clear to them and follows a path other than that of the believers, We will let them pursue what they have chosen, then burn them in Hell—what an evil end! | Following the praise of private talks that encourage good, this verse delivers a severe warning against the opposite: rebellious defiance of the Messenger. “And whoever defies the Messenger after guidance has become clear to them.” The word yushāqiq comes from shiqāq, meaning to oppose, to split away, to be in a faction against. The condition is that this defiance occurs “after guidance has become clear.” This refers to someone who has received the message of Islam, understood it, and had no legitimate doubt about its truth, yet still actively opposes the Prophet and sets themselves in a camp hostile to him. The second part of the description is “and follows a path other than that of the believers.” This is the practical manifestation of defiance. The “path of the believers” is the agreed-upon way of the Ahl al-Sunnah wa al-Jamāʿah, the main body of Muslims who adhere to the Qur’an and Sunnah as understood and practiced by the Companions. To abandon this path for a deviant sect, a heretical ideology, or a personal whim is a grave crime. The verse then announces the divine response to such a person: “We will let them pursue what they have chosen.” This is the most terrifying aspect of the punishment: istidrāj, being given over to one’s own choices. Allah does not force them back; He gives them the rope to continue on their path of misguidance, allowing their worldly life to proceed, while their spiritual doom is being sealed. Then comes the final outcome: “then burn them in Hell—what an evil end!” The sequence is chilling: they chose their path, Allah enabled them to go further, and the destination is the Fire. This verse underscores the gravity of unity within the Muslim community and the perilous nature of defying the consensus and clear teachings of the Messenger. It is not merely a personal opinion; it is a schism that leads to destruction. The verse serves as a powerful safeguard for orthodoxy and communal harmony, directing all believers to cling to the clear guidance and the collective path of the faithful.
116 | Surely Allah does not forgive associating ˹others˺ with Him ˹in worship˺, but forgives anything else of whoever He wills. Indeed, whoever associates ˹others˺ with Allah has clearly gone far astray. | This verse is a repeated and emphatic statement of a core theological truth that the surah previously declared in verse 48. Its repetition here is significant, as it follows the discussion of defying the Messenger and following other paths. “Surely Allah does not forgive associating others with Him in worship.” The particle “inna” adds strong emphasis. Shirk, the crime of assigning partners, equals, or intermediaries to Allah in His exclusive rights of worship, lordship, or names and attributes, is the single unforgivable sin for the one who dies without repenting from it. The reason is metaphysical: shirk is a violation of the very purpose of creation, which is to recognize and worship the One God. It is an infinite injustice because it directs what is owed to the Infinite Creator to finite creatures. “But forgives anything else of whoever He wills.” Every other sin, regardless of its magnitude—from murder to theft to slander—can be forgiven by Allah’s will. This forgiveness may come through sincere repentance or purely through His boundless mercy on the Day of Judgment. This clause opens an unimaginably vast door of hope for every sinner. The verse then reiterates: “Indeed, whoever associates others with Allah has clearly gone far astray.” The phrase “far astray” (ḍalālan baʿīdan) indicates a misguidance that is profound and distant from the truth. The repetition of this truth in the context of the surah’s discussions about hypocrisy, legal disputes, and factionalism suggests a deep connection: the root of all deviation, whether it is the conspiracy of the hypocrites, the theft of the armor, or the defiance of the Messenger, can often be traced back to a heart that has not fully established pure tawḥīd. The verse serves as a constant call to examine the purity of one’s faith and to recognize that while all other sins are eligible for mercy, the preservation of pure monotheism is the absolute priority. It is the foundation upon which the entire edifice of Islam stands.
117 | Instead of Allah, they only invoke female gods and they ˹actually˺ invoke none but a rebellious Satan— | The surah now turns to expose the reality of idolatrous worship. “Instead of Allah, they only invoke female gods.” The Arabic in yadʿūna min dūnihi illā ināthan refers to the pagan Arabs’ practice of worshipping goddesses like al-Lāt, al-ʿUzzā, and Manāt, whom they considered daughters of Allah, as mentioned in Sūrat al-Najm. The verse highlights the peculiar irony: the polytheists, who despised female offspring for themselves and often buried infant girls alive, assigned females as divine beings to Allah. This exposes the inconsistency and corruption of their beliefs. The verse then shatters any remaining illusion that these deities have any power: “and they actually invoke none but a rebellious Satan.” The particle “in” (translated as “actually”) emphasizes the stark, hidden reality. Despite the pagans’ belief that they are worshipping divine intermediaries, the true object of their invocation is Satan. It is Satan who whispers to them, beautifies shirk for them, and leads them to direct their worship away from Allah. The word “rebellious” (marīdan) describes Satan as one who is utterly disobedient, stripped of all good, and intensely hostile to the human race. The verse makes a profound theological point: there is no neutral ground in worship. A human being will either worship Allah or, directly or indirectly, fall into the worship of Satan. Any act of devotion directed to other than Allah is, in its essence, obedience to the devil who commanded and inspired it. This revelation was shocking to the pagan Arabs, who saw themselves as noble idol-worshippers. It is equally shocking today for those who may not bow to physical idols but direct their ultimate love, fear, and obedience to ideologies, desires, or human authorities that are in rebellion against Allah. The verse strips away the respectable veneer of idolatry and names its true master: a cursed and rebellious enemy of mankind.
118 | cursed by Allah—who said, “I will surely take hold of a certain number of Your servants. | The description of Satan continues, adding further details about his status and his declared mission. “Cursed by Allah” (laʿanahu Allāh) means that Allah has expelled him from His mercy, condemned him, and designated him as an accursed being. This is the state of the entity whom the polytheists are ultimately serving. The verse then quotes a statement Satan made to Allah, a declaration of war against humanity: “who said, ‘I will surely take hold of a certain number of Your servants.’” The words “I will surely take hold” (la-attakhidhanna) convey a determined, oath-bound resolution. Satan has pledged to capture, possess, and claim a specific, apportioned share (naṣīban mafrūḍan) of Allah’s servants for himself. This “certain number” refers to those who will respond to his call and follow his path to their own destruction. This is not an absolute power; Satan cannot take anyone by force. The Qur’an elsewhere confirms that he has no authority over the sincere, believing servants of Allah. His method is invitation and enticement. The “certain number” are those who freely choose to accept his invitation. The verse reveals the ancient enmity and the calculated, strategic nature of Satan’s mission. He is not randomly tempting; he is working to build his own cohort, his own following, to take them with him into the Fire. This verse should awaken a deep sense of vigilance in the believer. It identifies the enemy, his goal, and his method. The believer is in a tug-of-war between the call of Allah’s messengers and the call of Satan. Every sin, every act of shirk, every moment of heedlessness is a step toward becoming part of that “certain number.” The verse makes the abstract concept of Satanic temptation intensely personal and urgent. It is a call to consciously choose which group one will belong to: the servants of the Most Merciful or the share claimed by the Accursed.
119 | I will certainly mislead them and delude them with empty hopes. Also, I will order them and they will slit the ears of cattle and alter Allah’s creation.” And whoever takes Satan as a guardian instead of Allah has certainly suffered a tremendous loss. | Satan’s boastful and threatening speech continues, detailing his methods of corruption. “I will certainly mislead them.” This is the overarching goal: to divert humanity from the straight path of tawḥīd and obedience. “And delude them with empty hopes” (wa la-umanniyannahum). He will fill their minds with false, distant hopes—the hope that life is long, that repentance can always be delayed, that Allah’s mercy will forgive them without any effort on their part, that the afterlife is a myth. These empty hopes are a powerful tool of procrastination and spiritual complacency that keeps people from taking their salvation seriously. Satan then moves to more specific, tangible forms of corruption: “Also, I will order them and they will slit the ears of cattle.” This refers to the pagan Arab practice of slitting the ears of certain animals as a mark to dedicate them to idols, a superstitious mutilation. “And alter Allah’s creation.” This is a sweeping statement that has been interpreted by scholars to cover a range of forbidden alterations. Some understood it as physical mutilation, such as tattooing, filing teeth, or castration for non-medical reasons. Others included the alteration of the natural disposition (fiṭrah) upon which Allah created humans, by changing the religion of truth into falsehood and corrupting the innate human sense of right and wrong. The verse does not refer to beneficial or necessary changes, but to alterations driven by satanic inspiration that deface the natural order. Then comes the devastating conclusion: “And whoever takes Satan as a guardian instead of Allah has certainly suffered a tremendous loss.” To obey Satan, to follow his commands, and to make him one’s protector and guide is to abandon the guardianship of Allah, the All-Powerful, the All-Merciful. The trade is catastrophic: exchanging an infinitely benevolent Guardian for a malevolent deceiver. The “tremendous loss” (khusrānan mubīnan) is the loss of this world’s true purpose and the eternal bliss of the Hereafter. The verse is a comprehensive exposé of Satan’s strategy and the inevitable outcome for those who fall for it.
120 | Satan only makes them ˹false˺ promises and deludes them with ˹empty˺ hopes. Truly Satan promises them nothing but delusion. | The surah concludes this section on Satanic deception with a verse that serves as both a warning and a reassurance. “Satan only makes them false promises and deludes them with empty hopes.” The previous verse quoted Satan’s own words; this verse is Allah’s definitive evaluation of those promises. Every beautiful promise that Satan whispers—of pleasure without consequence, of wealth gained through dishonesty, of power without accountability, of a long life where repentance can be postponed—is a lie. The word yurīhim, translated as “empty hopes,” suggests that Satan shows people mirages, beautiful visions that have no reality. He makes a poor man dream of riches through gambling; he makes a young person feel invincible to the harms of intoxicants; he makes a sinner feel that his sin is too small to matter. All of it is a theatrical production, a show with no substance. The verse then delivers the final, absolute truth: “Truly Satan promises them nothing but delusion.” The Arabic ghurūr is derived from gharra, meaning to deceive, to delude, to cheat. Satan’s entire program is a grand deception. He has no power to actually fulfill any beneficial promise. He cannot protect from death, he cannot provide real sustenance, he cannot intercede on the Day of Judgment. His promises are like the promises of a con artist to his victim: alluring on the surface, but leading only to ruin. This verse empowers the believer with clarity. Once you understand that the enemy’s promises are intrinsically empty, you can resist them. The fleeting pleasure of sin is compared to the eternal consequence, and the equation becomes clear: you are being cheated. The verse also implicitly contrasts Satan’s false promises with the promises of Allah. Allah’s promises are absolutely true; they are backed by His eternal word and His boundless power. Paradise is real, forgiveness is real, and the reward for patience is real. The believer’s task is to choose which set of promises to trust: the delusive mirages of the enemy, or the certain, magnificent truth of the Creator.
121 | It is they who will have Hell as their home, and they will find no escape from it! | The previous verse concluded that Satan’s promises amount to nothing but delusion. This verse now states the final destination of those who fall for that delusion. “It is they who will have Hell as their home.” The demonstrative pronoun “they” points back to those who took Satan as a guardian instead of Allah, those who followed his misguidance, fell for his empty hopes, and participated in the alteration of Allah’s creation. Their “home” (maʾwā) is not a temporary lodging but a permanent abode. The word maʾwā conveys the idea of a refuge or a dwelling place, a bitter irony because Hell is the most horrific refuge imaginable. They sought shelter and protection in Satan; the shelter they receive is the Fire. The second part of the verse intensifies the horror: “and they will find no escape from it.” The Arabic maḥīṣan means a place to flee to, a way out, an exit. In this world, a person suffering in a difficult situation can often hope for a way out—whether through a change of circumstances, the help of a friend, or eventually death. But in Hell, this hope does not exist. The inhabitants will desire to die, but they will not die. They will try to escape, and every time they attempt to flee, they will be pushed back. The walls of their eternal prison are impenetrable. This verse strips away the final illusion that one can indulge in Satanic temptations and then somehow slip away from the consequences. The choice in this world is stark: make the Qur’an and the Messenger your guide and find Paradise as your home, or make Satan your guide and find Hell as your inescapable dwelling. The verse is a powerful conclusion to the long passage on Satanic deception, leaving the reader with a chilling image of eternal confinement that serves as a potent deterrent against the path of misguidance.
122 | And those who believe and do good, We will soon admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever. Allah’s promise is ˹always˺ true. And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s? | Having described the fate of the followers of Satan, the surah immediately balances the warning with the beautiful promise for the believers. “And those who believe and do good” combines the two essential elements of salvation: genuine faith in the heart and righteous actions that flow from that faith. The verse does not say “those who claim to believe” but “those who believe,” indicating a faith that has taken root in the heart, and it couples it with the tangible evidence of good works. The reward is described with the familiar, beloved Qur’anic image: “We will soon admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever.” The phrase “Gardens under which rivers flow” evokes a picture of lush, eternal orchards where streams glide beneath the trees, providing effortless sustenance and serene beauty. The permanence is emphasised by the double expression “for ever and ever” (khālidīna fīhā abadan), leaving no room for any fear that the bliss will end. This is the direct contrast to the inescapable Hell of the previous verse. The verse then anchors this promise in the most certain of foundations: “Allah’s promise is always true.” The Arabic waʿd Allāhi ḥaqqan means that this is not a possibility or a probability; it is a binding, truthful commitment from the Lord of the universe. To drive the point home, the verse poses a rhetorical question: “And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s?” The question challenges the reader to reflect: if you accept that Allah is the most truthful of all speakers, then the promise of Paradise for the believers must be the most certain fact in existence, more certain even than the ground beneath your feet. This verse, by juxtaposing the home of the disbelievers with the home of the believers, creates a powerful motivational structure that appeals to both the human desire for eternal joy and the human aversion to eternal suffering, channelling both toward the path of belief and righteousness.
123 | ˹Divine grace is˺ neither by your wishes nor those of the People of the Book! Whoever commits evil will be rewarded accordingly, and they will find no protector or helper besides Allah. | This verse corrects a fundamental misconception about salvation that was prevalent among various religious communities. “Divine grace is neither by your wishes nor those of the People of the Book!” The Muslims are addressed first, then the Jews and Christians. Some Muslims might have begun to think that their mere identity as followers of Muhammad was a guarantee of Paradise. Similarly, the Jews and Christians claimed that they were the chosen people and the beloved of God, and that only they would enter Paradise. The verse demolishes all such complacent assumptions. Salvation is not a matter of wishful thinking, group identity, or self-congratulatory claims. It is not enough to simply say, “We are believers” or “We are the community of the Prophet.” The second part of the verse states the actual criterion of judgment: “Whoever commits evil will be rewarded accordingly.” The Arabic word man yaʿmal sūʾan is universal; it applies to any human being, regardless of their label or community. The principle is that actions have consequences. If a person commits evil, they will face the recompense for that evil. This does not contradict the verses about divine forgiveness; rather, it establishes the default principle of justice. Without repentance and divine mercy, evil deeds earn punishment. The verse then adds: “and they will find no protector or helper besides Allah.” On the Day of Judgment, the false protectors and intercessors that people relied upon in this world—whether saints, priests, ancestors, or tribal affiliations—will vanish. No one will be able to shield the wrongdoer from the consequences of their deeds. The only true protector is Allah, and He protects those who approached Him with faith and righteousness, not those who merely claimed a title. This verse is a profound call to personal responsibility. It demolishes the walls of religious tribalism and forces every individual to confront their own deeds. It teaches that the scales of divine justice are perfectly balanced and that no amount of wishful thinking or group belonging can tip them in one’s favour.
124 | But those who do good—whether male or female—and have faith will enter Paradise and will never be wronged ˹even as much as˺ the speck on a date stone. | The previous verse stated the principle of justice for evildoers. This verse complements it with the promise of justice and reward for the doers of good. “But those who do good—whether male or female—and have faith will enter Paradise.” The verse explicitly mentions both genders, a recurring Qur’anic emphasis that in the sight of Allah, spiritual merit is not determined by gender. A woman’s good deeds are as valuable and rewarded as a man’s. The condition is twofold: “do good” and “have faith.” Good works without faith, such as acts of charity done for show or out of mere humanistic impulse without belief in Allah, do not earn eternal salvation. Faith without good works is also incomplete. The combination of sound belief and righteous action is the key. The reward is stated simply and gloriously: they “will enter Paradise.” The verse then guarantees the absolute fairness of this reward: “and will never be wronged even as much as the speck on a date stone.” The “speck” (naqīr) is the tiny indentation on the back of a date pit, an image of the smallest conceivable quantity. No one’s good deeds will be diminished by even that minuscule amount. The reward will be perfectly proportional to the faith and deeds of the individual. This verse is deeply comforting to every believer who strives to do good. It assures them that no effort, however small, is lost. The widow’s quiet charity, the struggling father’s honest work, the young person’s struggle against desire—every atom of good is recorded and will be rewarded without any reduction. The verse is also a powerful statement of gender equality in the spiritual realm. Men and women are equally eligible for the highest ranks of Paradise, judged by the exact same standard. This elevates the role of women in an era and region where they were often considered spiritually inferior or incapable of attaining salvation.
125 | And who is better in faith than those who ˹fully˺ submit themselves to Allah, do good, and follow the Way of Abraham, the upright? Allah chose Abraham as a close friend. | The surah now presents the ultimate model of faith, answering the rhetorical question with a beautiful description. “And who is better in faith than those who fully submit themselves to Allah.” The word aslama, from which Islam is derived, means to surrender one’s entire being, will, and desires to the Creator. This is not a partial or grudging submission but a complete, wholehearted yielding. The verse continues: “do good,” again pairing submission with righteous action. Then comes a third, profoundly significant element: “and follow the Way of Abraham, the upright.” The millah of Abraham is presented as the pure, original monotheism that preceded the divisions of Judaism and Christianity. Abraham is described as ḥanīfan, the one who turned away from all falsehood and idolatry to devote himself exclusively to the one true God. The Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him, was commanded to follow this same millah, and his followers are likewise instructed to do so. This establishes a direct spiritual lineage that bypasses the corrupted religious institutions of later times. The verse then declares the unique and exalted status of Abraham: “Allah chose Abraham as a close friend.” The Arabic khalīl is a word of profound intimacy. A khalīl is a friend whose love and companionship penetrate the innermost recesses of the heart. Allah took Abraham as His khalīl, a title that no other prophet shares in the same explicit manner. This friendship was the result of Abraham’s unparalleled submission, his willingness to sacrifice his son, his readiness to be thrown into the fire, and his unwavering devotion. The verse thus presents the complete picture of the best faith: a full internal surrender to Allah, manifested in righteous deeds, walking in the footsteps of the father of monotheism, Abraham, the intimate friend of God. This is the standard against which all claims of faith are measured.
126 | To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is Fully Aware of everything. | Following the exaltation of Abraham’s pure faith, the verse re-centers the discussion on the absolute sovereignty and knowledge of Allah. “To Allah alone belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth.” This is a comprehensive declaration of ownership. Everything in the celestial realms—the stars, the angels, the unseen worlds—and everything on the earth—the mountains, the oceans, the animals, and all human beings—is the possession of Allah. No one else has any independent ownership. Whatever human beings claim as their property is merely a temporary trust. This absolute ownership is the foundation of Allah’s right to command and legislate. The verse then pairs this with a second attribute: “And Allah is Fully Aware of everything.” The Arabic kāna Allāhu bi-kulli shayʾin muḥīṭan uses the image of complete encirclement. Allah’s knowledge encompasses every single thing: the vast galaxies and the tiniest ant, the public declarations and the most hidden secrets of the heart. Nothing escapes His awareness. This pairing of ownership and knowledge is central to the surah’s themes. Because Allah owns everything, He has the sole right to determine the shares of inheritance, the laws of marriage, and the rules of war. Because He knows everything, His laws are based on perfect wisdom and full awareness of every consequence. The verse serves as a powerful conclusion to the preceding discussion about faith and submission. The believer who truly understands that everything belongs to Allah and that He knows all will find it natural and logical to submit fully to His commands. Conversely, withholding any part of one’s life from that submission is a denial of these two fundamental truths. The verse is a call to live with a constant awareness that you, your wealth, your family, and your very breath are owned by the All-Knowing Lord.
127 | They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ regarding women. Say, “It is Allah Who instructs you regarding them. Instruction has ˹already˺ been revealed in the Book concerning the orphan women you deprive of their due rights but still wish to marry, also helpless children, as well as standing up for orphans’ rights. And whatever good you do is certainly well known to Allah.” | The verse reports a question posed to the Prophet about the rights of women, and the answer redirects the questioners to what Allah has already decreed. “They ask you O Prophet regarding women.” The questioners were some of the companions seeking clarity on the obligations towards women, including orphan girls. The Prophet is instructed to say, “It is Allah Who instructs you regarding them.” This emphasises that the rulings are not the Prophet’s personal opinions but divine legislation. The verse then specifies a pressing issue: “Instruction has already been revealed in the Book concerning the orphan women you deprive of their due rights but still wish to marry.” This refers back to the earlier verses of the surah, particularly verse 3, where guardians were warned about marrying orphan girls under their care without giving them their proper dowries or full rights. The guardians desired to marry these orphans, sometimes for their beauty or wealth, but they were neglecting the legal and financial protections that every woman, including an orphan, is entitled to. The verse reiterates that the Qur’an has already addressed this injustice. It then broadens the concern: “also helpless children, as well as standing up for orphans’ rights.” The command to protect orphans is not limited to female orphans; it extends to all vulnerable children. The verse closes with a powerful encouragement: “And whatever good you do is certainly well known to Allah.” Every just action, every fair treatment, every act of kindness toward women and orphans is fully recorded and will be rewarded. This verse demonstrates how the Qur’an consistently brings the community’s attention back to its most vulnerable members. It is a call to move from mere inquiry to action, from theoretical questions about women to the practical implementation of the justice that Allah has already commanded.
128 | If a woman fears indifference or neglect from her husband, there is no blame on either of them if they seek ˹fair˺ settlement, which is best. Humans are ever inclined to selfishness. But if you are gracious and mindful ˹of Allah˺, surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do. | This verse addresses a delicate marital situation. “If a woman fears indifference or neglect from her husband.” The Arabic nushūz here refers to the husband’s emotional detachment, lack of attention, or active aversion. The wife senses that her husband no longer desires her company, perhaps due to age, illness, or his inclination toward another wife. In pre-Islamic times, a husband might simply abandon such a wife, leaving her in a state of limbo—not divorced so she could remarry, but not treated as a wife. The verse provides a merciful alternative: “there is no blame on either of them if they seek fair settlement, which is best.” The wife can propose a reconciliation whereby she forgoes some of her financial rights, such as a portion of maintenance or equal nights, in exchange for the husband maintaining the marriage and treating her kindly instead of divorcing her. The husband is not to exploit this situation, but if both parties agree to a settlement that is just and voluntary, it is permissible and indeed described as “best” (khayr). This is better than divorce or a cold, bitter continuation. The verse then adds a profound psychological insight: “Humans are ever inclined to selfishness.” The Arabic al-shuḥḥ refers to an intense, deep-seated stinginess and self-centeredness. The husband might be unwilling to grant her full rights, and the wife might be unwilling to concede any rights. This natural selfishness is the obstacle that must be overcome by the higher Islamic values of graciousness and taqwā. The verse concludes: “But if you are gracious and mindful of Allah, surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do.” Graciousness (iḥsān) means going beyond the minimum obligation and acting with beauty and generosity. Mindfulness of Allah ensures that neither party oppresses the other. This verse is a masterpiece of marital guidance, providing a practical, peaceful resolution to a painful situation, acknowledging human weakness, and directing both spouses toward the higher ground of selfless grace and God-consciousness.
129 | You will never be able to maintain ˹emotional˺ justice between your wives—no matter how keen you are. So do not totally incline towards one leaving the other in suspense. And if you do what is right and are mindful ˹of Allah˺, surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | This verse addresses the inner dimension of polygyny, complementing the earlier discussion of material justice. “You will never be able to maintain emotional justice between your wives—no matter how keen you are.” The verse acknowledges an irreducible human reality: the heart’s inclinations are not fully under voluntary control. A man may be able to distribute his nights and his material provisions equally, but he cannot command his heart to love each wife with exactly the same intensity. One wife may simply be closer to his heart than another. This is not a sin in itself, because it is beyond his capacity. The verse then gives a practical command based on this reality: “So do not totally incline towards one leaving the other in suspense.” Even if the heart has a preference, the husband must not translate that preference into complete, visible favoritism. He must not shower one wife with all his affection, time, and attention while neglecting the other to the point where she feels like a suspended woman (ka-al-muʿallaqah)—neither properly married nor divorced. She has a right to companionship and care. The husband must strive, as far as humanly possible, to maintain a fair and equitable external treatment. The verse closes with a message of hope and mercy: “And if you do what is right and are mindful of Allah, surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” If the husband sincerely tries to be just, corrects his outward behavior, and maintains a constant awareness of Allah, then Allah will forgive the inevitable, involuntary leanings of the heart. This verse demonstrates the profound realism of Islamic law. It does not demand the impossible (complete emotional equality), but it strictly regulates the possible (outward treatment). It protects the vulnerable wife from being cast aside while acknowledging the complex dynamics of the human heart, all under the canopy of divine forgiveness.
130 | But if they choose to separate, Allah will enrich both of them from His bounties. And Allah is Ever-Bountiful, All-Wise. | After the previous verses on reconciliation and the difficulty of maintaining justice, this verse addresses the option of divorce with a tone of hope and reassurance. “But if they choose to separate.” The Arabic yatafarraqā indicates a mutual decision or a situation where reconciliation is no longer possible or healthy. If the efforts at settlement and fair treatment have not succeeded, and the marriage has become a source of misery and harm rather than tranquillity, separation is a permissible, and sometimes the best, course of action. The verse then immediately dispels the fear that often prevents people from ending a dysfunctional marriage: the fear of poverty, loneliness, or an uncertain future. “Allah will enrich both of them from His bounties.” This is a divine promise. Allah, in His vast generosity, can provide for each of them independently. He may grant the wife a new, loving husband and the husband a new, compatible wife, or enrich them with other forms of fulfillment and sustenance. The word “bounties” (saʿah) suggests a spaciousness and abundance that replaces the constriction of a difficult marriage. The verse closes with two divine names that underscore this promise: “And Allah is Ever-Bountiful, All-Wise.” Wāsiʿ means the Vast, the All-Encompassing, whose treasures are inexhaustible. He can easily compensate each spouse. Ḥakīm means the All-Wise, who legislated divorce as a merciful exit from a situation that, if prolonged, could lead to greater sin and suffering. This verse is a crucial corrective to any cultural stigma that treats divorce as an unmitigated evil. In Islam, while marriage is the preferred norm and reconciliation is strongly encouraged, divorce is a lawful solution when the purposes of marriage—love, mercy, and the establishment of a righteous home—are no longer achievable. The verse encourages believers to trust in Allah’s provision and wisdom, and not to cling to a broken marriage out of fear of what the future might hold.
131 | To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Indeed, We have commanded those given the Scripture before you, as well as you, to be mindful of Allah. But if you disobey, then ˹know that˺ to Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth. And Allah is Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy. | This verse weaves together the themes of divine ownership, universal prophecy, and the obligation of God-consciousness.
