It begins with a declaration that has been repeated throughout the surah: “To Allah alone belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth.” This reminder of Allah’s absolute ownership grounds everything that follows. The verse then states a unifying principle of all revelation: “Indeed, We have commanded those given the Scripture before you, as well as you, to be mindful of Allah.” The Arabic ittaqū Allāh (be mindful of Allah, fear Allah) is the core command given to every community that ever received a prophet. The Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur’an all converge on this single, essential call: to live with a constant awareness of the Creator, to fear His displeasure, and to act accordingly. The verse then addresses the possibility of rejection: “But if you disobey, then know that to Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth.” The disobedience of humanity does not diminish Allah’s dominion in the slightest. He does not need their worship; they need Him. The verse concludes with two profound names: “And Allah is Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy.” Al-Ghanī means He is absolutely rich, free from all need. Human obedience adds nothing to His majesty; human disobedience takes nothing away. Al-Ḥamīd means He is inherently Praiseworthy, deserving of all gratitude and worship, regardless of whether creatures offer it. This verse is a powerful blend of warning and consolation. For the believer, it reinforces the importance of taqwā as the timeless core of all religion. For the skeptic, it declares that their rebellion harms only themselves and does not affect the Self-Sufficient Lord. The verse places the believer in a posture of humility, recognizing that the command to be mindful of Allah is not a burden imposed by a needy deity but a gift from a Lord who is in no need of His creation and is worthy of all praise.
132 | To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs. | The repetition of the declaration of Allah’s ownership, almost identical to the beginning of the previous verse, is a deliberate rhetorical emphasis. “To Allah alone belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth.” The surah has now repeated this truth multiple times, each time pairing it with a different divine attribute. This repetition serves to hammer home the foundational reality that must govern every aspect of a believer’s life: you are not the ultimate owner of anything. Your wealth, your children, your very self belong to Allah. This has direct implications for the surah’s themes—how you spend your wealth, how you treat your dependents, and how you conduct your battles must all be shaped by the consciousness that you are merely a trustee, and Allah is the true Owner. The verse then pairs the declaration of ownership with a new attribute: “And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs.” The word Wakīl is beautifully rich. A wakīl is someone to whom you entrust your affairs, relying on them to manage, protect, and bring about the best outcome. The verse declares that Allah alone is sufficient to be that ultimate Trustee. When a believer genuinely internalises that Allah owns everything and that He is the best Disposer of affairs, a deep tranquillity descends upon the heart. You no longer need to be consumed by anxiety over what you might lose, because nothing was truly yours to begin with. You no longer need to scheme and manipulate to secure your interests, because the best Trustee is managing your affairs. This verse is a source of immense psychological and spiritual liberation. It calls the believer to place their complete trust in Allah, to surrender their worries, and to act with the calm confidence that the Owner and Manager of the universe is handling their matters.
133 | If it is His Will, He can remove you altogether, O humanity, and replace you with others. And Allah is Most Capable to do so. | After the declarations of ownership and sufficiency, this verse delivers a stark reminder of human replaceability and divine power. “If it is His Will, He can remove you altogether, O humanity, and replace you with others.” The address is broad: “O humanity” (ayyuhā al-nās). Every human being, regardless of status, wealth, or piety, is reminded that their continued existence on this earth is not a necessity but a gift sustained moment by moment by Allah’s will. If Allah so willed, He could cause the entire human race to perish and create an entirely different creation in their place. The verse echoes other Qur’anic passages that state that if people turn away, Allah will replace them with a people who are better and more devoted. This is a profound humbling of human arrogance. Nations, empires, and civilizations rise and feel invincible, but their power is borrowed and their tenure is temporary. The verse forcefully counters any attitude of entitlement or complacency. A person might think, “Allah needs my prayers,” or “My tribe is too powerful to fall,” or “I can disobey because I am important.” This verse sweeps all such illusions away. Allah is in no need of any particular person or group. The final phrase, “And Allah is Most Capable to do so,” reinforces the absolute ease with which this could occur. The word Qadīr means He has the complete, unrestricted power to bring this about effortlessly. For the believer, this verse inspires a deep sense of humility and urgency. It is a reminder to be grateful for the opportunity to serve Allah, to hasten to obedience, and to never assume that one’s place is guaranteed. It also provides comfort to the oppressed believer, who can look at their powerful oppressors and remember that Allah can sweep them away and replace them with better people whenever He wills.
134 | Whoever desires the reward of this world, then ˹let them know that˺ with Allah are the rewards of this world and the Hereafter. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing. | This verse addresses the orientation of human desire and redirects it toward the ultimate source of all good. “Whoever desires the reward of this world.” There are people whose primary motivation for their actions is the immediate, tangible reward of the worldly life: wealth, status, pleasure, or the praise of others. The verse does not dismiss the value of worldly goods; instead, it corrects the direction of the desire. “Then let them know that with Allah are the rewards of this world and the Hereafter.” The worldly rewards that people chase are ultimately in Allah’s hands. They do not belong to an employer, a business deal, or a political patron in any ultimate sense. If a person desires a good worldly outcome, they should seek it from Allah, who controls all worldly and otherworldly rewards. This teaching reorients the believer’s pursuit. Instead of making worldly gain the direct object of their striving, they make Allah’s pleasure the object, knowing that He can grant them the best of both worlds. The verse then grounds this in two divine attributes: “And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing.” He hears every request, every whispered prayer for provision, every cry of need. He sees every action, every honest effort, every hidden intention. The one who desires the reward of this world should therefore ask the All-Hearing and trust the All-Seeing to provide it in the best way and time. This verse is a powerful corrective to the materialistic mindset that treats the dunyā as an independent source of fulfillment. It teaches that the dunyā and the ākhirah are not in the hands of two different givers but in the single hand of Allah. The believer should, therefore, direct all desires toward Allah and work with the intention of earning His pleasure, trusting that the worldly rewards that are good for them will follow as part of the divine provision. It expands the believer’s horizon from the narrow, anxious pursuit of worldly means to the broad, peaceful reliance on the Lord of all means.
135 | O believers! Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives. Be they rich or poor, Allah is best to ensure their interests. So do not let your desires cause you to deviate ˹from justice˺. If you distort the testimony or refuse to give it, then ˹know that˺ Allah is certainly All-Aware of what you do. | This verse is widely regarded as one of the most powerful and comprehensive commands for justice in the entire Qur’an. It begins with the direct address: “O believers! Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah.” The Arabic qawwāmīna bi al-qisṭ is intensive; it means to be staunchly, persistently, habitually standing for justice. The believers are to be pillars of justice. The purpose of their testimony is not to please people or to serve tribal interests but to be “witnesses for Allah,” which means their testimony must reflect the truth that Allah commands, regardless of the personal cost. The verse then enumerates the most difficult scenarios: “even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives.” The natural human inclination is to protect oneself, to honour one’s parents, and to be loyal to one’s family. These are, in normal circumstances, virtuous inclinations. But when they conflict with justice, they become tests. The verse commands the believer to testify truthfully even if that testimony results in personal loss, brings shame or punishment upon one’s parents, or harms the interests of one’s beloved relatives. The verse then addresses another common source of bias: “Be they rich or poor, Allah is best to ensure their interests.” A witness might be tempted to favor the rich, hoping for a reward, or to pity the poor and give false testimony to help them. The verse reminds that Allah is the true guardian (awlā bihimā) of both. He knows what is truly in their best interest, which is justice. Falsifying testimony to help the poor is not true kindness; it is injustice that harms society and the soul. The verse then warns: “So do not let your desires cause you to deviate from justice.” Hawā, personal desire and bias, is the great enemy of truth. The verse concludes with a severe warning: “If you distort the testimony or refuse to give it, then know that Allah is certainly All-Aware of what you do.” Even if human courts cannot detect the perjury or the omission, the divine court has a complete and perfect record. This verse is the bedrock of the Islamic concept of justice. It demands that the believer be a person of truth even when the truth cuts against their own flesh and blood. It is a call to elevate the principle of justice above every other loyalty, and it places the ultimate accountability for this in the perfect knowledge of Allah.
136 | O believers! Have faith in Allah, His Messenger, the Book He has revealed to His Messenger, and the Scriptures He revealed before. Indeed, whoever denies Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day has clearly gone far astray. | After the powerful call for justice, the verse turns to the articles of faith, presenting them as the indispensable foundation of the believer’s identity. “O believers! Have faith in Allah, His Messenger, the Book He has revealed to His Messenger, and the Scriptures He revealed before.” The command “have faith” (āminū) is given to those who already call themselves believers. This indicates that faith is not a static, one-time declaration but an ongoing, deepening, and renewed commitment. The verse commands the believers to continually cultivate and renew their belief in Allah, in the specific messenger sent to them (Muhammad, peace be upon him), in the specific revelation given to him (the Qur’an), and in the entire chain of previous scriptures. This comprehensive belief distinguishes the Muslim community from those who believe in some prophets and reject others, or who accept some scriptures but deny the final one. The verse then presents the opposite state: “Indeed, whoever denies Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day has clearly gone far astray.” The six articles of faith are succinctly listed: Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day. The sixth, belief in divine decree, is implied in the comprehensive belief in Allah. Denying any of these pillars constitutes kufr, a rejection that leads to a misguidance described as “far astray” (ḍalālan baʿīdan). This verse serves as a clear boundary marker. It distinguishes the comprehensive faith of Islam from partial or selective belief. It teaches that the Muslim’s creed is an integrated whole; one cannot pick and choose among the articles of faith. It also implicitly warns the believers not to slide into the errors of those communities that came before, who accepted some prophets and rejected others, thereby nullifying their own claim to faith. The verse is a call to wholehearted, undivided, and intellectually consistent belief in the entire package of divine revelation.
137 | Indeed, those who believed then disbelieved, then believed and again disbelieved—˹only˺ increasing in disbelief—Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to the ˹Right˺ Way. | This verse describes a specific and tragic spiritual trajectory: the pattern of vacillating between belief and disbelief. “Indeed, those who believed then disbelieved, then believed and again disbelieved—only increasing in disbelief.” The verse outlines a sequence where a person repeatedly enters faith and then exits it, each time sinking deeper into disbelief. This is not the occasional doubt of a sincere seeker; it is a cynical oscillation, often attributed to the hypocrites of Medina who would profess Islam, then revert to open hostility, then superficially return when it suited their interests. Their pattern was not one of honest confusion but of insincere manipulation. The verse pronounces a severe divine judgment: “Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to the Right Way.” The finality of this judgment reflects the nature of their crime. They did not simply disbelieve out of ignorance; they tasted the truth, recognized it, and then repeatedly betrayed it. Each act of apostasy after belief represents a deeper, more deliberate rejection. The heart that engages in this pattern becomes progressively hardened until the door of repentance is effectively sealed. Allah will not grant them the forgiveness that is available to those who sin and repent sincerely, because their pattern demonstrates a complete lack of sincerity. The guidance to the Right Way is also withheld, as a just consequence of their repeated refusal to remain on it. This verse serves as a dire warning against treating faith as a game or a tool for worldly convenience. It teaches that faith is a sacred trust, and that toying with it—entering it and discarding it repeatedly—is a path that can lead to a point of no return. The believer is reminded to cherish and guard their faith, to seek steadfastness, and to fear the spiritual death that comes from making disbelief a habit.
138 | Give good news of a painful punishment to hypocrites, | This short verse delivers a shocking rhetorical inversion. The Arabic bashshir, “give good news,” is normally used for the most joyful, welcome tidings. Here, it is applied to a “painful punishment.” The irony is devastating. The hypocrites are being told that their “good news” is a torment that is excruciatingly painful. This use of the word bashshir for punishment is a Qur’anic technique that combines mockery with profound warning. It is as if to say: the only thing awaiting you that you can look forward to with certainty is this punishment. The verse succinctly identifies the recipients of this news: “the hypocrites” (al-munāfiqīn). Throughout the surah, the hypocrites have been a recurring theme—those who outwardly profess faith but inwardly conceal disbelief, those who plot against the community, those who lag behind in the struggle, those who mock the believers, and those who seek judgment from false sources. This verse is a summary judgment against all of them. The punishment is described as “painful” (alīm), emphasizing that it is not just a theoretical or distant threat but an experienced, felt agony. The verse serves multiple purposes within the surah’s rhetoric. For the sincere believers, it is a vindication of their patience in the face of the hypocrites’ schemes; justice is promised. For the hypocrites who might hear or read this verse, it is a final, blaring alarm bell, a call to abandon their double-dealing before the painful punishment becomes their inescapable reality. For all readers, it is a stark reminder that Allah knows the secrets of the hearts, and that those who play games with faith are in a far more perilous position than open disbelievers. The verse’s brevity and rhetorical force make it one of the most memorable warnings in the Qur’an.
139 | who choose disbelievers as allies instead of the believers. Do they seek honour and power through that company? Surely all honour and power belongs to Allah. | The previous verse pronounced the painful punishment for the hypocrites. This verse explains one of their defining characteristics that earns them that punishment. “Who choose disbelievers as allies instead of the believers.” The hypocrites of Medina would secretly align themselves with the pagan Quraysh or the hostile Jewish tribes, sharing intelligence, promising support, and seeking protection from them. They preferred the company and alliance of the enemies of Islam over the fellowship of the believers. This was not a matter of casual friendship; it was a strategic choice of loyalty and identity. The verse then diagnoses the root motivation behind this choice: “Do they seek honour and power through that company?” The Arabic ʿizzah means strength, dignity, honour, and the power that commands respect. The hypocrites believed that aligning with the strong disbelievers would grant them security and status. They feared that the Muslim community might be defeated, and they wanted to be on the winning side. This is a profound miscalculation of the source of true power. The verse responds with the definitive declaration: “Surely all honour and power belongs to Allah.” Al-ʿizzah jamīʿan is absolute. Every particle of strength, every source of dignity, every shred of lasting power belongs to Allah alone. Any power the disbelievers possess is temporary, borrowed, and under Allah’s control. To seek ʿizzah from other than Allah is to seek it from a source that is fundamentally bankrupt. The verse exposes the tragic irony of the hypocrites’ position: in their desperate attempt to secure honour through human alliances, they are aligning themselves with those whom Allah will ultimately humiliate, and they are cutting themselves off from the only true source of honour. This verse is a timeless lesson for believers. In every age, there are temptations to seek security, validation, and power through alliances that compromise faith and loyalty to the community of believers. The verse redirects the heart to Allah as the sole possessor of all that is sought.
140 | He has already revealed to you in the Book that when you hear Allah’s revelations being denied or ridiculed, then do not sit in that company unless they engage in a different topic, or else you will be like them. Surely Allah will gather the hypocrites and disbelievers all together in Hell. | This verse establishes a critical principle of social ethics and the protection of faith. “He has already revealed to you in the Book” refers to an earlier revelation, specifically in Sūrat al-Anʿām, where the same command was given. The repetition here in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ underscores its importance. The instruction is clear: “when you hear Allah’s revelations being denied or ridiculed, then do not sit in that company unless they engage in a different topic.” If a believer finds themselves in a gathering where the verses of Allah are being mocked, argued against in bad faith, or treated with contempt, they are obligated to leave. They cannot be a passive, silent spectator. Remaining in such an environment is a form of complicity, a silent endorsement of the sacrilege. The only exception is if the topic of conversation genuinely changes to something else. The verse then delivers a stark warning: “or else you will be like them.” If you choose to stay, you are effectively joining them in their sin. Your physical presence in the gathering while the revelation is being mocked aligns you with the mockers, even if in your heart you disagree. This is because the outward stance of a believer should be one of clear dissociation from the mockery of the divine. The verse then ties this back to the fate of the hypocrites: “Surely Allah will gather the hypocrites and disbelievers all together in Hell.” The hypocrites and the open disbelievers, despite their different outward presentations in this world, share the same ultimate destination. They will be gathered together, a fitting punishment for their shared mockery and rejection. This verse is a powerful guide for believers navigating hostile or secular environments. It teaches that there is a point at which tolerance and coexistence end and the duty to disassociate from active mockery of the sacred begins. It instils in the believer a protective dignity: the words of Allah are too precious to be sit silently by as they are trampled.
121 | It is they who will have Hell as their home, and they will find no escape from it! | The surah now draws the final conclusion about those who follow Satan’s delusions instead of Allah’s guidance. The pronoun “they” refers back to the people described in the previous verses: those who take Satan as a guardian, who fall for his empty promises, and who ultimately reject the truth. The verdict is definitive and terrifying: “It is they who will have Hell as their home.” The word “home” (maʾwā) is particularly striking. A home is a place of permanent residence, of return, of belonging. For these unfortunate souls, Hell is not a temporary prison or a passing punishment; it is the abode that fits their spiritual state, the destination they have earned through their choices. The verse then adds an emphatic negation: “and they will find no escape from it.” The Arabic maḥīṣan means a place of refuge, a way out, a means of evasion. There will be no exit door, no reduction of sentence, no asylum, no rescuer. This total hopelessness is the direct opposite of the false hopes that Satan had whispered to them. Satan promised them that everything would be fine, that they could enjoy life and escape consequences. Here, the divine truth shatters that lie: there is no escape. This verse serves as the closing bracket for the long section that began with the description of Satan’s rebellion and his plan to mislead humanity. It answers the question that the believer might have: what is the final end of those who follow that path? The answer is Hell, without any possibility of flight. The verse is meant to trigger a profound existential urgency. If this is the destination of one path, then every intelligent person must ask: am I on that path? The only way to avoid this home is to turn back to Allah, to seek His guardianship, and to abandon the false promises of Satan. The surah will immediately follow this with the beautiful alternative, continuing the Qur’anic pattern of pairing warnings with glad tidings.
122 | And those who believe and do good, We will soon admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever. Allah’s promise is ˹always˺ true. And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s? | In perfect balance with the previous verse, the surah now describes the eternal home of the believers. “And those who believe and do good” is the Qur’anic formula that combines inner conviction with outward action. Belief alone is not sufficient; it must manifest in righteous deeds. “We will soon admit them into Gardens under which rivers flow.” The use of the royal “We” conveys majesty, and the future particle “soon” (sa-) gives a sense of nearness and certainty. The phrase “under which rivers flow” is one of the most frequent descriptions of Paradise, evoking an image of perpetual, effortless abundance. The gardens are lush, shaded, and fed by flowing water, the most precious commodity in the Arabian desert. The phrase “to stay there for ever and ever” (khālidīna fīhā abadan) uses a double emphasis to remove any possibility of the bliss ending. This permanence is the greatest joy: no fear of eviction, no anxiety about losing happiness, an eternal security. The verse then anchors this promise in the most solid foundation: “Allah’s promise is always true.” The word “promise” (waʿd) is a verbal commitment from the Creator. For a believer, the fact that Allah has said it is the ultimate guarantee. Then comes the rhetorical question that silences any lingering doubt: “And whose word is more truthful than Allah’s?” The Arabic aṣdaqu min Allāhi qīlan means whose speech can possibly be more truthful, more accurate, more reliable? The obvious answer is no one. This question calls upon the reader’s innate rationality. If you believe in Allah, you must believe that His word is the most truthful. And if His word is the most truthful, then the promise of Paradise is more certain than any fact you perceive with your senses. This verse is an invitation to build one’s life on the solid rock of divine promise, to trade the fleeting, uncertain pleasures of this world for the guaranteed, eternal bliss of the next. It fills the heart with hope, courage, and a profound sense of God’s faithfulness.
123 | ˹Divine grace is˺ neither by your wishes nor those of the People of the Book! Whoever commits evil will be rewarded accordingly, and they will find no protector or helper besides Allah. | After the beautiful promise of Paradise, the surah grounds the believers in a fundamental reality: salvation is not based on wishful thinking or group identity. “Divine grace is neither by your wishes nor those of the People of the Book!” The Arabic laysa bi-amāniyyikum wa lā amāniyyi ahli al-kitāb is a sharp corrective. The Muslims might start thinking that they are automatically saved simply by virtue of being Muslims. The Jews and Christians claimed that they were the chosen people and that Paradise was exclusively theirs. The verse demolishes all such complacent assumptions. Entry into Paradise is not granted based on what a person wishes, hopes, or claims. It is based on the reality of faith and deeds, as the previous verse stated. The verse then sets out a universal principle of justice: “Whoever commits evil will be rewarded accordingly.” The word “rewarded” (yujza bihi) is used in a neutral sense of recompense. Every evil action, no matter how small, carries a consequence that is proportional to it. This applies to Muslims and non-Muslims alike. The justice of Allah does not discriminate by religious label; a wrongdoing believer will face the consequences of their wrongs, unless they repent and are forgiven. The verse then adds a sobering truth: “and they will find no protector or helper besides Allah.” On the Day of Judgment, or even in the trials of this world as a result of their sins, they will have no patron, no intercessor, no defender who can shield them from the divine justice unless Allah Himself wills to show mercy. This verse serves as a powerful antidote to religious arrogance and spiritual complacency. It calls the believers to a constant state of self-examination and humility, recognizing that their hope lies not in their identity as Muslims but in Allah’s mercy, which they must strive to earn through sincere faith and righteous action. It also serves as a warning to the People of the Book, telling them that their ancestral claims will not avail them if they persist in rejecting the final messenger.
124 | But those who do good—whether male or female—and have faith will enter Paradise and will never be wronged ˹even as much as˺ the speck on a date stone. | Having established the principle of individual accountability in the previous verse, the surah now opens the door of hope with a remarkable emphasis on the equality of men and women before Allah. “But those who do good—whether male or female—and have faith will enter Paradise.” The verse deliberately pairs male and female, making it unmistakably clear that gender has absolutely no bearing on spiritual merit or reward. In a cultural context where women were often treated as inferior and spiritually insignificant, this declaration was profoundly revolutionary. The path to Paradise is the same for all: doing good (ʿamila ṣāliḥan) and having faith (huwa muʾmin). The two conditions are inseparable. The good deeds must be rooted in faith, and the faith must be validated by good deeds. The verse then guarantees a perfect justice that exceeds all human standards: “and will never be wronged even as much as the speck on a date stone.” The “speck on a date stone” (naqīr) is the tiny dot on the back of a date pit, an image of the smallest conceivable quantity. Allah’s justice is so precise and so merciful that no believer will have even that minuscule amount of their good deeds discounted or their reward diminished. Conversely, no sin of theirs will be punished beyond what they deserve. This verse is a beautiful promise to every sincere believer who worries that their efforts are insufficient or that their gender or social status might disadvantage them. It teaches that Allah sees the hearts and the deeds, and His reward is based purely on that, with a generosity that ensures no one is short-changed. The mention of “male or female” explicitly connects to the surah’s broader theme of women’s rights and dignity, showing that the spiritual equality of women is the foundation upon which their social and economic rights, detailed earlier in the surah, are built.
125 | And who is better in faith than those who ˹fully˺ submit themselves to Allah, do good, and follow the Way of Abraham, the upright? Allah chose Abraham as a close friend. | The surah continues to define the highest standard of faith, in contrast to the hollow claims of religious communities. “And who is better in faith than those who fully submit themselves to Allah.” The Arabic aslama wajhahu lillāh means to surrender one’s whole face, one’s entire being and direction, to Allah. This is the essence of Islam: complete, loving submission. The person does not reserve any corner of their life from divine authority. The verse then adds two more qualities. First, “do good” (muḥsin), performing excellence in worship and conduct, living as if seeing Allah. Second, “and follow the Way of Abraham, the upright.” The mention of Abraham is deeply significant. He is the patriarch revered by Jews, Christians, and Muslims alike. The verse declares that the true faith is not a new invention but the restoration of the pure monotheism of Abraham, who was neither a Jew nor a Christian but a ḥanīf, an upright monotheist who turned away from all idolatry. This directly challenges the claims of the People of the Book who tried to claim Abraham for their own exclusive sects. The verse then mentions Abraham’s unique station: “Allah chose Abraham as a close friend.” The Arabic khalīl means an intimate, beloved friend. This is a rank of the highest honour, a special relationship that Abraham attained through his unwavering devotion, his hospitality, his willingness to sacrifice everything for Allah. By mentioning this, the verse invites the believers to aspire to walk in Abraham’s footsteps, to seek that closeness to Allah through submission and goodness. It also serves as a unifying call to all who claim to follow Abraham: if you truly love Abraham, then follow his way, which is the way of pure submission to the One God, embodied in the final message of Islam.
126 | To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is Fully Aware of everything. | This brief but profound verse acts as a universal conclusion to the previous discussion about faith, Abraham, and competing claims. “To Allah alone belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth.” This is a declaration of absolute divine ownership and sovereignty. Everything that exists—the stars, the planets, the angels, the mountains, the oceans, every human soul, every atom—is the sole property of Allah. There is no co-ownership, no partnership, no autonomous domain that exists independently of His will. This comprehensive ownership is the basis for everything else: for His right to be worshipped alone, for His right to legislate, for His right to judge, and for the fact that no human can claim inherent superiority based on lineage or race, because all belong equally to Him. The verse then adds: “And Allah is Fully Aware of everything.” The Arabic kāna bi-kulli shayʾin muḥīṭan uses the powerful image of complete encircling knowledge. Allah’s awareness encompasses the outward and the inward, the past, present, and future, the visible and the invisible. This verse serves multiple functions in the surah. It reminds the believers that their disputes about women, orphans, and inheritance are all under the watchful eye of the Owner. It tells the People of the Book that their exclusive claims to divine favour are absurd, because the entire universe belongs to Allah, and He knows their true state. It tells the hypocrites that their hidden scheming is fully known. And it tells the sincere believers that their secret acts of devotion are fully seen and will never be lost. The verse is a powerful dose of humility and trust: everything we have is borrowed, and everything we do is witnessed. The only sensible response is to submit the face, as mentioned in the previous verse, to the One who owns all and knows all.
127 | They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ regarding women. Say, “It is Allah Who instructs you regarding them. Instruction has ˹already˺ been revealed in the Book concerning the orphan women you deprive of their due rights but still wish to marry, also helpless children, as well as standing up for orphans’ rights. And whatever good you do is certainly well known to Allah.” | The surah returns to the rights of women, triggered by questions from the community. “They ask you O Prophet regarding women.” The companions, seeking clarity on the many new rulings about marriage, inheritance, and orphans, requested further elaboration. The Prophet is instructed to respond: “Say, ‘It is Allah Who instructs you regarding them.’” This opening immediately redirects attention from human opinion to divine authority. The answers are not the Prophet’s personal views but revelations from the All-Knowing. The verse then specifies a key issue: “Instruction has already been revealed in the Book concerning the orphan women you deprive of their due rights but still wish to marry.” This refers back to verse 3 and the practice of guardians marrying orphan girls under their care without giving them a fair dowry or treating them justly. Some guardians, attracted to the orphan girl’s beauty or wealth, would marry her but deny her the full bridal due owed to a free woman. The verse rebukes this: “you deprive of their due rights but still wish to marry.” The conflict of interest is exposed. The “Book” mentioned is this very surah, where these rulings have been given. The verse then expands the category of the vulnerable: “also helpless children, as well as standing up for orphans’ rights.” The instruction is not limited to female orphans but extends to all orphans and weak children who cannot defend their own interests. The verse calls for standing firm for them (qawwāmīna), the same word used for men’s responsibility toward women, indicating a communal obligation of care and justice. The verse closes with a reminder: “And whatever good you do is certainly well known to Allah.” Every act of justice, every kindness shown to an orphan, every fair dealing is fully recorded and will be rewarded. This verse demonstrates that the Qur’an addresses social problems directly and repeatedly, ensuring that the message of protecting the vulnerable is deeply ingrained in the community’s conscience.
128 | If a woman fears indifference or neglect from her husband, there is no blame on either of them if they seek ˹fair˺ settlement, which is best. Humans are ever inclined to selfishness. But if you are gracious and mindful ˹of Allah˺, surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do. | From the rights of orphans, the surah turns to a delicate marital situation. “If a woman fears indifference or neglect from her husband.” The Arabic nushūz here refers to the husband’s behavior: he turns away from her, withholds affection, fails to fulfill her rights of companionship and maintenance, and shows signs of wanting to separate or abandon her emotionally. The woman is in a vulnerable position; she fears the marriage is breaking down through no fault of her own. The verse provides a solution: “there is no blame on either of them if they seek fair settlement, which is best.” The “fair settlement” (ṣulḥan) is a mutual agreement to reconcile and adjust the terms of the marriage. For example, the wife might voluntarily forgo some of her financial rights, such as a portion of her maintenance or her nights with the husband, in exchange for him staying in the marriage and treating her kindly. The verse explicitly states that such a settlement is “best” (khayr), better than divorce. The preservation of the family unit, even with some compromises, is preferable to its dissolution. The verse then offers a profound psychological observation: “Humans are ever inclined to selfishness.” The Arabic uḥḍirati al-anfusu al-shuḥḥa means that souls are naturally steeped in greed and self-interest. The husband may be unwilling to be generous; the wife may be unwilling to give up any of her rights. This inherent selfishness is the obstacle to the fair settlement. The verse then encourages rising above this nature: “But if you are gracious and mindful of Allah, surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do.” Graciousness (tuḥsinū) means doing more than what is strictly required, showing kindness and generosity. Mindfulness (tattaqū) means fearing Allah’s observation. When both spouses prioritize their fear of Allah over their personal desires, they can reach a settlement that preserves the marriage. The closing reminder that Allah is All-Aware assures them that any sacrifice made for the sake of family harmony is fully seen and will be greatly rewarded. This verse is a masterpiece of marital guidance, acknowledging human greed while pointing to the higher path of grace and God-consciousness.
129 | You will never be able to maintain ˹emotional˺ justice between your wives—no matter how keen you are. So do not totally incline towards one leaving the other in suspense. And if you do what is right and are mindful ˹of Allah˺, surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | This verse addresses a profound psychological reality in polygynous marriages, continuing the surah’s theme of fairness to women. It begins with a striking statement: “You will never be able to maintain emotional justice between your wives—no matter how keen you are.” While verse 3 required justice in material provisions and time, this verse acknowledges the limits of human capacity. Emotional justice—equal love, equal affection, equal inclination of the heart—is beyond human control. The heart loves whom it loves, and a man may naturally feel more drawn to one wife over another. This is not a sin, as it is not voluntary. However, the verse immediately follows with a command based on this reality: “So do not totally incline towards one leaving the other in suspense.” The phrase “totally incline” (tamīlū kulla al-mayl) means to give all your attention, affection, and companionship to the favored wife while completely neglecting the other, leaving her in a state of limbo—neither fully married nor divorced, “suspended” (ka-al-muʿallaqah). This is a form of cruelty. The husband may not be able to control his heart, but he can and must control his actions. He must maintain the rotation of nights and the basic companionship that is the right of every wife. The verse then provides an upward path: “And if you do what is right and are mindful of Allah, surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” By doing what is right (tuṣliḥū) in terms of outward justice, and by being mindful of Allah (tattaqū), acknowledging one’s inner limitations and seeking His help to be fair, the believer places himself under the canopy of divine forgiveness and mercy. The verse ends with these two names to comfort the believing man who struggles with this natural inclination but genuinely strives to be just. It also comforts the wife who feels less favored, as she knows that her husband’s efforts are seen and rewarded, and that Allah’s mercy will compensate for any human shortcoming. This is a balanced, compassionate, and realistic legislation that acknowledges human nature while upholding the highest ethical standards.
130 | But if they choose to separate, Allah will enrich both of them from His bounties. And Allah is Ever-Bountiful, All-Wise. | After outlining the possibilities of reconciliation and the challenges of emotional justice, the surah acknowledges that sometimes separation is the best outcome. “But if they choose to separate” (yatafarraqā) refers to divorce. Despite the previous encouragements toward settlement, the verse recognizes that some marriages reach an impasse where the spouses cannot live together in kindness and mutual fulfillment. In such cases, they may separate. The verse immediately offers a divine reassurance to both parties, who might fear that divorce will lead to poverty, loneliness, or a diminished life: “Allah will enrich both of them from His bounties.” The word “enrich” (yughni) means to make self-sufficient, to provide for, to render free of need. The husband who feared financial ruin due to the costs of divorce, and the wife who feared destitution without a provider, are both assured that Allah’s bounties are vast. He may grant a new, better spouse; He may open doors of provision that were previously closed; He may grant contentment and peace in a new life. The verse uses the dual form “both of them” (kullan), emphasizing that the divine care extends individually to each ex-spouse. The promise is grounded in two divine names: “And Allah is Ever-Bountiful, All-Wise.” Wāsiʿ means Vast, All-Encompassing in bounty; His resources are limitless, so He can easily enrich both. Ḥakīm means that His decree of separation, painful as it may be, contains wisdom that the spouses may not immediately perceive. Sometimes a marriage that has become toxic is blocking greater good for both individuals. This verse removes the fear that often keeps people trapped in miserable, destructive marriages. It teaches that believers should not cling to a broken relationship out of fear of the future, because the future is in the hands of the Bountiful and Wise, who can bring forth unexpected blessings from the dissolution of what seemed like an irreplaceable bond.
131 | To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Indeed, We have commanded those given the Scripture before you, as well as you, to be mindful of Allah. But if you disobey, then ˹know that˺ to Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth. And Allah is Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy. | The surah now widens the lens to a universal principle that encompasses all people and all revelations. The verse opens for the third time in this surah with the declaration: “To Allah alone belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth.” This ownership is the foundation of the command that follows: “Indeed, We have commanded those given the Scripture before you, as well as you, to be mindful of Allah.” The message of taqwā, God-consciousness and piety, is not new. It was the core command given to every previous religious community—Jews, Christians, and all others who received scriptures. And now it is commanded to the Muslim ummah. This unifies all divine messages around a central axis: fearing Allah and being mindful of Him. The verse then addresses the hypothetical case of disobedience: “But if you disobey, then know that to Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth.” The repetition of the ownership statement here is powerful. It means that human disobedience does not diminish Allah’s dominion in the slightest. He does not need human obedience; humans need it for their own salvation. If you reject the command, you harm only yourself, because the entire universe still belongs to Allah, and He remains in absolute control. The verse closes with two names that underscore this self-sufficiency: “And Allah is Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy.” Al-Ghanī means He is completely free of any need. Your worship adds nothing to Him; your rebellion takes nothing away. Al-Ḥamīd means He is intrinsically Praiseworthy, deserving of all praise regardless of whether anyone praises Him. This verse shatters any human arrogance that might think God needs their devotion. It calls believers to taqwā not for Allah’s benefit but for their own, and it comforts the faithful that the rejection of others will not harm the cause of truth, because the Lord of all worlds is independent and glorious beyond any human action.
132 | To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs. | The surah repeats the declaration of divine ownership, this time paired with a different divine name that carries a specific implication. The statement “To Allah alone belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth” is a foundational truth that the believer must internalize. Recognizing that everything belongs to Allah means that one’s own life, wealth, family, and future are not truly one’s own; they are trusts from the true Owner. This recognition is the cure for excessive anxiety, greed, and fear of loss. The verse then adds: “And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs.” The word Wakīl means the One who is entrusted with managing affairs, the Disposer of all things. If everything belongs to Allah, then He is also the one who takes care of it all. The believer is being told: you do not need to carry the weight of the world on your shoulders. Entrust your affairs to their true Owner. He is a sufficient Trustee. This verse is a source of immense comfort. In the context of the surah’s discussions about marital discord, the challenges of justice, the threat of enemies, and the schemes of hypocrites, this declaration acts like a deep breath for the soul. It says: the entire cosmos is in Allah’s hands; He manages it perfectly. So, place your trust in Him. This is not a call to passivity but to a state of active reliance where you do your best and then hand over the outcome. The repetition of the ownership phrase throughout these verses serves as a rhythmic anchor, pulling the heart back from the details of life’s problems to the big picture of divine sovereignty. It teaches that the believer’s peace of mind does not come from perfect circumstances but from a perfect trust in the One who owns and governs all circumstances.
133 | If it is His Will, He can remove you altogether, O humanity, and replace you with others. And Allah is Most Capable to do so. | This verse is a direct address to all of humanity, a stark reminder of their complete dependence on the divine will. “If it is His Will, He can remove you altogether, O humanity.” The Arabic yudhhibkum means to cause you to perish, to eliminate you entirely. The phrase “O humanity” (ayyuhā al-nās) makes the warning universal. No nation, no civilization, no generation is indispensable. If Allah wills, He can wipe out the entire human race and nothing of His dominion would be lost. This is a humbling thought that cuts at the root of human arrogance. The verse continues: “and replace you with others.” The Arabic yaʾti bi-ākharīn means He can bring a completely new creation, other beings who might be more obedient and grateful. The verse then underscores this capacity: “And Allah is Most Capable to do so.” The word Qadīr means fully able, completely powerful. There is no force that could prevent this, no difficulty in its execution. This verse, placed after the reminders of Allah’s ownership and self-sufficiency, serves as a powerful check on any sense of entitlement or complacency. The believers are being reminded that their existence as a community is a gift that can be withdrawn if they fail to fulfill the purpose for which they were created. It echoes the Qur’anic principle that Allah is not in need of anyone’s worship and that He can easily replace any people with better ones. The verse is meant to inspire a sense of urgency, gratitude, and humility. Every day of life, every opportunity to do good, is a grace that could have been otherwise. The only appropriate response is to live in a state of mindful obedience, recognizing that our continued existence is not a right but a mercy, and that the One who granted life can, in an instant, take it back and bring forth a new creation that fulfills the purpose of existence more faithfully.
134 | Whoever desires the reward of this world, then ˹let them know that˺ with Allah are the rewards of this world and the Hereafter. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing. | This verse corrects a fundamental misunderstanding about the source of all benefit. “Whoever desires the reward of this world” refers to a person whose primary goal and intention is worldly gain—wealth, status, pleasure, and comfort. They seek the “reward” (thawāb) of the dunya, the immediate payoff of their efforts. The verse does not condemn having worldly desires, but it redirects the seeker to the correct address: “then let them know that with Allah are the rewards of this world and the Hereafter.” If you want the world, do not seek it directly from the world as if it were an independent source. Seek it from Allah, who owns both this world and the next. The worldly benefits you crave are in His hands, and He gives them to whom He wills. More importantly, if you are going to seek a reward, why limit your ambition to the temporary, flawed rewards of this life when the same Lord holds the infinite, perfect rewards of the Hereafter? The verse is an invitation to upgrade one’s aspirations. Rather than chasing the dunya for its own sake, chase the pleasure of the One who controls the dunya and the ākhirah. By making your intention purely for Allah, you may receive both the worldly provision that suffices you and the eternal bliss that awaits you. The verse concludes with two divine names that are perfectly suited to this context: “And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing.” He hears every supplication for provision, every whisper of desire, every complaint of poverty. He sees every effort, every sacrifice, every hidden intention behind the pursuit of worldly goals. Because He is All-Hearing and All-Seeing, you can trust that no sincere dua is ignored and no honest effort is overlooked. This verse teaches the believer to spiritualize their worldly ambitions, to transform the pursuit of a livelihood into an act of worship by directing the heart to the true Provider, and to always remember that the best reward is the one that lasts forever.
135 | O believers! Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives. Be they rich or poor, Allah is best to ensure their interests. So do not let your desires cause you to deviate ˹from justice˺. If you distort the testimony or refuse to give it, then ˹know that˺ Allah is certainly All-Aware of what you do. | This verse is one of the most powerful calls to absolute justice in the entire Qur’an. It begins with the address “O believers!” calling upon the faith of the community to manifest in the highest ethical standard. “Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah.” The Arabic qawwāmīna bi al-qisṭ conveys the image of people who are constantly upright, resolutely committed to equity. They are “witnesses for Allah,” meaning their testimony is not for any human party but for the sake of Allah, the embodiment of truth. The verse then presents the most difficult test cases of justice: “even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives.” The order is a descending scale of human loyalty. First, the self: a person must testify against their own interest, admitting their own guilt or error. Second, parents: the most beloved and respected figures in a person’s life. Third, close relatives: the clan and family upon whom one depends. In all these cases, the truth must prevail over personal affection, family honor, and tribal solidarity. The verse then addresses another potential bias: “Be they rich or poor, Allah is best to ensure their interests.” Sometimes justice is bent in favor of the rich out of fear or hope of gain. Sometimes it is bent in favor of the poor out of misplaced pity. The verse prohibits both. Leave the consequences to Allah; He is the best caretaker of both the rich and the poor. Your job is to speak the truth. “So do not let your desires cause you to deviate from justice.” The word “desires” (hawā) encompasses all personal inclinations—love, hatred, greed, fear. Following these emotions leads to distortion. The verse closes with a severe warning: “If you distort the testimony or refuse to give it, then know that Allah is certainly All-Aware of what you do.” Distorting means twisting the words, altering the facts; refusing to give it means withholding evidence. Both are major sins, and Allah’s complete awareness makes the hidden crime fully known. This verse establishes the bedrock of Islamic judicial ethics, a standard so high that it challenges the deepest human attachments, calling the believer to a loyalty to truth that surpasses all other loyalties.
136 | O believers! Have faith in Allah, His Messenger, the Book He has revealed to His Messenger, and the Scriptures He revealed before. Indeed, whoever denies Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day has clearly gone far astray. | The surah now calls the believers to a comprehensive and renewed faith. “O believers! Have faith in Allah, His Messenger, the Book He has revealed to His Messenger, and the Scriptures He revealed before.” Addressing the believers with “have faith” might seem redundant, but it is a call to deepen, consolidate, and maintain their faith. The four objects of faith are listed in a specific order. First, Allah—the foundation. Second, His Messenger—the channel of guidance. Third, the Book revealed to him—the Qur’an, the immediate source of law and wisdom. Fourth, the Scriptures He revealed before—the Torah, the Gospel, and other revealed texts in their original, uncorrupted forms. This fourth point was crucial in the Medinan context where Muslims lived alongside Jews and Christians and needed to affirm the original truth of their revelations while recognizing the Qur’an as the final, preserved criterion. The verse then presents the opposite state and its consequence: “Indeed, whoever denies Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day has clearly gone far astray.” The articles of faith are expanded here to include angels and the Last Day, completing the pillars of iman. Denial of any of these articles constitutes kufr. The phrase “has clearly gone far astray” (ḍalla ḍalālan baʿīdan) describes a misguidance that is profound and distant from the truth. The verse serves as a creedal summary for the Muslim community, defining the boundaries of faith. It warns that a selective belief—accepting some prophets and rejecting others, or believing in Allah but not the Day of Judgment—is not acceptable. True faith is an integrated whole. This verse also has a continuous relevance: it calls on believers in every age to examine whether their faith encompasses all that Allah has commanded, or if they have, consciously or unconsciously, excluded parts of the divine revelation.
137 | Indeed, those who believed then disbelieved, then believed and again disbelieved—˹only˺ increasing in disbelief—Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to the ˹Right˺ Way. | This verse describes a specific category of people whose spiritual vacillation has led them to a point of no return. “Indeed, those who believed then disbelieved, then believed and again disbelieved—only increasing in disbelief.” The verse refers to the hypocrites or to certain groups among the People of the Book, such as some Jews who believed in Moses and then disbelieved by rejecting subsequent prophets, or who wavered in their acceptance of the Prophet Muhammad. The pattern is a cycle of faith and rejection, but with a critical trajectory: “only increasing in disbelief.” Each relapse was deeper than the previous one. Their hearts did not remain static; they progressively hardened. The verse then delivers a devastating verdict: “Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to the Right Way.” This does not contradict the general promise that Allah forgives all sins if one repents. It refers to those who have reached a terminal state of hypocrisy and rejection, where their constant oscillation and ultimate persistence in disbelief at the time of death seal their fate. Their sin is not just disbelief but a pattern of trifling with faith, entering it and then abandoning it repeatedly until the door of their hearts closed forever. The “Right Way” (sabīlan) here means the path of guidance that leads to Paradise. Their own choices have rendered them incapable of finding it. This verse is a terrifying warning against playing games with faith, treating it as a social convenience or a philosophical experiment. Faith is a sacred commitment. To enter it and leave it, to treat the truth as something to be tried on and discarded, is a path that can lead to a hardened heart that no longer responds to guidance. The verse underscores the urgency of a sincere, stable, and ever-deepening faith, not a shallow, fluctuating affiliation.
138 | Give good news of a painful punishment to hypocrites, | This brief verse delivers a powerful ironic shock. “Give good news” (bashshir) is the word used for glad tidings, the announcement of something joyful. The Prophet is being commanded to use this term for the hypocrites, but the content of the “good news” is “a painful punishment.” The irony is a form of rhetorical punishment. The hypocrites, who mocked the believers and thought they were clever and safe, are being told to look forward to their future as if it were good news, when in fact it is the most horrific reality. This bitter sarcasm serves to highlight the horror of their situation. The hypocrites are those who outwardly profess faith but inwardly conceal disbelief. They believe they have deceived the believers and secured their own interests. But this verse reveals that their true destiny is not the safety they imagined but a painful torment. The brevity of the verse adds to its impact. It is a sharp, sudden pronouncement, cutting through the hypocrites' pretensions. The command to the Prophet to “give good news” is also a psychological preparation for the believers: they should not be distressed by the apparent success of the hypocrites, because behind the facade, a painful punishment awaits them. The verse serves as a severe warning and a declaration of divine justice. It is placed immediately before a verse that exposes one of the hypocrites' specific traits, which explains why this punishment is their due.
139 | who choose disbelievers as allies instead of the believers. Do they seek honour and power through that company? Surely all honour and power belongs to Allah. | This verse continues the description of the hypocrites who are to receive the painful punishment, identifying a key characteristic of their betrayal. “Who choose disbelievers as allies instead of the believers.” The hypocrites, in their hearts, were not convinced of the truth of Islam. They felt more affinity with the disbelieving tribes, whether the pagan Arabs or hostile Jewish factions. They would form secret alliances with them, share intelligence, and seek their protection and friendship, while outwardly claiming to be part of the Muslim community. This dual loyalty is a hallmark of nifāq. The verse then poses a rhetorical question that exposes the flaw in their calculation: “Do they seek honour and power through that company?” The word ʿizzah means might, honour, dignity, and invincibility. The hypocrites believed that by aligning with the stronger worldly powers, they would secure their own honour and safety. They thought that the disbelievers, with their wealth and numbers, were the source of real strength. The verse refutes this assumption with a categorical statement: “Surely all honour and power belongs to Allah.” Al-ʿizzah jamīʿan lillāh—every form of honour, every shred of true power, belongs entirely and exclusively to Allah. He grants it to whom He wills, and He strips it from whom He wills. Seeking honour from sources opposed to Allah is a delusion. The disbelievers have no independent power to grant; their might is a fleeting illusion that will vanish. This verse corrects the believer’s and the hypocrite’s worldview. True honour is not found in alliances with the powerful of this world if those alliances compromise faith and principle. It is found in submission to the One who owns all power. The hypocrites, in their pursuit of worldly security, lose both their worldly honour and their eternal salvation, while the believers, who may appear weak and outnumbered, possess the ultimate source of strength.
140 | He has already revealed to you in the Book that when you hear Allah’s revelations being denied or ridiculed, then do not sit in that company unless they engage in a different topic, or else you will be like them. Surely Allah will gather the hypocrites and disbelievers all together in Hell. | This verse issues a crucial communal directive to protect the faith from the corrosive influence of mockery. “He has already revealed to you in the Book” refers to earlier revelations, including a similar command in Sūrat al-Anʿām, emphasizing that this is a consistent divine principle. The rule is: “when you hear Allah’s revelations being denied or ridiculed, then do not sit in that company.” It is not enough to inwardly disapprove while physically remaining in a gathering where the signs of Allah are mocked. The believer must physically leave. The reason is twofold: to show clear rejection of the sin, and to protect one’s own heart from being gradually desensitized to the mockery. The command includes an exception: “unless they engage in a different topic.” If the conversation shifts away from the mockery, then the believer may remain. The verse then delivers a severe warning: “or else you will be like them.” If you stay and listen to the mockery without objection, you share in their sin and their spiritual state. This is a profound principle: silent complicity in sin is a form of participation. It applies to all contexts where the truth is being ridiculed—whether in social gatherings, media, or online platforms. The believer cannot claim to be a passive, unaffected bystander. The verse closes with a divine promise that encompasses the fate of both the mockers and those who stay with them: “Surely Allah will gather the hypocrites and disbelievers all together in Hell.” The hypocrites, who might outwardly have claimed belief but participated in or tolerated such gatherings, will be gathered alongside the open disbelievers. There will be no distinction in their final abode. This verse is a powerful call to moral courage, to the willingness to stand up and walk away from environments where faith is degraded. It teaches that preserving one’s iman sometimes requires a physical, social separation, and that the fear of social awkwardness or isolation must never override the fear of Allah. It also offers a warning to those who rationalize their presence in such gatherings as harmless; the verse explicitly labels such a person as potentially becoming “like them.”
141 | ˹The hypocrites are˺ those who wait to see what happens to you. So if Allah grants you victory, they say ˹to you˺, “Were we not on your side?” But if the disbelievers have a share ˹of victory˺, they say ˹to them˺, “Did we not have the advantage over you, yet we protected you from the believers?” Allah will judge between ˹all of˺ you on the Day of Judgment. And Allah will never grant the disbelievers a way over the believers. | The surah now paints a detailed portrait of the hypocrites' opportunistic behaviour. “The hypocrites are those who wait to see what happens to you.” The Arabic word yatarabbaṣūna means they lie in wait, watching and biding their time. They do not commit wholeheartedly to the community of believers; they stand on the sidelines, observing the fortunes of war and politics as a spectator might watch a game, ready to align with whichever side emerges victorious. Their guiding principle is not truth but self-interest. The verse then exposes the two-faced response to outcomes. “So if Allah grants you victory, they say to you, ‘Were we not on your side?’” When the believers triumph, the hypocrites rush forward to claim a share of the spoils and the honour, insisting that they were always allies. They invoke their presence, their words of support, anything that can retroactively paint them as loyal. But when the tide turns, “if the disbelievers have a share of victory, they say to them, ‘Did we not have the advantage over you, yet we protected you from the believers?’” In defeat or when the enemy gains an upper hand, the same hypocrites pivot to the other side. They boast to the disbelievers that they secretly worked for their benefit, holding back information or effort from the Muslims, and that their presence among the believers was a strategic cover. They play both ends against the middle, always ensuring their own safety and profit. The verse then pronounces the divine verdict: “Allah will judge between all of you on the Day of Judgment.” All the secret dealings, the whispered betrayals, and the double-faced flattery will be laid bare in the court of the Almighty. The hypocrites will not be able to charm or deceive their way out. Finally, the verse delivers a powerful reassurance to the believers: “And Allah will never grant the disbelievers a way over the believers.” This is a statement of ultimate victory and divine protection, not necessarily in every single worldly battle, but in the grand, eschatological sense, and in the principle that the truth will always prevail. Even when the believers suffer temporary setbacks, the disbelievers are not given an absolute, permanent triumph. This verse is a profound source of comfort for the faithful, reminding them that the hypocrisy and opportunism of the weak-hearted will be exposed, and that the final outcome is secured by divine promise.
142 | Surely the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah, but He outwits them. When they stand up for prayer, they do it half-heartedly only to be seen by people—hardly remembering Allah at all. | The exposure of the hypocrites continues by unveiling their inner delusion and their hollow worship. “Surely the hypocrites seek to deceive Allah.” The Arabic yukhādiʿūna Allāh is striking. The hypocrites imagine that by outwardly professing faith while inwardly concealing disbelief, they can fool Allah just as they fool the believers. They think their secret scheming is hidden. But the verse immediately shatters this illusion: “but He outwits them.” Allah is the ultimate planner. Their deception is turned back upon them. How? By granting them respite in this world, allowing them to continue in their hypocrisy, and thus accumulating sin for a crushing punishment. What they think is a successful con is actually the mechanism of their own destruction. The verse then provides a vivid description of the hypocrites' prayer, the most visible act of worship: “When they stand up for prayer, they do it half-heartedly only to be seen by people.” The Arabic kusālā means lazy, sluggish, with a heavy reluctance. They drag themselves to prayer not out of love for Allah or hope for His reward, but out of social pressure and fear of exposure. Their bodies are present in the mosque, but their souls are elsewhere. Their prayer is not a conversation with the Divine but a theatrical performance, a riyāʾ, an act of showing off. The goal is to maintain their standing in the community, not to connect with their Creator. The verse then adds a devastating detail: “hardly remembering Allah at all.” In a state that is supposed to be the pinnacle of dhikr, they barely allow a flicker of genuine remembrance to cross their minds. Their thoughts are on the opinions of the onlookers, on the timing, on anything but Allah. This description is not just historical; it is a permanent mirror for every believer to examine their own prayer. Do we stand before Allah with laziness and distraction, performing hollow motions while our hearts are absent? The verse warns that such prayer is a sign of hypocrisy, and it calls the believers to infuse their worship with presence, sincerity, and the vivid remembrance of the One before whom they stand.
143 | Torn between belief and disbelief—belonging neither to these ˹believers˺ nor those ˹disbelievers˺. And whoever Allah leaves to stray, you will never find for them a way. | The previous verse described the hypocrites' lazy prayer. This verse encapsulates their entire existential condition. “Torn between belief and disbelief.” The Arabic mudhabdhabīna bayna dhālika conveys an image of wavering, swinging back and forth, never settling in one place. The hypocrite is a spiritual schizophrenic. He does not have the courage of full conviction, whether for Islam or for open kufr. He stands in a no-man's land, his heart attached to neither the truth nor a clear falsehood. This is a state of profound inner torment and instability. The verse then defines their lack of belonging: “belonging neither to these believers nor those disbelievers.” They are not truly of the believers, because their faith is a mere outward show. They are not openly of the disbelievers, because they fear the social and legal consequences. They are a third, wretched category, stranded between two solid grounds. This state is far worse than open disbelief, because the open disbeliever may one day recognize his error and repent, but the hypocrite is comfortable in his deception and sees no need to change. The verse concludes with the divine principle: “And whoever Allah leaves to stray, you will never find for them a way.” When a person persistently chooses this wavering path, rejecting the clear guidance, Allah leaves them to their own confusion. Their spiritual compass becomes permanently broken. No human guide, no preacher, no amount of evidence can bring them to the straight path because the straying is now sealed by their own choices. This is a terrifying warning against the sin of hypocrisy. It teaches that truth demands a wholehearted commitment, and that lingering in the grey zone of doubt and deception is a path that leads to being lost with no way back. The believer must constantly seek steadfastness and beg Allah to keep their heart firmly on the straight path, avoiding the wretched state of being suspended between two worlds.
144 | O believers! Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of the believers. Would you like to give Allah solid proof against yourselves? | After the devastating exposé of hypocrisy, the verse turns to the sincere believers with a clear command to safeguard their loyalties. “O believers! Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of the believers.” The Arabic awliyāʾ means close friends, protectors, and allies to whom one entrusts secrets and upon whom one relies for support. The command is to not make those who reject the truth one's intimate companions and confidants in preference to the believing community. This is a necessary boundary for the preservation of faith and the cohesion of the Muslim ummah. It does not prohibit kindness, justice, or fair dealings with non-Muslims; those are commanded elsewhere. What is prohibited is a depth of loyalty and dependence that replaces the brotherhood of faith with an attachment to those who are, by definition, not sharing the core commitment to Allah and His Messenger. The verse then poses a sharp rhetorical question: “Would you like to give Allah solid proof against yourselves?” The Arabic sulṭānan mubīnan means a clear, undeniable authority or argument. If believers choose the enemies of Allah as their closest allies, they are essentially handing Allah a clear case against themselves. Their actions are testifying that their claim to faith is hollow. This is a powerful psychological deterrent. It frames the choice not just as a sin but as a self-destructive act of providing the prosecution with the evidence for one's own condemnation. The verse calls the believers to examine their circle of intimate friendship, their business partnerships, and their sources of emotional reliance. Are they rooted in shared faith, or are they drawing the heart away from Allah and His community? It teaches that loyalty is a precious commodity that must be reserved for those who share the ultimate purpose of life. This command is not about isolation but about prioritising the bond of faith as the strongest and most defining of all human connections.
145 | Surely the hypocrites will be in the lowest depths of the Fire—and you will never find for them any helper— | The severity of the punishment for hypocrisy is now declared with absolute clarity. “Surely the hypocrites will be in the lowest depths of the Fire.” The Arabic dark al-asfal min al-nār means the very bottom, the deepest pit of Hell. Hypocrisy, being a disease of the innermost heart and a crime of deception against God and His community, merits the most severe punishment. Unlike open disbelievers, the hypocrites had the truth presented to them, outwardly accepted it, and then actively worked to undermine it from within. Their sin combines disbelief with treachery and mockery of the divine. Hence, their station in Hell is the worst of all. This is a consistent Qur’anic theme: the hypocrites are in the lowest part of the Fire because their crime is the most insidious. The verse then strips away any remaining hope they might have clung to: “and you will never find for them any helper.” No intercessor, no ally from among those disbelievers they courted, no worldly power they relied upon will be able to lift them from that abyss or reduce their suffering even slightly. The absolute negation “never find” (lan tajida) emphasizes the eternal hopelessness of their state. The friends they chose instead of the believers will be of no avail. This verse is a terrifying warning that strikes deep into the heart of any believer who might harbour duplicity. It shows that the show of faith is worthless if the heart is diseased with hypocrisy. The only cure is sincere repentance, a complete internal and external alignment with the truth. For the sincere believers, this verse provides comfort: those who secretly worked to harm the community, who mocked and plotted, will not escape divine justice. Their ultimate humiliation is certain, and their schemes will crumble. The verse reinforces that the true hierarchy is not based on worldly status but on spiritual state, and those at the bottom of that hierarchy are the hypocrites, lodged in the deepest pit of the Fire.
146 | except those who repent, mend their ways, hold fast to Allah, and are sincere in their devotion to Allah; they will be with the believers. And Allah will grant the believers a great reward. | The terrible warning of the previous verse is immediately followed by the open door of mercy. “Except those who repent.” The hypocrite is not doomed if they turn back sincerely before death. The verse outlines a path of restoration that involves four steps. First, repentance (tābū), a deep, heartfelt turning away from the sin of hypocrisy and a resolve never to return to it. Second, mending their ways (aṣlaḥū), actively correcting the damage they caused, reforming their character, and aligning their actions with the truth. Third, holding fast to Allah (iʿtaṣamū bi-Allāh), clinging to their faith with firmness, seeking refuge in Allah from doubt and wavering, and making their relationship with Allah the anchor of their life. Fourth, being sincere in their devotion to Allah (akhlaṣū dīnahum lillāh), purifying their faith from all traces of showing off, from hidden agendas, and from mixed motives. Their worship, their obedience, their entire religious life becomes purely for Allah. This fourfold path is a comprehensive rehabilitation program for the hypocritical heart. The verse then pronounces the beautiful outcome: “they will be with the believers.” They are not left in a limbo; they are fully reintegrated into the community of faith. They regain the brotherhood they had forfeited. They are no longer in the lowest depths but will share the company of the sincere. The verse then adds a note of encouragement and promise: “And Allah will grant the believers a great reward.” The repentant, now counted among the believers, are included in this promise of immense, everlasting reward. This verse demonstrates the infinite mercy of Allah, which extends even to the hypocrite who has lived a life of deception, provided they turn back with genuine transformation. It teaches that no matter how far one has strayed, the door back to Allah is open, but the return must be real and comprehensive, involving the heart, the actions, and a pure devotion that will never again be shared with anything but the love and fear of the One True God.
147 | Why should Allah punish you if you are grateful and faithful? Allah is ever Appreciative, All-Knowing. | The surah now poses a beautiful rhetorical question that encapsulates the divine attitude toward the believers. “Why should Allah punish you if you are grateful and faithful?” The question assumes that there is no reason. Allah does not punish without cause. If a person is grateful (shakartum)—acknowledging Allah’s blessings, using them in obedience, and expressing thanks through worship—and faithful (āmantuṃ)—maintaining true belief in their heart—then punishment has no basis. The verse implies that the default relationship between Allah and the believer is one of mercy and reward, not of punishment. Punishment is only for those who reject, rebel, or are ungrateful. This is an immensely comforting and motivating statement. It teaches that the way to avoid divine punishment and secure divine pleasure is beautifully simple in its essence: be grateful for what you have been given, and let your heart be filled with faith. Gratitude and faith are the shields against the Fire. The verse then reveals two divine names that explain why this is so: “Allah is ever Appreciative, All-Knowing.” Shākir means that Allah appreciates and recognises even the smallest good deed. He is not a taskmaster who overlooks effort. When a servant is grateful, Allah appreciates that gratitude and rewards it manifold. ʿAlīm means that He knows the reality of your faith and gratitude.
