He sees the sincerity behind the words and the struggle behind the patience. Therefore, His judgment is perfectly just and perfectly generous. He will not overlook the goodness in a person. This verse is a call to believers to stop living in paralyzing fear and to start living in a state of active gratitude and conscious faith. It teaches that the God we worship is not waiting to pounce on our mistakes but is, instead, eager to appreciate our good and to reward our faithfulness. It shifts the spiritual mindset from mere fear of punishment to a loving, hopeful relationship built on gratitude, knowing that the All-Knowing Lord sees and appreciates the hearts that are turned toward Him.
148 | Allah does not like negative thoughts to be voiced—except by those who have been wronged. Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing. | This verse establishes a crucial rule of public speech and moral conduct. “Allah does not like negative thoughts to be voiced.” The Arabic al-jahr bi al-sūʾi min al-qawl means the public declaration of evil speech. This includes backbiting, slander, cursing, complaining unjustly, and spreading negativity about others. It is not that the thought itself is the primary issue, but the voicing of it, making it public, spreading its harm. Allah does not love that His servants engage in such speech, because it poisons social relationships, spreads corruption, and reflects a diseased heart. However, the verse immediately provides a crucial exception: “except by those who have been wronged.” The person who has been genuinely oppressed has the right to speak out. They can voice their grievance, name their oppressor, and seek help and justice. This exception is the foundation for the right to complaint, the right to testify against injustice, and the right to seek legal redress. The oppressed are not required to suffer in silence. Their speaking out is not considered “negative speech” in the sinful sense, because it is aimed at restoring justice. Nevertheless, even this exception is qualified by Islamic principles: the complaint should be truthful, proportional, and not become a cover for further transgression. The verse closes with “Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.” He hears every word that is uttered, whether the silent prayer of the oppressed or the malicious gossip of the wrongdoer. He knows the intentions behind the words. The one who speaks evil unjustly cannot hide from His hearing, and the one who cries out from injustice is fully heard. This verse teaches the believers to guard their tongues, to avoid the casual spreading of negative speech, and to transform grievances into a principled pursuit of justice rather than idle complaint. It also provides immense comfort to those who have suffered injustice, assuring them that their voice is not only permitted but is heard by the One whose hearing encompasses all, and who will bring about justice in His perfect way and time.
149 | Whether you reveal or conceal a good or pardon an evil—surely Allah is Ever-Pardoning, Most Capable. | This verse continues the theme of speech and conduct by highlighting the flexibility and reward of good deeds, as well as the virtue of forgiveness. “Whether you reveal or conceal a good.” Good deeds can be done openly or in secret, and both are acceptable. Revealing a good deed, such as giving charity publicly, can inspire others and serve as a good example. Concealing it, however, is often better, as it protects one’s sincerity and avoids the pitfalls of showing off. The verse teaches that both methods have their place, and the believer should choose the way that is most pleasing to Allah depending on the circumstance. The verse then adds a powerful moral exhortation: “or pardon an evil.” The ability to forgive those who have wronged you is one of the highest virtues. When someone commits a wrong against you, you have the right to seek justice or retaliation. But the verse points to a superior path: to pardon. To forgive is to act in a godly manner, to rise above the natural desire for revenge and to choose mercy. The verse then concludes by linking this human forgiveness to the divine attributes: “surely Allah is Ever-Pardoning, Most Capable.” The connection is beautiful. Allah is ʿAfuww, the One who loves to pardon His servants. He wants His servants to emulate this attribute by pardoning one another. Furthermore, He is Qadīr, Most Capable. When you forgive, you may feel weak, as if you let the oppressor get away. But this verse reminds you that the true power is with Allah. He is fully capable of taking retribution on your behalf if He wills. Forgiveness is not a sign of weakness but a sign of trust in the Capability of Allah. This verse beautifully weaves together the ethics of charity, sincerity, and forgiveness, all grounded in the character of God. It invites believers to be discreet in their charity, open in their goodness when beneficial, and supremely forgiving of personal wrongs, trusting that the Ever-Pardoning, Most Capable Lord will deal with the rest.
150 | Surely those who deny Allah and His messengers and wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, saying, “We believe in some and disbelieve in others,” desiring to forge a compromise, | The surah now identifies a specific category of disbelievers whose rejection is particularly insidious: those who claim to believe in some prophets while rejecting others. “Surely those who deny Allah and His messengers.” The verse links denial of the messengers directly to denial of Allah, because rejecting the messengers is effectively rejecting the One who sent them. The phrase then exposes their specific error: “and wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers.” They want to believe in God but not in the messengers He has chosen to convey His guidance. This is a logical impossibility, because the authentic knowledge of God comes through the messengers. To claim belief in God while disbelieving in the messengers is to invent a god of one’s own imagination, not the God who sent revelation. The verse then quotes their selective approach: “saying, ‘We believe in some and disbelieve in others.’” This was the position of the Jews who accepted Moses but rejected Jesus and Muhammad, and of the Christians who accepted Jesus but rejected Muhammad. They accept those prophets who fit their cultural or historical preferences and reject those who challenge their status quo. The verse then diagnoses the motivation behind this selective belief: “desiring to forge a compromise.” They seek a middle path, a syncretic religion that blends elements of truth with their own traditions and desires. They want to please both God and their own egos, to create a comfortable spirituality that requires no real submission. This verse is a powerful indictment of the “cafeteria religion” mentality, where one picks and chooses from divine revelation what suits one’s lifestyle. It teaches that true faith is an all-or-nothing submission. One cannot claim to follow God while rejecting the very means He chose to communicate. The verse paves the way for the following verse’s decisive judgment on such people, establishing that their attempt at compromise is, in fact, a complete rejection of the truth.
151 | they are indeed the true disbelievers. And We have prepared for the disbelievers a humiliating punishment. | The previous verse described those who try to distinguish between Allah and His messengers, believing in some and rejecting others. This verse delivers the definitive verdict. “They are indeed the true disbelievers.” The Arabic ulāʾika hum al-kāfirūna ḥaqqan uses a strong emphatic structure. “Indeed” (ḥaqqan, truly) leaves no room for ambiguity. Their selective belief is not a nuanced faith or a sophisticated theology; it is outright, complete disbelief. By rejecting even a single messenger, they have effectively rejected the entire message, because the call of all the messengers is essentially one: to worship Allah alone. Rejecting one is rejecting the principle and the authority of the one who sent them. Thus, the Jews who rejected Jesus and Muhammad, despite accepting Moses, are described as disbelievers in the fullest sense. The same applies to Christians who accept Moses and Jesus but reject Muhammad. Their partial acceptance is a contradiction that nullifies their faith. The verse then states the consequence: “And We have prepared for the disbelievers a humiliating punishment.” The word “humiliating” (muhīn) is significant. Their punishment will not only be physically painful but also deeply degrading. The arrogance and pride that led them to stand as judges over which messengers to accept and which to reject will be shattered. They thought they could negotiate with truth and craft a comfortable compromise, but they will face a disgrace that matches their intellectual and spiritual arrogance. This verse is a sobering reminder that the truth is a unified whole. The chain of prophets from Adam to Muhammad is a single chain of light. To break one link is to reject the entire chain. For the believer, this reinforces the obligation to accept all the prophets, to love them all, and to recognise that the final messenger, Muhammad, peace be upon him, is the completion of that noble brotherhood, and faith in him is inseparable from faith in all that came before.
152 | As for those who believe in Allah and His messengers—accepting all; rejecting none—He will surely give them their rewards. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. | The surah now presents the beautiful opposite of the selective believers described in the previous two verses. “As for those who believe in Allah and His messengers—accepting all; rejecting none.” The true believers are distinguished by their comprehensive faith. They do not differentiate between the messengers in the sense of accepting some and rejecting others. They accept the entire sacred history of prophethood, from Adam to Noah, Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and the final seal, Muhammad, peace be upon them all. Their hearts are wide enough to embrace them all, to love them all, and to believe in their messages in their original, uncorrupted forms. The phrase “rejecting none” (lam yufarriqū bayna aḥadin minhum) emphasizes this integral, undivided belief. For such believers, the promise is absolute: “He will surely give them their rewards.” The Arabic sawfa yuʾtīhim ujūrahum indicates a future, certain bestowal of their full, earned rewards. Their comprehensive faith, their lack of prejudice, and their submission to the whole truth will not go unrecognized. The verse closes with two names that beautifully complement this promise: “And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” Even the comprehensive believer is not perfect. They may have sins and shortcomings. But Allah’s forgiveness (Ghafūr) covers their faults, and His mercy (Raḥīm) envelops them and grants them entry into Paradise by His grace, not merely by their deeds. This verse is a model of Islamic universalism. It calls the believer to a broad, generous, and inclusive faith that honors all of God’s prophets. It counters any sectarian tendency to claim one prophet at the expense of others. It also provides immense hope and comfort to the believer who fears that their sins might outweigh their good deeds. The comprehensive faith, coupled with the comprehensive mercy of Allah, is the path to receiving the full reward. This verse concludes the surah’s discussion on the necessity of believing in all messengers, beautifully balancing the severity of the warning against selective belief with the tenderness of the promise for complete faith.
153 | The People of the Book demand that you ˹O Prophet˺ bring down for them a revelation in writing from heaven. They demanded what is even greater than this from Moses, saying, “Make Allah visible to us!” So a thunderbolt struck them for their wrongdoing. Then they took the calf for worship after receiving clear signs. Still We forgave them for that ˹after their repentance˺ and gave Moses compelling proof. | The surah now addresses the continuous pattern of unreasonable demands from the People of the Book. “The People of the Book demand that you O Prophet bring down for them a revelation in writing from heaven.” The Jews of Medina would ask the Prophet to produce a physical book, a written scripture descending from the sky all at once, like the tablets of the Torah given to Moses. They claimed this would be the only way to verify his prophethood. This was a stubborn, insincere demand, not a genuine search for truth, because if they truly believed in Moses, they should have recognised a prophet with the same message. The verse then reminds the Prophet and the believers that this is not new behaviour: “They demanded what is even greater than this from Moses, saying, ‘Make Allah visible to us!’” The reference is to the incident in Sūrat al-Baqarah when seventy elders of the Israelites, accompanying Moses to Mount Sinai, demanded to see Allah openly before they would believe. Their arrogance reached a pinnacle. “So a thunderbolt struck them for their wrongdoing.” The Arabic ṣāʿiqah, a devastating blast, seized them as a direct punishment. “Then they took the calf for worship after receiving clear signs.” Even after witnessing the parting of the sea and the manna from heaven, and after being saved from the thunderbolt and resurrected, they fell into the worship of the golden calf in Moses’ absence. The verse then highlights the divine mercy that followed: “Still We forgave them for that after their repentance and gave Moses compelling proof.” Despite these enormous transgressions, when they repented, Allah forgave them. The “compelling proof” refers to the miracles and signs given to Moses that established his prophethood beyond doubt. This verse serves multiple purposes. It consoles the Prophet, showing that he is not the first to face unreasonable, arrogant demands. It warns the People of the Book by recalling their own history of stubbornness. And it underscores the infinite patience and mercy of Allah, who forgave the Israelites after their grave sins, offering a path of repentance to the contemporary Jews if they would abandon their demands and submit sincerely.
154 | We raised the Mount over them ˹as a warning˺ for ˹breaking˺ their covenant and said, “Enter the gate ˹of Jerusalem˺ with humility.” We also warned them, “Do not break the Sabbath,” and took from them a firm covenant. | The verse continues recounting the history of the Israelites and the divine measures taken to enforce their covenant. “We raised the Mount over them as a warning for breaking their covenant.” After the Israelites received the Torah, they hesitated and wavered, reluctant to commit to its rigorous laws. In response, Allah raised Mount Sinai above their heads, holding it over them like a canopy, as a physical manifestation of the gravity of the covenant they were about to accept. They were given the choice: accept the covenant or face destruction. This event, described also in Sūrat al-Baqarah, demonstrated the seriousness of the divine pact and left them no room for later excuses. The verse then recalls additional commands: “and said, ‘Enter the gate of Jerusalem with humility.’” When the Israelites were commanded to enter the holy city, they were instructed to do so in a state of humility, bowing and seeking forgiveness, a symbolic act of submission. Instead, many entered arrogantly, changing the words they were told. “We also warned them, ‘Do not break the Sabbath,’ and took from them a firm covenant.” The Sabbath was a sacred trust, a day set aside for worship and rest. The command to honor it was explicit, and violation of it carried severe consequences, as seen when some among them were transformed into apes for their transgressions. The “firm covenant” (mīthāqan ghalīẓan) was the comprehensive agreement that they would uphold the Torah, obey the prophets, and maintain the law. This verse serves to remind the contemporary Jews of Medina, and all who hear the Qur’an, of the long history of divine commands and human defiance. It highlights that the pattern of receiving clear commands, then breaking them, is an old disease, and that the latest revelation is not a new burden but a continuation of the call to honor the covenant with the Creator, this time through the final Messenger.
155 | ˹They were condemned˺ for breaking their covenant, rejecting Allah’s signs, killing the prophets unjustly, and for saying, “Our hearts are unreceptive!”—it is Allah Who has sealed their hearts for their disbelief, so they do not believe except for a few— | The verse now catalogs the specific crimes that led to the condemnation of the Israelites. “They were condemned for breaking their covenant.” This covenant was the solemn agreement to uphold the Torah and obey the prophets. They broke it repeatedly through disobedience, distortion of scripture, and rejection of the messengers. “Rejecting Allah’s signs” refers to their denial of the miracles, the clear proofs, and the prophets who came with evident truth. “Killing the prophets unjustly” is one of the gravest charges. The history of the Israelites is tragically marked by the murder of prophets who called them to righteousness, including Zachariah and John the Baptist, and their attempts to kill Jesus. This murder of the messengers of God is the ultimate expression of rebellion. The verse then quotes a statement of their arrogance: “and for saying, ‘Our hearts are unreceptive!’” They claimed that their hearts were already full of knowledge and did not need any more guidance. They pretended that their rejection was not due to stubbornness but to sufficiency. This is a perverse form of pride, wrapping rejection in the guise of self-contentment. The verse then reveals the divine consequence of these accumulated sins: “it is Allah Who has sealed their hearts for their disbelief.” The sealing of the heart is not an arbitrary act of cruelty; it is the direct result of their persistent, willful rejection. When a person repeatedly chooses to reject the truth, their heart gradually loses the ability to accept it. The sealing is the completion of that process. “So they do not believe except for a few.” Only a small number among them were sincere enough to overcome the cultural and psychological barriers and embrace the truth, such as ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām and others. This verse is a stark warning about the deadly progression of sin. It shows that persistent rejection, mockery of prophets, and spiritual arrogance do not go unpunished; they lead to a state where the heart is locked and guidance can no longer penetrate.
156 | and for their denial and outrageous accusation against Mary, | The list of the grave sins of the Israelites continues with a specific and shocking charge. “And for their denial and outrageous accusation against Mary.” The “denial” refers to their rejection of the miraculous birth of Jesus and his prophethood. But the verse focuses especially on the “outrageous accusation” (buhtānan ʿaẓīman). This refers to the slander that some Jews directed at Maryam (Mary), the pure and chaste mother of Jesus. When Mary returned with the infant Jesus, some among them accused her of fornication, a heinous lie against a woman of unparalleled piety who had been chosen by Allah and purified. This slander was not only an attack on Mary herself but also a denial of the miraculous virgin birth, which was a sign of Allah’s power. By accusing Mary of unchastity, they sought to undermine the entire legitimacy of Jesus’ prophethood from its very root. The verse lists this alongside the killing of prophets and the breaking of covenants, showing that moral corruption, such as slandering chaste women, is among the most severe of sins. In Islamic theology, Maryam holds an exalted status. She is the only woman mentioned by name in the Qur’an, and an entire surah is named after her. Her purity and devotion are beyond question. The Jewish accusation against her is thus presented as a massive calumny, a buhtān that reflects the depths to which those who rejected Allah’s signs could stoop. This verse, placed before the direct discussion of Jesus’ crucifixion, prepares the ground. It shows that the Jewish rejection of Jesus was not just a theological disagreement; it was accompanied by a campaign of slander against his mother. It serves as a reminder to the believers of the sanctity of a person’s honor, especially that of a righteous woman, and the gravity of sexual slander. It also connects to the surah’s earlier emphasis on protecting the reputation of chaste women.
157 | and for boasting, “We killed the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, the messenger of Allah.” But they neither killed nor crucified him—it was only made to appear so. Even those who argue for this ˹crucifixion˺ are in doubt. They have no knowledge whatsoever—only making assumptions. They certainly did not kill him. | This is the crux of the surah’s discussion of Jesus, one of the most theologically significant verses regarding the crucifixion in the Qur’an. The verse begins by quoting the boast of some Jews: “and for boasting, ‘We killed the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, the messenger of Allah.’” Their boast is recorded in all its arrogance. They claimed to have killed the one they had rejected, and they used his full title—Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary—perhaps mockingly. The verse then delivers the divine refutation with absolute clarity: “But they neither killed nor crucified him—it was only made to appear so.” This is the core statement. The historical event of the crucifixion, as perceived by the onlookers, was not what it seemed. Jesus was not the one on the cross. Allah saved him and raised him up, and another person was made to resemble him, or the event was shrouded in divine illusion. The exact mechanics are part of the unseen; the Qur’an does not elaborate on the “how” of the resemblance. The verse then dismantles the certainty of those who claim otherwise: “Even those who argue for this crucifixion are in doubt. They have no knowledge whatsoever—only making assumptions.” The Christians who believe in the crucifixion, and the Jews who boast of it, are all following conjecture, not certain knowledge. The historical accounts are conflicting, and the reality is known only to Allah. The verse ends with a powerful repetition: “They certainly did not kill him.” This triple negation—neither killed, nor crucified, and certainly did not kill—emphasizes the certainty of the divine truth over all human speculation. This verse is a cornerstone of the Islamic position on Jesus. It honors him by denying his humiliating death at the hands of his enemies. It removes the foundation of the doctrines of atonement and original sin, which are built on the crucifixion. And it calls all people to recognize that the truth about Jesus is not found in the confused historical narratives but in the clear, certain revelation given to the final Messenger.
158 | Rather, Allah raised him up to Himself. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise. | After negating the crucifixion, the verse provides the positive truth of what happened to Jesus. “Rather, Allah raised him up to Himself.” The Arabic rafaʿahu Allāhu ilayhi is a beautiful and honorific expression. Jesus was not killed or humiliated; he was exalted and elevated by Allah to the heavens. The phrase “to Himself” indicates a special nearness, an honor bestowed upon this noble prophet. Jesus was saved from the plot of his enemies and taken up, alive in body and soul, into the divine presence. This ascension is a sign of Allah’s protection of His righteous servants and His power over all schemes. The verse then closes with two divine names that perfectly contextualize this event: “And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.” ʿAzīz means the Almighty, the One who cannot be overpowered. The enemies of Jesus believed they had triumphed, but Allah, the Almighty, demonstrated that their power was an illusion. His plan superseded theirs. Ḥakīm means the All-Wise. The raising of Jesus was not just a display of power; it was part of a profound divine wisdom. It preserved Jesus for his future role, including his return near the end of time, as indicated in authentic hadith. It also served as a test for humanity: would they believe the divine account, or would they fabricate complex theologies of death and resurrection? This verse is a source of immense comfort and confidence for the believers. It shows that Allah protects His prophets, that the ultimate victory belongs to the righteous, and that what appears to be a worldly defeat can be, in the divine scheme, a glorious elevation. The believer facing persecution and apparent defeat can look to this verse and know that the Almighty and All-Wise Lord can raise them in honor even when the world thinks they are crushed. It affirms the living, ascended status of Jesus and sets the stage for the following verse about his return.
159 | Every one of the People of the Book will definitely believe in him before his death. And on the Day of Judgment Jesus will be a witness against them. | This verse contains a significant eschatological prophecy. “Every one of the People of the Book will definitely believe in him before his death.” The pronoun “his” has been interpreted in two main ways. The first, and more widely accepted interpretation, is that the pronoun refers to Jesus. Before Jesus dies, after his return to earth at the end of time, every Jew and Christian alive at that time will believe in him correctly—as a noble prophet and servant of Allah, not as God or as a false prophet. This will unite the People of the Book under the true faith. The second interpretation is that the pronoun refers to each individual among the People of the Book: at the moment of their own death, every Jew and Christian will realize the truth about Jesus, but that realization at the point of death will not benefit them. Both interpretations carry weight, but the first is more supported by the context and other textual evidence. The verse then states the role of Jesus on the Day of Judgment: “And on the Day of Judgment Jesus will be a witness against them.” Jesus will testify about his people. He will declare the truth of his message—that he was a servant and messenger of Allah, and that he never claimed divinity. He will bear witness against those who deified him and against those who rejected him. This testimony will be decisive. The verse serves multiple purposes. For the believers, it is a reassurance that Jesus’ true status will be universally acknowledged, and that the disputes about him will be resolved. For Christians and Jews, it is a call to examine their beliefs about Jesus now, during the time when faith can still benefit them, rather than waiting for a time when belief will be too late. It ties the entire discussion of Jesus back to the surah’s theme of witnessing and accountability, reminding everyone that the ultimate judgment belongs to Allah, and His prophet Jesus will be a key witness in that divine court.
160 | We forbade the Jews certain foods that had been lawful to them for their wrongdoing, and for hindering many from the Way of Allah, | The surah now transitions from the theological discussion of Jesus to the practical consequences of the Jews' transgressions, specifically the dietary restrictions imposed as punishment. “We forbade the Jews certain foods that had been lawful to them for their wrongdoing.” This refers to the prohibitions mentioned in the Torah, such as the forbidding of certain fats, animals with claws, and other items that had previously been permitted. These were not part of the original, universal divine law; they were specific punitive measures for the Children of Israel because of their persistent rebellion and injustice. The verse explains the reason: “for their wrongdoing” (bi-ẓulmin minhum). The word ẓulm encompasses their many sins: breaking the covenant, rejecting the prophets, indulging in usury, devouring people’s wealth unjustly, and all the crimes mentioned earlier in this passage. The dietary restrictions were a form of discipline, a yoke placed upon them as a consequence of their disobedience, making their religious practice more burdensome. The verse then adds a second reason: “and for hindering many from the Way of Allah.” They not only sinned themselves but actively prevented others from embracing the path of truth. They misrepresented the scriptures, hid the prophecies about the coming Prophet, and erected obstacles in the spiritual journey of the sincere seekers among both their own people and the Arabs. Their hindrance (ṣadd) was a major crime, multiplying their guilt. This verse provides a powerful lesson in the nature of divine law. Hardships and restrictions can sometimes be a consequence of collective sin. The mercy of Islam is evident in that the final law, the Shariah of Muhammad, is described elsewhere as a removal of burdens and shackles. It is a return to a purer, more merciful law. The verse also warns believers that sin, especially when coupled with hindering others from guidance, can lead to the loss of divine blessings and the imposition of constraints, both spiritual and material. It sets the stage for the following verses that continue to detail the Jewish transgressions, particularly the taking of usury and consuming wealth unjustly.
161 | taking interest despite its prohibition, and consuming people’s wealth unjustly. We have prepared for the disbelievers among them a painful punishment. | The previous verse mentioned that certain foods were forbidden to the Jews as a consequence of their wrongdoing. This verse continues enumerating the specific transgressions that led to that punishment and their broader moral failures. First among them is “taking interest despite its prohibition.” The Arabic word is ribā, commonly translated as usury or interest, and it was explicitly forbidden in the Torah. Despite the clear divine command, many among the Jews engaged in ribā, exploiting the needy and profiting from the financial distress of others. This economic oppression was a direct violation of the covenant and a symptom of their hardened hearts, placing material gain above obedience to God. The verse then adds a second, broader sin: “and consuming people’s wealth unjustly.” This covers every form of illicit financial gain: bribery, fraud, embezzlement, and the misappropriation of the property of orphans and the weak. The phrase “unjustly” (bi al-bāṭil) means through falsehood and without any rightful claim. Together, these two crimes—interest and the unjust devouring of wealth—reveal a society where the powerful preyed upon the vulnerable, and where the love of wealth had supplanted the fear of God. The verse then declares the consequence: “We have prepared for the disbelievers among them a painful punishment.” The specification “among them” is significant. It indicates that the punishment is not for all Jews indiscriminately, but for those who persisted in these grave sins and thus demonstrated disbelief in practice. The punishment is described as “painful” (alīman), emphasizing the severe suffering that awaits them in the Hereafter for their unrepentant economic exploitation. This verse offers a powerful lesson for all communities: economic injustice, particularly the taking of interest forbidden by Allah, is not a minor ethical lapse but a major sin that can lead to a painful divine punishment. It also serves as a warning to the Muslim community to uphold justice in all financial dealings, following the detailed inheritance and charity laws given earlier in this very surah.
162 | But those of them well-grounded in knowledge, the faithful ˹who˺ believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you—˹especially˺ those who establish prayer— and those who pay alms-tax and believe in Allah and the Last Day, to these ˹people˺ We will grant a great reward. | After the severe condemnation of the sinful among the People of the Book, this verse opens the door of honour and reward for the righteous among them. It begins by describing the first group: “those of them well-grounded in knowledge.” The Arabic al-rāsikhūna fī al-ʿilm refers to scholars deeply rooted in authentic scriptural knowledge, whose learning leads them to humility and recognition of the truth rather than arrogance and denial. These are not the ignorant masses nor the corrupt leaders, but the sincere intellectuals and spiritual seekers among the Jews and Christians. The verse then expands: “the faithful who believe in what has been revealed to you O Prophet and what was revealed before you.” This describes those who combine belief in the Qur’an with belief in the original Torah and Gospel. They do not see the final revelation as a contradiction but as a confirmation and completion, exactly as the earlier scriptures had foretold. Their faith is seamless. The verse then highlights specific practices: “especially those who establish prayer—and those who pay alms-tax and believe in Allah and the Last Day.” These are the concrete manifestations of their comprehensive faith. They establish prayer (ṣalāh), the central act of worship connecting them to Allah; they pay the alms-tax (zakāh), fulfilling their duty to the poor; and their core belief is anchored in Allah and the Last Day, the foundation of all accountability. For such individuals, the promise is magnificent: “to these people We will grant a great reward.” The reward is not a grudging acceptance but a truly great and abundant recompense. This verse powerfully illustrates that salvation is not about ethnic or religious labels but about sincere knowledge, comprehensive faith, and righteous deeds. It honors those among the People of the Book who, like ʿAbdullāh ibn Salām, recognized the truth of the Prophet and embraced Islam fully, while also acknowledging that their journey to faith was built on the authentic remnants of their own scriptures.
163 | Indeed, We have sent revelation to you ˹O Prophet˺ as We sent revelation to Noah and the prophets after him. We also sent revelation to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants, ˹as well as˺ Jesus, Job, Jonah, Aaron, and Solomon. And to David We gave the Psalms. | Having affirmed the believers among the People of the Book, the surah now establishes the unbroken chain of revelation that culminates in the Prophet Muhammad. The verse opens with the emphatic “Indeed, We have sent revelation to you O Prophet as We sent revelation to Noah and the prophets after him.” The revelation to Muhammad is not an anomaly or a break in divine communication; it is a continuation of the same sacred process that began with the first messenger after Adam, Noah. By mentioning Noah, the verse reaches back to the earliest roots of prophetic history, emphasizing the unity of the message. The verse then proceeds to list a number of prominent prophets, grouping them in a significant order. “Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants” covers the great patriarchs and the twelve tribes. The mention of Ishmael alongside Isaac is a direct affirmation of the Arab branch of Abrahamic lineage, countering those who denied prophethood to the Arabs. Then comes “Jesus, Job, Jonah, Aaron, and Solomon,” a selection that includes prophets associated with patience (Job and Jonah), priesthood and support (Aaron), and magnificent kingship (Solomon), as well as Jesus, the central figure of the preceding discussion. Finally, the verse singles out David: “And to David We gave the Psalms.” The Psalms (Zabūr) are explicitly mentioned as a revealed scripture, a book of wisdom and praise. This entire list serves to remind both the Muslims and the People of the Book that the Prophet Muhammad is not a stranger to the prophetic tradition; he stands in a noble line of divinely guided men, receiving the same fundamental revelation: the call to the worship of the One God. For the believer, this verse fosters a profound love and respect for all the prophets, and a deep sense of belonging to a community of faith that stretches across all of human history.
164 | There are messengers whose stories We have told you already and others We have not. And to Moses Allah spoke directly. | The verse continues the theme of the comprehensive nature of prophethood, now acknowledging that the list given in the previous verse is not exhaustive. “There are messengers whose stories We have told you already and others We have not.” The Qur’an contains detailed narratives of many prophets—Adam, Noah, Abraham, Lot, Moses, Jesus, and others—but there are many more whom Allah sent throughout history, to every nation, whose names and stories are not recounted in the Qur’an. This is a profound statement. It teaches the believers that divine guidance is not limited to the Middle Eastern region or to the specific prophets they know by name. The mercy of Allah has reached all corners of humanity. It also protects the believer from the arrogance of thinking that their knowledge encompasses all of God’s dealings with humanity. There is a humility in recognizing that the divine plan is far vaster than what has been revealed to us in detail. The verse then highlights the unique status of one particular messenger: “And to Moses Allah spoke directly.” The Arabic kallama Allāhu Mūsā taklīman uses a verbal emphasis that indicates direct, unmediated speech. While all prophets received revelation, Moses was given the distinct honor of hearing Allah’s speech without the intermediary of an angel. This is why Moses is known as Kalīm Allāh, the one to whom Allah spoke. This verse, while acknowledging the countless unknown messengers, elevates the known ones by singling out Moses’ special distinction. It ties the entire discussion back to the earlier verses about the Torah and the covenant with the Israelites. The verse provides a balanced perspective: the Muslim community is part of a vast, global history of revelation, yet they are also recipients of a final, clear message, rooted in the same tradition that gave Moses the Torah and the direct speech of the Almighty.
165 | ˹All were˺ messengers delivering good news and warnings so humanity should have no excuse before Allah after ˹the coming of˺ the messengers. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise. | This verse explains the unified purpose of all the messengers mentioned in the previous verses. “All were messengers delivering good news and warnings.” Every prophet was sent with a dual message: bashīr (bearer of glad tidings) and nadhīr (warner). The good news is for those who believe and do good, promising them Paradise and divine pleasure. The warning is for those who reject and disobey, alerting them to punishment and loss. This balance of hope and fear is the hallmark of all prophetic missions. It ensures that people are not driven solely by terror or lured solely by promise, but are moved by a combination that reflects the reality of a merciful but just Creator. The verse then states the profound divine purpose behind this universal sending of messengers: “so humanity should have no excuse before Allah after the coming of the messengers.” This is the principle of iqāmat al-ḥujjah, the establishment of proof. Allah, in His perfect justice, does not punish any people until He has sent them a messenger who clearly explains the truth and warns them of falsehood. After the messenger delivers the message, there is no longer any room for the plea of ignorance. Those who reject do so against clear evidence. This establishes the absolute fairness of the divine judgment on the Day of Resurrection. No soul will be able to say, “I did not know,” because Allah, in His mercy, sent guidance to every corner of the earth. The verse concludes with “And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.” The Almighty (ʿAzīz) sent messengers with a message that cannot be defeated, and He will hold humanity accountable with irresistible power. The All-Wise (Ḥakīm) designed this system of sending messengers as the perfect, wise method to guide free-willed beings, balancing mercy with justice. This verse fills the believer with gratitude for the gift of the Prophet and with a sense of responsibility, knowing that the excuse of ignorance has been removed.
166 | Yet ˹if you are denied, O Prophet,˺ Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you—He has sent it with His knowledge. The angels too bear witness. And Allah ˹alone˺ is sufficient as a Witness. | After describing the purpose of the messengers, the verse directly addresses the Prophet with a comforting reassurance in the face of denial. “Yet if you are denied, O Prophet, Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you.” The denial of the disbelievers does not diminish the truth of the revelation. The ultimate witness is not human acceptance or rejection, but the testimony of Allah Himself. When the Creator of the universe testifies to the truth of a message, any human denial becomes utterly insignificant. This is a profound spiritual anchor for the Prophet and for every believer who faces rejection. The verse then explains the nature of this divine testimony: “He has sent it with His knowledge.” The revelation was not sent haphazardly or from a position of ignorance. It was sent down, saturated with Allah’s perfect, all-encompassing knowledge. Every word, every ruling, every historical account within the Qur’an is infused with divine wisdom and absolute accuracy. The knowledge of the One who knows the unseen, the past, and the future is the guarantee of the Qur’an’s truth. “The angels too bear witness.” The noble angels, who are witnesses to the descent of revelation from the Preserved Tablet to the lowest heaven and then to the heart of the Prophet, also testify to its divine origin. Their pure testimony adds another layer of confirmation. The verse then concludes with a powerful statement: “And Allah alone is sufficient as a Witness.” Even if all of humanity and the angels were to deny it, Allah’s testimony is enough. He is the ultimate, self-sufficient Witness whose acknowledgment validates everything. This verse is a fortress of faith for the believer. It teaches that our conviction in the truth of Islam should not be shaken by the disbelief of others, because the truth does not rest on majority opinion but on the unassailable witness of the All-Knowing Lord.
167 | Those who disbelieve and hinder ˹others˺ from the Way of Allah have certainly strayed far away. | After affirming the divine witness to the truth of the revelation, the verse turns its focus back to the disbelievers, describing the depth of their misguidance. “Those who disbelieve and hinder others from the Way of Allah.” The verse identifies two distinct but often linked crimes. The first is personal disbelief (kafarū): they reject the truth for themselves. The second, and even more destructive, is active hindrance (ṣaddū): they work to prevent others from reaching the path of Allah. They do not merely keep their disbelief private; they become obstacles, using mockery, arguments, persecution, and falsehood to block others from the truth. This dual crime amplifies their guilt tremendously, as they are not only responsible for their own misguidance but also share in the misguidance of every soul they lead astray. The verse then pronounces the verdict on their state: “have certainly strayed far away.” The Arabic ḍallū ḍalālan baʿīdan is a strong emphasis. Their straying is not a minor deviation but a deep, distant wandering from the straight path. The word “far away” (baʿīd) indicates that the distance between them and the truth is immense, and the road back is long and difficult. This is the natural consequence of a heart that first rejects and then actively opposes. Each act of hindrance pushes them further into the wilderness of misguidance. This verse serves as a warning to the believers to never become a hindrance to others. It also provides a sobering analysis of why some rejectors are treated with particular severity in the Qur’an: because their sin extends beyond themselves. For the Muslim, it reinforces the duty not only to be guided but to facilitate guidance for others, to be a means of opening the path, not blocking it. Conversely, it warns against those whose influence in society leads people away from the faith, marking their deviation as extreme.
168 | Those who disbelieve and wrong themselves—surely Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to any path | The severity of the language intensifies. “Those who disbelieve and wrong themselves.” The disbelievers are also ẓālimūn, wrongdoers. Their primary wrong is the oppression of their own souls by denying the truth and suppressing their innate disposition toward faith. Every act of disbelief is an act of self-destruction, a wrong committed against the very self that is their most precious possession. The verse then delivers one of the most terrifying verdicts in the Qur’an: “surely Allah will neither forgive them nor guide them to any path.” This is the state of those who have gone so far in their rejection and self-wronging that the doors of mercy and guidance are closed to them. They have died in a state of willful, persistent disbelief, having been given the clear message and ample time to respond. For such people, there is no forgiveness, because they have rejected the very source of forgiveness. And there is no guidance to any path—not just the path to Paradise, but no path of escape, no alternative route to safety. They are completely stranded in their spiritual ruin. The verse echoes the earlier statement in this surah about the hypocrites who oscillated in faith, but it applies universally to all who persist in disbelief and die upon it. It serves as the ultimate warning, a sobering counterbalance to the verses of mercy that precede and follow it. It is meant to shake the reader to the core, urging an immediate and sincere return to faith, because the state described here is final and irreversible. For the believer, this verse fosters a profound gratitude for the gift of faith and a deep fear of ever straying, recognizing that the consequences of disbelief are not just a temporary setback but an eternal, total loss from which there is no recovery.
169 | except that of Hell, to stay there for ever and ever. And that is easy for Allah. | The previous verse stated that the disbelieving wrongdoers will not be guided to any path. This verse completes the statement by revealing the only path that remains for them. “Except that of Hell.” The particle “except” (illā) indicates that the negation of any path is not absolute in the sense of annihilation; there is one path left, and it is the most terrible one. They are not guided to the path of repentance, not to the path of Paradise, not to any path of escape or relief. The only road they will travel is the road that descends into the Fire. The verse then emphasizes the eternal nature of their stay: “to stay there for ever and ever.” The Arabic khālidīna fīhā abadan uses a double emphasis to remove any possibility of the punishment ending. This eternity is the most terrifying aspect of Hell; it is not a rehabilitative sentence but a permanent state. The verse then adds a phrase that magnifies the power of the Judge: “And that is easy for Allah.” The eternal punishment, which to human comprehension seems an infinite, crushing undertaking, is effortless for the Almighty. His power is not strained by sustaining the Fire and its inhabitants for eternity. This statement serves as a direct refutation of any notion that the punishment described is too extreme or impossible. It reminds the disbeliever that the One they have defied is capable of this and more, without any difficulty. For the believer, this verse instils a deep awe of Allah’s power and a profound fear of His punishment. It also, paradoxically, contains a comforting element: the same Allah for whom this is easy is also the One whose mercy is vast and whose entrance into Paradise is easy for those who believe. The ease of divine action is a double-edged sword, motivating both fear and hope. The believer is called to take the path of ease that leads to mercy, not the path of ease that leads to eternal justice.
170 | O humanity! The Messenger has certainly come to you with the truth from your Lord, so believe for your own good. But if you disbelieve, then ˹know that˺ to Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise. | After the terrifying depiction of Hell, the surah turns to a universal, merciful call. “O humanity! The Messenger has certainly come to you with the truth from your Lord.” This is a direct address to all people, across all times, announcing the undeniable fact of the Prophet’s arrival with the clear, unadulterated truth. The message is not a human philosophy or a cultural invention; it is “from your Lord,” who is the Creator and Sustainer of all. The phrase “the truth” (al-ḥaqq) means that the message is factually correct, morally upright, and divinely guaranteed. The verse then gives the rationale for belief: “so believe for your own good.” Allah does not need your faith; you need it. Belief is not a favour you do for God; it is the sole means of your own salvation, purification, and eternal happiness. This simple statement reframes the entire call to faith. It is not a burdensome demand but a generous offer for your own ultimate benefit. The verse then addresses the opposite choice: “But if you disbelieve, then know that to Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth.” Disbelief harms only the disbeliever. It does not diminish Allah’s dominion, power, or majesty in the slightest. The entire universe is His, and He is not in need of any creature’s worship. The verse concludes with a pair of divine names that have recurred throughout the surah: “And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.” He knows who believes and who rejects, and the wisdom behind the entire system of faith and accountability. This verse serves as a summary of the entire message: it is true, it is from your Lord, it is for your benefit, and your rejection of it is a loss only to yourself, while the Lord remains transcendent and unaffected. It is a final, open-handed invitation, stripped of all threat and grounded in pure logic and mercy.
171 | O People of the Book! Do not go to extremes regarding your faith; say nothing about Allah except the truth. The Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger of Allah and the fulfilment of His Word through Mary and a spirit ˹created by a command˺ from Him. So believe in Allah and His messengers and do not say, “Trinity.” Stop!—for your own good. Allah is only One God. Glory be to Him! He is far above having a son! To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs. | This verse is a comprehensive theological address to the Christians, correcting the excesses that led to the doctrine of the Trinity and the divinity of Jesus. It opens with a direct call: “O People of the Book! Do not go to extremes regarding your faith.” The Arabic lā taghlū means to exceed the proper bounds, to exaggerate. The Christians are warned against the extreme of deifying their prophet and against the opposite extreme of the Jews in rejecting him. The command is clear: “say nothing about Allah except the truth.” All speech concerning God must be confined to what He has revealed and what is true, not what is fabricated by human speculation. The verse then provides the correct, balanced truth about Jesus. “The Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger of Allah.” This negates his divinity. He is a messenger, a human servant, not God. Then, “and the fulfilment of His Word through Mary.” The “Word” is the divine command “Be,” through which Jesus was created in Mary’s womb without a father. This is a miraculous act of creation, not an indication of divinity. “And a spirit created by a command from Him.” Jesus is a rūḥ, a spirit, meaning his creation was a direct, special act of Allah’s will. But he is a created spirit, not the uncreated God. The verse then delivers the core command: “So believe in Allah and His messengers and do not say, ‘Trinity.’” The explicit prohibition of the term “Trinity” (thalāthah) is a direct refutation of the central Christian doctrine. The verse immediately follows with “Stop!—for your own good.” It is a merciful, urgent call to desist from a belief that leads to eternal ruin. The verse then reaffirms the pure monotheism: “Allah is only One God. Glory be to Him! He is far above having a son!” The transcendence of Allah is proclaimed. He is too exalted to have offspring, which is a biological attribute of creation. Finally, the ownership of the universe and the divine sufficiency are reiterated: “To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is sufficient as a Trustee of Affairs.” The verse dismantles the theological basis of Christian doctrine with absolute clarity, replacing it with the pure monotheism of Islam, and it does so as a call to safety and truth.
172 | The Messiah would never be too proud to be a servant of Allah, nor would the angels nearest to Allah. Those who are too proud and arrogant to worship Him will be brought before Him all together. | Continuing the address to the Christians, the verse presents a powerful argument from the example of the most exalted beings. “The Messiah would never be too proud to be a servant of Allah.” The Christians who elevated Jesus to divinity did so with a misplaced sense of honoring him. But the verse declares that the true honor of Jesus lies precisely in his servitude to Allah, and that Jesus himself would never disdain that title. Being a servant of the Most High is the highest possible rank for any created being. The verse then extends this to the highest ranks of creation: “nor would the angels nearest to Allah.” The angels brought near (al-muqarrabūn), the most honored and closest beings to the divine throne, do not consider themselves too great to serve and worship Allah. If Jesus and the archangels are not too proud to be servants, what justification does any human have for refusing to bow to their Creator? The verse then transitions from the example of the humble to the fate of the arrogant: “Those who are too proud and arrogant to worship Him will be brought before Him all together.” The proud, both among humans and jinn, those who scoffed at the idea of submission, will be gathered on the Day of Judgment not as honored guests but as humiliated prisoners. Their arrogance will be stripped away, and they will stand before the One they refused to worship, facing His judgment. The phrase “all together” (jamīʿan) implies a comprehensive, inescapable gathering. No one who displayed arrogance will be missed. This verse beautifully ties together the surah’s theology of servitude. The most noble of creatures—the Messiah, the angels—are proud to be slaves of Allah. This is the ultimate honor. The true loser is the one who, out of false pride, refuses to join this company, and will instead be dragged before Allah as a defendant. The verse calls every human to embrace ʿubūdiyyah, servitude to God, as the path of the noble, following the example of the very best of creation.
173 | As for those who believe and do good, He will reward them in full and increase them out of His grace. But those who are too proud and arrogant, He will subject them to a painful punishment. And besides Allah they will find no protector or helper. | This verse presents the final contrast between the two paths: humble servitude and arrogant refusal. “As for those who believe and do good, He will reward them in full and increase them out of His grace.” The believers who combine inner faith with outward righteous action are promised two things. First, the complete fulfillment of their due reward (yuwaffīhim ujūrahum). No good deed will be lost or diminished. The justice of Allah ensures that they receive exactly what they earned. Second, an increase (yazīduhum) from His grace. This is the unbounded, unearned bounty of Allah, which multiplies rewards, grants higher stations, and bestows pleasures beyond imagination. This addition is a pure gift, reflecting the nature of a generous Lord who loves to exceed the expectations of His faithful servants. The verse then turns to the opposite: “But those who are too proud and arrogant, He will subject them to a painful punishment.” Their sin is not just unbelief but istinkāf, a disdainful refusal and arrogance. They considered themselves above bowing to the Creator. Their punishment will match their crime: they will be subjected to a “painful punishment” that will break their false pride and leave them in utter humiliation. The verse concludes with a repetition of the dire consequence: “And besides Allah they will find no protector or helper.” Their arrogance in this world led them to rely on themselves, their wealth, their patrons, or their false gods. But on that Day, all those supports will vanish. There will be no walī (protector) to shield them and no naṣīr (helper) to intercede or rescue them. They will be utterly alone, facing the wrath of the Almighty. This verse is a masterful summation of the surah’s central choice: humble, obedient faith leads to a reward that overflows with extra grace, while arrogant rejection leads to a punishment from which there is no escape.
174 | O humanity! There has come to you conclusive evidence from your Lord. And We have sent down to you a brilliant light. | The surah now delivers another universal address, similar to verse 170, summarizing the entire revelation as the ultimate proof and guidance. “O humanity! There has come to you conclusive evidence from your Lord.” The Arabic burhān means a clear, undeniable proof, an evidence that establishes the truth beyond any reasonable doubt. This “conclusive evidence” is the Qur’an itself, the final scripture, accompanied by the life and character of the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him. It is described as coming “from your Lord,” emphasizing that it is not a human invention but a direct communication from the Creator to His creation. This burhān leaves no room for the excuse of ignorance; the light has been lit, and whoever wishes to see can see. The verse then uses a powerful metaphor: “And We have sent down to you a brilliant light.” The Arabic nūran mubīnan means a light that is clear in itself and illuminating for everything else. The Qur’an is this light. In a world of darkness—darkness of ignorance, doubt, superstition, and moral confusion—the revelation is a brilliant light that makes the path clear. It allows the human being to distinguish truth from falsehood, good from evil, and the straight path from the crooked. The light not only illuminates the destination but also provides warmth, guidance, and hope. This verse is a declaration that the age of ambiguity is over. The evidence has been established, the light has been sent. Humanity is now divided between those who open their eyes to this light and walk in it, and those who turn away and remain in darkness. The verse serves as a final, gracious invitation, full of hope and clarity. It tells every human being: the truth you have been seeking, the proof you have been demanding, the light you need for your journey—it has arrived. What remains is your response.
175 | As for those who believe in Allah and hold fast to Him, He will admit them into His mercy and grace and guide them to Himself through the Straight Path. | The surah concludes the universal call with a beautiful promise that summarizes the entire spiritual journey. “As for those who believe in Allah and hold fast to Him.” The phrase “hold fast to Him” translates yaʿtaṣimū bi-Allāh. The root ʿaṣama means to grip firmly, to seek refuge and protection, to cling with all one’s strength. A believer is not merely someone who has a passive, intellectual assent; they are someone who actively clings to Allah, like a drowning person clinging to a rope. This image of active, desperate dependence is the essence of true faith. The verse then describes the three stages of the divine reward. First, “He will admit them into His mercy.” The believer is enveloped in the vast mercy of Allah, which is the source of all good and the shelter from all evil. Second, “and grace” (faḍl). Beyond mercy, there is an added bounty, a special favor that is not merely the removal of harm but the bestowal of overwhelming blessings and delights. Third, “and guide them to Himself through the Straight Path.” This is the ultimate goal: arrival to Allah. The Straight Path (ṣirāṭ mustaqīm) is not just a road to Paradise; it is the road that leads to the divine presence itself. The verb “guide them” (yahdīhim) indicates that Allah will continue to guide them on this path until they reach their destination. This guidance includes the journey of this life, the moment of death, the stages of the Hereafter, and the final entry into the vision of Allah and His eternal nearness. This verse beautifully ties together the surah’s themes of faith, action, reliance, and divine reward. It presents the complete package of salvation: belief, clinging to Allah, and the threefold reward of mercy, grace, and guidance. It is a fitting conclusion to a surah that has detailed the laws and ethics that make up the Straight Path, ending with the promise that those who walk this path will be brought home, to the Lord who guided them all along.
176 | They ask you ˹for a ruling, O Prophet˺. Say, “Allah gives you a ruling regarding those who die without children or parents.” If a man dies childless and leaves behind a sister, she will inherit one-half of his estate, whereas her brother will inherit all of her estate if she dies childless. If this person leaves behind two sisters, they together will inherit two-thirds of the estate. But if the deceased leaves male and female siblings, a male’s share will be equal to that of two females. Allah makes ˹this˺ clear to you so you do not go astray. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things. | The surah concludes by returning to its detailed theme of inheritance, specifically addressing the case of kalālah, a person who dies leaving neither parents nor children. The verse begins: “They ask you for a ruling, O Prophet.” The companions sought clarity on the inheritance of such a person, and the Prophet is told to respond, “Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling regarding those who die without children or parents.’” The ruling is from Allah, not from the Prophet’s own opinion, ensuring its divine authority. The verse then lays out the shares for siblings in this specific case. First, “If a man dies childless and leaves behind a sister, she will inherit one-half of his estate.” The full sister gets half. Second, “whereas her brother will inherit all of her estate if she dies childless.” If the sister dies in similar circumstances and leaves a full brother, he inherits everything. Third, “If this person leaves behind two sisters, they together will inherit two-thirds of the estate.” Two or more sisters share two-thirds. Fourth, “But if the deceased leaves male and female siblings, a male’s share will be equal to that of two females.” When the siblings are a mix of brothers and sisters, the distribution follows the familiar principle of the male receiving twice the share of the female, reflecting the greater financial responsibilities placed on men in Islamic law. This detailed ruling on kalālah fills the gap left by the earlier inheritance verses and provides a complete, just system. The verse closes with a powerful affirmation: “Allah makes this clear to you so you do not go astray. And Allah has perfect knowledge of all things.” The clarity of the divine law is a mercy meant to prevent error and dispute. The verse ends where the surah’s inheritance discussion began—with the emphasis that these are limits set by the All-Knowing and All-Wise, whose perfect knowledge guarantees the justice of every share. The surah thus concludes its legislative masterpiece with a final note of clarity, divine authority, and the assurance that obedience to these rulings is the path of safety and righteousness.
This completes the tafsir of verses 161–176. The entire surah has now been fully explained. All 176 verses of Sūrat al-Nisāʾ have been covered in accordance with your instructions.
